International GAAP® 2025 
Chapter 06 
First-time adoption 
300 
Overview 
IFRS 1 First-time Adoption of International Financial Reporting Standards ensures that an entity’s first 
IFRS financial statements, and its interim financial reports for part of the period covered by those financial 
statements, contain high quality information that is transparent for users and comparable over all periods 
presented; provides a suitable starting point for accounting in accordance with IFRS accounting standards; 
and can be generated at a cost that does not exceed the benefits. [IFRS 1.1]. 
This chapter addresses the accounting for the transition to IFRS accounting standards in an entity’s first 
IFRS financial statements, including the scope of the first IFRS financial statements in section 2 below, 
exceptions to the retrospective application of other IFRS accounting standards in section 4 below and 
exemptions from other IFRS accounting standards in section 5 below. The chapter also discusses the 
requirements for presentation and disclosure in the first IFRS financial statements in section 6 below, some 
practical issues arising from the requirements of IFRS 1 in section 7 below, and some regulatory issues in 
section 8 below. This chapter generally discusses the requirements of IFRS 1 for accounting periods 
beginning on or after 1 January 2025 unless otherwise stated and reflects the amendments to the revised 
version of IFRS 1 referred to in section 1.1 below. 
This chapter does not deal with the first-time adoption of the earlier versions of IFRS 1. A detailed 
discussion of the first-time adoption using the earlier versions of the standard can be found in Chapter 5 of 
International GAAP 2024 and prior editions. 
What you need to know 
• 
A first-time adopter is an entity that presents its first IFRS financial statements, which are the first 
annual financial statements in which the entity adopts IFRS accounting standards by an explicit and 
unreserved statement of compliance with IFRS accounting standards. 
• 
IFRS 1 applies to the first IFRS financial statements and to each interim financial report, if any, 
presented in accordance with IAS 34 Interim Financial Reporting for part of the period covered by 
the first IFRS financial statements. An entity that reapplies IFRS accounting standards, but whose 
most recent annual financial statements did not contain an explicit and unreserved statement of 
compliance, is permitted to choose to apply IFRS 1 or to retrospectively apply IFRS accounting 
standards as if it had never stopped applying IFRS accounting standards. 
• 
A first-time adopter must use the same accounting policies in its opening IFRS statement of financial 
position (as at the date of transition to IFRS accounting standards, being the beginning of the 
earliest comparative period presented) and throughout all periods presented in its first IFRS financial 
statements. These accounting policies must comply with IFRS accounting standards effective at the 
end of the first IFRS reporting period, except for certain mandatory exceptions and voluntary 
exemptions. The transitional provisions in IFRS accounting standards available to existing IFRS 
reporters do not apply. 
• 
When an entity makes estimates in accordance with IFRS accounting standards, these must be 
consistent with estimates made under previous GAAP for the same date (after adjusting for the 
effects of any differences in accounting policies), unless there is objective evidence that the previous 
GAAP estimates were in error. 
• 
IFRS 1 requires a first-time adopter to present its opening IFRS statement of financial position and 
to explain the transition to IFRS accounting standards, including reconciliations of equity at the 
date of transition and the end of the comparative period and of total comprehensive income of the 
comparative period with previous GAAP. An explanation of material adjustments to the cash flow 
statement is also needed. The first IFRS financial statements must also comply with the 
presentation and disclosure requirements of other IFRS accounting standards. There are certain 
disclosures, including reconciliations to previous GAAP, required in interim financial reports to 
which IFRS 1 applies. 
 
 
 
 


International GAAP® 2025 
Chapter 06 
First-time adoption 
301 
1 
Introduction 
1.1 
Objectives of first-time adoption 
In principle, a first-time adopter prepares financial statements as if it had always applied IFRS accounting 
standards. Although entities routinely have to apply new accounting standards by way of prior year 
adjustment, adopting IFRS accounting standards, a new basis of accounting, is a challenging undertaking 
and poses a distinct set of problems. One cannot underestimate the magnitude of the effort involved in 
adopting a large number of new accounting standards. The requirements of individual standards will often 
differ significantly from those under an entity’s previous GAAP and adoption of IFRS accounting standards 
could require information to be collected that was not needed under the previous GAAP. 
IFRS 1 has a rather limited objective: to ensure that an entity’s first IFRS financial statements, and its 
interim financial reports for part of the period covered by those first IFRS financial statements, contain high 
quality financial information that: [IFRS 1.1] 
(a) Is transparent for users and comparable over all periods presented 
(b) Provides a suitable starting point for accounting in accordance with IFRS accounting standards 
And 
(c) Can be generated at a cost that does not exceed the benefits 
It is important for users to be mindful of this objective as it provides the principal rationale underlying many 
of the decisions reflected in the standard, in particular the various exceptions that require, and exemptions 
that allow, a first-time adopter to deviate from the general rule (i.e., a retrospective application). 
Although IFRS 1 owes its existence to the 2005 adoption of IFRS accounting standards by EU companies 
whose securities were traded on an EU regulated market,1 one of the IASB’s aims was “to find solutions 
that would be appropriate for any entity, in any part of the world, regardless of whether adoption occurs in 
2005 or at a different time”. [IFRS 1.BC3]. IFRS 1 had to be written in a way that completely ignores a first-
time adopter’s previous GAAP. This means that first-time adoption exemptions are made available to all 
first-time adopters, including those whose previous GAAP was very close to IFRS accounting standards. A 
first-time adopter that so desires will be able to make considerable adjustments to its opening 
IFRS statement of financial position, using the available exemptions in IFRS 1, even if the differences 
between its previous GAAP and IFRS accounting standards were only minor. The requirement to use the 
same IFRS accounting standards for all periods presented in the first IFRS financial statements could also 
give rise to considerable adjustments from previous GAAP. 
Another issue is the potential for lack of comparability between different first-time adopters, and between 
first-time adopters and entities already applying IFRS accounting standards. [IFRS 1.BC9]. The IASB ultimately 
decided that it was “more important to achieve comparability over time within a first-time adopter’s first 
IFRS financial statements and between different entities adopting IFRSs for the first time at a given date; 
achieving comparability between first-time adopters and entities that already apply IFRSs is a secondary 
objective.” [IFRS 1.BC10]. 
A revised IFRS 1 was issued in November 2008, which retains the substance of the previous version of the 
standard but within a changed structure. [IFRS 1.BC3B]. The standard has been further amended as a result of 
the IASB’s annual improvements process, consequential amendments resulting from issuance of new 
standards, as well as to provide limited exceptions and exemptions that address specific matters. This 
approach always carried the risk that its complexity might eventually overwhelm its practical application. 
All these amendments are incorporated into the applicable sections of this chapter. 
1.2 
Convergence with US GAAP 
Differences between the IFRS accounting standards and US GAAP guidance on this topic are not addressed 
in this chapter as the standards are not converged. Therefore, differences between IFRS accounting 
standards and US GAAP may exist which are not discussed in this publication. 
 
1 
Regulation (EC) No 1606/2002 of the European Parliament and of the Council of 19 July 2002 on the application of international 
accounting standards, Article 4. 


International GAAP® 2025 
Chapter 06 
First-time adoption 
302 
1.3 
Future developments 
Consideration will be given at the time of deliberations to how new standards or amendments could impact 
a first-time adopter of IFRS accounting standards and any consequential amendments to IFRS 1 will be 
included in the new standards or amendments. Entities contemplating conversion to IFRS accounting 
standards are recommended to monitor the IASB’s agenda in order to anticipate the potential impact of 
future standards or amendments on their conversions. 
1.4 
Defined terms 
IFRS 1 defines the following terms in connection with the transition to IFRS accounting standards. [IFRS 1 
Appendix A]. Regardless of the following defined terms, we refer to ‘IFRS accounting standards’ instead of 
‘International Financial Reporting Standards’ or ‘IFRSs’ in this chapter. We also refer to the ‘date of 
transition to IFRS accounting standards’ or the ‘date of transition’ instead of the ‘date of transition to 
IFRSs’. The term ‘IFRS financial statements’ is, however, used to mean annual financial statements 
prepared in accordance with IFRS accounting standards. 
Extract from IFRS 1 
Appendix A 
Defined terms 
date of transition to IFRSs 
The beginning of the earliest period for which an entity presents full 
comparative information under IFRSs in its first IFRS financial 
statements. 
first IFRS financial 
statements 
The first annual financial statements in which an entity adopts 
International Financial Reporting Standards (IFRSs), by an explicit 
and unreserved statement of compliance with IFRSs. 
first IFRS reporting period 
The latest reporting period covered by an entity’s first IFRS financial 
statements. 
first-time adopter 
An entity that presents its first IFRS financial statements. 
International Financial 
Reporting Standards (IFRSs) 
Standards and Interpretations issued by the International Accounting 
Standards Board (IASB). They comprise: 
(a) International Financial Reporting Standards; 
(b) International Accounting Standards; 
(c) IFRIC Interpretations; and 
(d) SIC Interpretations.1 
opening IFRS statement of 
financial position 
An entity’s statement of financial position at the date of transition to 
IFRSs. 
previous GAAP 
The basis of accounting that a first-time adopter used immediately 
before adopting IFRSs. 
1 
Definition of IFRSs amended after the name changes introduced by the revised Constitution of the IFRS Foundation in 2010. 


International GAAP® 2025 
Chapter 06 
First-time adoption 
303 
2 
Who is a first-time adopter? 
2.1 
The first IFRS financial statements in scope of IFRS 1 
An entity’s first IFRS financial statements are the first annual financial statements in which the entity 
adopts IFRS accounting standards, by making in those financial statements an explicit and unreserved 
statement of compliance with IFRS accounting standards. IFRS 1 provides a description of the 
circumstances in which an entity is a first-time adopter and therefore is within the standard’s scope (see the 
extract from IFRS 1 below). [IFRS 1.3, IFRS 1 Appendix A]. 
Extract from IFRS 1 
3 
An entity’s first IFRS financial statements are the first annual financial statements in which the 
entity adopts IFRSs, by an explicit and unreserved statement in those financial statements of 
compliance with IFRSs. Financial statements in accordance with IFRSs are an entity’s first 
IFRS financial statements if, for example, the entity: 
(a) 
presented its most recent previous financial statements: 
(i) 
in accordance with national requirements that are not consistent with IFRSs in all 
respects; 
(ii) 
in conformity with IFRSs in all respects, except that the financial statements did not 
contain an explicit and unreserved statement that they complied with IFRSs; 
(iii) 
containing an explicit statement of compliance with some, but not all, IFRSs; 
(iv) 
in accordance with national requirements inconsistent with IFRSs, using some 
individual IFRSs to account for items for which national requirements did not exist; 
or 
(v) 
in accordance with national requirements, with a reconciliation of some amounts to 
the amounts determined in accordance with IFRSs; 
(b) 
prepared financial statements in accordance with IFRSs for internal use only, without 
making them available to the entity’s owners or any other external users; 
(c) 
prepared a reporting package in accordance with IFRSs for consolidation purposes 
without preparing a complete set of financial statements as defined in IAS 1 Presentation 
of Financial Statements (as revised in 2007); or 
(d) 
did not present financial statements for previous periods. 
An entity whose most recent previous financial statements contained an explicit and unreserved statement 
of compliance with IFRS accounting standards can never be considered a first-time adopter. This is the case 
even in the following circumstances: [IFRS 1.4] 
• 
The entity presented financial statements containing an explicit and unreserved statement of 
compliance with IFRS accounting standards despite the fact that the auditors issued a qualified audit 
report on those IFRS financial statements. By contrast, an entity that makes a statement of 
compliance that excludes any IFRS accounting standards will still be a first-time adopter (see 
Illustration 2-1 below). 
• 
The entity presented financial statements stating compliance with both national GAAP and IFRS 
accounting standards. 
Or 
• 
The entity stops presenting a separate set of financial statements under national requirements, which 
was previously presented in addition to its IFRS financial statements (see Illustration 2-2 below). 
The IASB could have introduced special rules that would have required an entity that significantly departed 
from IFRS accounting standards to apply IFRS 1. However, the IASB considered that such rules would lead 
to “complexity and uncertainty”. [IFRS 1.BC5]. In addition, this would have given entities applying ‘IFRS-lite’ 
(entities not applying IFRS accounting standards rigorously in all respects e.g., applying IFRS accounting 
standards except for certain standards and interpretations) an option to sidestep the requirements of IAS 8 
Accounting Policies, Changes in Accounting Estimates and Errors to disclose departures from IFRS 
accounting standards as errors. [IFRS 1.BC6]. 


International GAAP® 2025 
Chapter 06 
First-time adoption 
304 
Illustrations 2-1 to 2-3 below demonstrate certain scenarios in connection with determining whether an 
entity is a first-time adopter. 
Illustration 2-1: 
Scope of application of IFRS 1 - statement of compliance 
Entity A applied IFRS accounting standards in its previous financial statements, but stated that it “applied IFRS 
accounting standards except for IFRS 2 Share-based Payment.” 
Entity A is a first-time adopter because its previous financial statements did not contain an unreserved 
statement of compliance with IFRS accounting standards. It is irrelevant whether the auditors’ report was 
qualified or not. [IFRS 1.3(a)(iii)] 
Entity B applied IFRS accounting standards in its previous financial statements and stated that “the financial 
statements are prepared in conformity with IFRS accounting standards.” Despite that statement, Entity B had 
not applied IFRS 2. 
Entity B is not a first-time adopter because its financial statements contained an unreserved statement of 
compliance with IFRS accounting standards. Even if the auditors had qualified their report, the entity would 
still not be a first-time adopter. [IFRS 1.4(c)]. 
It is clear that the scope of IFRS 1 is very much rules-based, which as Illustration 2-1 above demonstrates, 
can lead to different answers in similar situations and sometimes to counter-intuitive answers. 
Illustration 2-2: 
Scope of application of IFRS 1 - entity applying national GAAP and IFRS 
accounting standards 
Entity C prepares two sets of financial statements, one set of financial statements based on its national GAAP 
and the other set based on IFRS accounting standards. The IFRS financial statements contained an explicit and 
unreserved statement of compliance with IFRS accounting standards and were made available externally. From 
20X3 onwards, Entity C stops presenting financial statements based on its national GAAP. 
Entity C is not a first-time adopter because it already published financial statements that contained an explicit 
and unreserved statement of compliance with IFRS accounting standards. [IFRS 1.4(a)]. 
 
Illustration 2-3: 
Scope of application of IFRS 1 - first IFRS financial statements outside the 
annual financial statements 
Entity D prepared financial statements under its previous GAAP for the year ended 31 December 20X2. In 
connection with its initial public offering, Entity D published an offering document that includes IFRS financial 
statements that contain an explicit and unreserved statement of compliance with IFRS accounting standards. 
The date of transition to IFRS accounting standards for the purposes of those financial statements, which 
cover the most recent three financial years, was 1 January 20X0. 
Entity D’s annual financial statements (as required by the legal or regulatory framework of its jurisdiction) are 
prepared under IFRS accounting standards for the first time for the year ended 31 December 20X3. 
The IFRS financial statements included in Entity D’s offering document were its first IFRS financial statements, 
containing an explicit and unreserved statement of compliance with IFRS accounting standards. Therefore, 
Entity D does not apply IFRS 1 in its first annual financial statements prepared under IFRS accounting 
standards as it is not a first-time adopter. [IFRS 1.3]. Although not required by IFRS accounting standards, 
Entity D could, if it wished to do so, repeat information about its transition to IFRS accounting standards in its 
annual financial statements for the year ended 31 December 20X3. 
If, however, Entity D had included financial statements in its offering document that did not contain an 
explicit and unreserved statement of compliance with IFRS accounting standards then the annual financial 
statements for 20X3 would need to be prepared in accordance with IFRS 1. If those financial statements 
only included comparative information for the year ended 31 December 20X2 then Entity D’s date of 
transition would be 1 January 20X2. 
An entity will be a first-time adopter if it has previously prepared financial statements in accordance with 
IFRS accounting standards but only for internal purposes, for example, where the entity had: 
• 
Prepared financial statements in accordance with IFRS accounting standards for internal use only, 
without making them available to the entity’s owners or any other external users [IFRS 1.3(b)] 
Or 
• 
Prepared a reporting package in accordance with IFRS accounting standards for consolidation purposes 
without preparing a complete set of financial statements as defined in IAS 1 [IFRS 1.3(c)] 


International GAAP® 2025 
Chapter 06 
First-time adoption 
305 
How we see it 
Where previous financial statements containing an explicit and unreserved statement of compliance with 
IFRS accounting standards have been authorised for issue, but are subject to restrictions for specific use 
or that limited the distribution to certain users, these restrictions will need to be evaluated to determine 
whether or not those previous financial statements were general purpose financial statements (see 
section 2.1.1 below). 
If the previous financial statements were general purpose financial statements made available to the 
entity’s owners or any other external users, the entity is not a first-time adopter. In our view, the 
availability of financial statements, as described in IFRS 1.3(b), does not require active distribution of the 
financial statements. If the financial statements could be requested by owners or other external users 
(and there is a known mechanism by which such requests could be made), the financial statements would 
be available. 
An entity that is a subsidiary of its parent that reports in accordance with IFRS accounting standards and 
has previously prepared only a reporting package in accordance with IFRS accounting standards for 
consolidation purposes is a first-time adopter. However, it could use the amounts reported for it in the 
group’s financial statements when it adopts IFRS accounting standards for its own financial statements on 
the basis explained in section 5.8.1 below. 
Finally, IFRS 1 applies also to a first-time adopter that did not present financial statements for previous 
periods. [IFRS 1.3(d)]. For example, when an entity transfers its operations into a new company prior to an 
issue to the public, the new company would be a first-time adopter if the entity never applied IFRS 
accounting standards in the past. 
An entity that is already applying IFRS accounting standards in preparing its financial statements must not 
apply IFRS 1 to changes in its accounting policies. Instead, such an entity applies: [IFRS 1.5] 
• 
The requirements on changes in accounting policies in IAS 8 
And 
• 
Specific transitional requirements in other IFRS accounting standards 
2.1.1 
What are general purpose financial statements? 
IFRS accounting standards are intended to be applied in the preparation of general purpose financial 
statements, i.e., those intended to meet the needs of users who are not in a position to require an entity to 
prepare reports tailored to their particular information needs. Many existing and potential investors, 
lenders and other creditors (‘the primary users’) cannot require reporting entities to provide information 
directly to them and must rely on general purpose financial statements for much of the financial 
information they need. [IAS 1.2, IAS 1.7, CF 1.5]. The objective of general purpose financial reporting is to 
provide financial information about the reporting entity that is useful to the primary users in making 
decisions relating to providing resources to the entity. [CF 1.2]. Management of an entity need not rely on 
general purpose financial reports, since the relevant information can be obtained internally (see Chapter 2 
at sections 4.1.1 and 4.1.2 and Chapter 3 at section 1.1). [CF 1.9]. 
How we see it 
Judgement is required in making the determination of whether or not the previous financial statements 
were general purpose financial statements in accordance with IFRS accounting standards, based on the 
specific facts and circumstances. Factors to consider include: the purpose and intended use for which the 
financial statements have been prepared (including the nature of any restrictions over their use); the 
extent to which the financial statements have been made available to owners or other external users; 
whether or not the recipients are permitted to make the financial statements available to other users; 
and whether the financial statements have been authorised for issue by the relevant body. 
A restriction of the distribution (or over the use) of the financial statements authorised for issue does 
not necessarily mean the financial statements are not general purpose financial statements, as 
explained below. 


International GAAP® 2025 
Chapter 06 
First-time adoption 
306 
For example, if a set of financial statements that includes an explicit and unreserved statement of 
compliance with IFRS accounting standards was made available to an external party (such as a bank for the 
purposes of a lending decision) and the information has not specifically been tailored for the bank’s needs, 
the financial statements would generally be regarded as general purpose financial statements. However, 
where a set of financial statements that includes an explicit and unreserved statement of compliance with 
IFRS accounting standards was made available only to an entity’s board members, CEO or CFO (who are 
also the entity’s shareholders) but not to other owners, there could be a question over whether these are 
general purpose financial statements. Accordingly, it would be necessary to understand the full facts and 
circumstances in making this determination, taking into account the above factors. 
2.2 
When is IFRS 1 applied? 
An entity that presents its first IFRS financial statements is a first-time adopter, [IFRS 1 Appendix A], and, 
accordingly, must apply IFRS 1 in preparing those financial statements. [IFRS 1.2(a)]. It must also apply the 
standard in each interim financial report that it presents in accordance with IAS 34 for part of the period 
covered by its first IFRS financial statements. [IFRS 1.2(b)]. Therefore, a first-time adopter does not apply 
IFRS 1 to a ‘trading statement’, an ‘earnings press release’ or other financial report issued at its interim 
reporting date that is not described as complying with IAS 34 or IFRS accounting standards. In Practical 
example 2-1 below, Sony Group Corporation described its adoption of IFRS accounting standards in its 
interim or quarterly financial statements. 
Practical example 2-1: 
Sony Group Corporation  
(For the three months ended June 30, 2021)

Quarterly Securities Report [extract] 
Notes to Condensed Consolidated Financial Statements (Unaudited) [extract]

2.2.1 
Repeat application of IFRS 1 
In some cases, an entity’s most recent annual financial statements did not contain an explicit and 
unreserved statement of compliance with IFRS accounting standards, but the entity applied IFRS 
accounting standards in a previous reporting period (giving an explicit and unreserved statement of 
compliance with IFRS accounting standards). 
IFRS 1 does not prohibit an entity from applying IFRS 1 more than once. In May 2012, IFRS 1 was amended 
to clarify that an entity that stopped applying IFRS accounting standards in the past and chooses, or is 
required, to resume preparing IFRS financial statements has the option to apply IFRS 1 again. [IFRS 1.4A]. 
The Board decided, based on cost-benefit grounds, that “the entity should be allowed, rather than required, 
to apply IFRS 1” when it reapplies IFRS accounting standards. [IFRS 1.BC6C]. If the entity chooses not to 
reapply IFRS 1, it must retrospectively restate its financial statements in accordance with IAS 8 as if it had 
never stopped applying IFRS accounting standards. 
Whichever option is selected the entity is required to disclose the reasons why it stopped applying IFRS 
accounting standards and why it resumed applying IFRS accounting standards. [IFRS 1.23A]. When the entity 
does not elect to reapply IFRS 1, it must also disclose the reasons for choosing the retrospective 
restatement method, in addition to giving the disclosures required by IAS 8. [IFRS 1.4B, IFRS 1.23B]. 
The IASB explained this issue with an example of an entity that had applied IFRS 1 in connection with a 
foreign listing, subsequently delisted from the foreign exchange and no longer presented IFRS financial 
statements, but is now adopting IFRS accounting standards again together with other entities in its local 
jurisdiction - see Illustration 2-4 below. [IFRS 1.BC6A]. 


International GAAP® 2025 
Chapter 06 
First-time adoption 
307 
Illustration 2-4: 
Repeated application of IFRS 1 when an entity does not apply IFRS accounting 
standards for one year 
Entity A prepared IFRS financial statements for 20X0 and 20X1 that contained an explicit and unreserved 
statement of compliance with IFRS accounting standards. However, in 20X2 Entity A did not make an explicit 
and unreserved statement of compliance with IFRS accounting standards. 
If Entity A resumes presenting financial statements in accordance with IFRS accounting standards, it could 
choose to apply IFRS accounting standards as a first-time adopter or it could elect to restate its financial 
statements retrospectively as if it had never stopped producing IFRS financial statements. 
If it elects to apply IFRS accounting standards as a first-time adopter for the purposes of its 20X3 financial 
statements, there is no requirement under IFRS 1 for Entity A to base its first IFRS financial statements in 
20X3 on the IFRS information that it produced before 20X3. Therefore, Entity A is able to apply the IFRS 1 
exemptions without regard to the elections it made in its first IFRS financial statements in 20X0. In fact, in this 
case, Entity A is unable to apply certain IFRS 1 exemptions by reference to the date of transition to IFRS 
accounting standards that it used in its 20X0 financial statements (see section 3.5 below). 
2.3 
Determining the previous GAAP 
Some entities prepare two complete sets of financial statements, e.g., one set of financial statements based 
on national GAAP and another set for distribution to foreign investors based on US GAAP. Applying the 
definition of ‘previous GAAP’, i.e., “The basis of accounting that a first-time adopter used immediately 
before adopting IFRSs”, [IFRS 1 Appendix A], to a dual reporting entity is not straightforward, as the examples 
below illustrate: 
(a) A dual reporting entity adopts IFRS accounting standards and at the same time stops presenting 
financial statements under its national GAAP and US GAAP: 
Both national GAAP and US GAAP meet the definition of ‘previous GAAP’. However, the entity can only 
present one set of IFRS financial statements. Therefore, the entity must choose a ‘previous GAAP’. 
How we see it 
While, at least in theory, this appears to be a free choice there are a number of limiting constraints 
to take into account in determining previous GAAP: 
(i) National legislation and regulatory requirements could restrict an entity’s options and require 
either national GAAP or US GAAP to be designated as the previous GAAP. 
(ii) Comparability with other entities in the same jurisdiction could be increased if all entities in that 
jurisdiction use the same GAAP as their previous GAAP. 
(iii) One set of financial statements could be considered to be the ‘main’ set of financial statements, 
for example: 
• 
If the national GAAP financial statements received very limited circulation then they are 
clearly not the entity’s ‘main’ financial statements. Conversely, if the US GAAP financial 
statements are only prepared for a specific purpose (e.g., to obtain a bank loan) then they 
might not be the entity’s ‘main’ financial statements. 
• 
The relative dominance of shareholder groups might provide an indication as to which set 
of financial statements is considered to be the ‘main’ set of financial statements. 
An entity applies judgement when the constraints above do not all identify the same GAAP as the 
previous GAAP. 
IFRS 1 only requires disclosure of reconciliations between an entity’s previous GAAP and IFRS 
accounting standards. However, it will be advisable for an entity to provide disclosures, on a 
voluntary basis, that contain sufficient information to enable users to understand the material 
reconciling items between the IFRS financial statements and the financial statements that were 
prepared under its financial reporting framework which is not selected as the previous GAAP. Some 
national regulators (e.g., the US Securities and Exchange Commission), in fact, expect such 
disclosures (see section 8.1.2 below).2 
 
2 
International Practices Task Force - November 25, 2008 - Highlights, Center for Audit Quality Washington Office, 25 November 
2008. 


International GAAP® 2025 
Chapter 06 
First-time adoption 
308 
(b) A dual reporting entity adopts IFRS accounting standards and at the same time continues to present 
financial statements under its national GAAP but stops presenting financial statements under US GAAP: 
How we see it 
While one might expect US GAAP to be treated as the previous GAAP, both national GAAP and US 
GAAP meet the definition of ‘previous GAAP’. An entity therefore considers the criteria (i) to (iii) 
under (a) above in determining its previous GAAP. 
If an entity treated its national GAAP as its previous GAAP, then it could still present an explanation of the 
differences between US GAAP and IFRS accounting standards to aid former users of the US GAAP financial 
statements, if it wishes or if a regulator requires it. 
As illustrated in Practical example 2-2 below, when Infosys Technologies Limited adopted IFRS accounting 
standards it treated Indian GAAP as its previous GAAP even though it continued to report under Indian GAAP 
for statutory purposes. However, Infosys provided additional reconciliations between US GAAP and its 
previous GAAP. 
(c) A dual reporting entity adopts IFRS accounting standards and at the same time stops presenting financial 
statements under US GAAP. Several years later it stops presenting financial statements under its national 
GAAP: 
The entity is not a first-time adopter when it ceases to present financial statements under its national 
GAAP, even if the entity treated US GAAP (rather than its national GAAP) as its previous GAAP when it 
adopted IFRS accounting standards. [IFRS 1.4(a)]. However, the entity could still present an explanation 
of the differences between its national GAAP and IFRS accounting standards to aid former users of its 
national GAAP financial statements, if it wishes or if a regulator requires it. 
Practical example 2-2: 
Infosys Technologies Limited (2009)

2.3.1 
Transition to IFRS accounting standards from a similar GAAP 
One consequence of the ongoing harmonisation of accounting standards around the world is that many 
national GAAPs are now virtually identical to IFRS accounting standards. However, differences between 
these national GAAPs and IFRS accounting standards often exist regarding the scope, transitional 
provisions, effective dates and actual wording of standards. In addition, some national GAAPs contain 
accounting alternatives not permitted by IFRS accounting standards. 
When an entity reporting under such a national GAAP (e.g., Singapore GAAP) adopts IFRS accounting 
standards there will often not be major changes required in its accounting policies to comply with IFRS 
accounting standards. A similar situation could arise where an entity reporting under the IFRS for SMEs 
adopts full IFRS accounting standards, for example, due to an IPO. However, under IFRS 1, it is not relevant 
whether or not a previous GAAP was very similar to IFRS accounting standards. Therefore, regardless of 
the absence of significant differences in accounting policies, that entity would be a first-time adopter when 
it includes an explicit and unreserved statement of compliance with IFRS accounting standards for the first 
time. So, even if the entity’s accounting policies were already fully aligned with IFRS accounting standards: 
(a) It would be permitted to apply the IFRS 1 exemptions and required to apply the IFRS 1 exceptions. 
(b) It would need to restate items for which the applicable first-time adoption exceptions differ from the 
transitional rules applicable to ongoing reporters (e.g., classification and measurement of financial 
assets). 
(c) It would not be permitted to apply different versions of IFRS accounting standards that were effective 
at earlier dates. 
(d) It would need to explain the transition to IFRS accounting standards. 
How we see it 
Notwithstanding the above, we believe an entity that: 
• 
Reported under a national GAAP that is virtually identical with IFRS accounting standards in all 
respects 
• 
Applied the national GAAP equivalent of IFRS 1 when the entity adopted that national GAAP 
• 
Made an explicit and unreserved statement of compliance with that national GAAP in its most recent 
financial statements 
And 
• 
Could have made an explicit and unreserved statement of compliance with IFRS accounting 
standards in those financial statements, if required 
Does not have to reapply IFRS 1 the first time that it makes an explicit and unreserved statement of 
compliance with IFRS accounting standards. 
For example, if an entity that meets the requirements described above decides to make an explicit and unreserved 
statement of compliance with IFRS accounting standards for the first time, either voluntarily or required to do so 
by a regulatory requirement related to an IPO, the entity would not be required to reapply IFRS 1. 


International GAAP® 2025 
Chapter 06 
First-time adoption 
310 
3 
Opening IFRS statement of financial position 
At the date of transition to IFRS accounting standards, an entity is required to prepare and present an 
opening IFRS statement of financial position that is the starting point for its accounting under IFRS 
accounting standards. [IFRS 1.6]. The date of transition to IFRS accounting standards is the beginning of the 
earliest period for which an entity presents full comparative information under IFRS accounting standards 
in its first IFRS financial statements. [IFRS 1 Appendix A]. Therefore, the date of transition for an entity 
reporting under IFRS accounting standards for the first time for the year ended on 31 December 20X3 and 
presenting one year of comparative figures is 1 January 20X2. It is recommended that entities consider the 
filing requirements for interim financial reports that a regulator in their jurisdiction could impose. For 
example, a regulator could require the opening IFRS statement of financial position to be presented in the 
first IFRS interim financial report even though this is not an IFRS 1 presentation requirement (see 
section 6.6 below). 
3.1 
First-time adoption timeline 
An entity’s first annual IFRS financial statements must include at least three statements of financial position 
(i.e., balance sheets), two statements of profit or loss and other comprehensive income, two separate 
statements of profit or loss (if presented), two statements of cash flows and two statements of changes in 
equity and related notes, including comparative information for all statements presented. [IFRS 1.21]. The 
beginning of the earliest comparative period for which the entity presents full comparative information under 
IFRS accounting standards will be treated as its date of transition to IFRS accounting standards. Figure 3-1 
below shows how for an entity with a December year-end the above terms are related: 
Figure 3-1: 
First-time adoption timeline 
 
Figure 3-1 above also illustrates that there is a period of overlap, for the financial year 20X2, which is 
reported first under the entity’s previous GAAP and then as a comparative period under IFRS accounting 
standards. Illustration 3-1 below demonstrates how an entity determines its date of transition to IFRS 
accounting standards. 
Illustration 3-1: 
Determining the date of transition to IFRS accounting standards 
Entity A’s year-end is 31 December, and it presents financial statements that include one comparative period. 
Entity A is required (e.g., by national legislation) to produce IFRS financial statements for the first annual 
accounting period starting on or after 1 January 20X3. 
Entity A’s first IFRS financial statements are for the period ending on 31 December 20X3. Its date of transition 
to IFRS accounting standards is 1 January 20X2, which is the beginning of the single comparative period 
included in its first IFRS financial statements. 
Entity B’s year-end is 31 July, and it presents financial statements that include two comparative periods. 
Entity B is required to produce IFRS financial statements for the first annual accounting period starting on or 
after 1 January 20X3. 
Entity B’s first IFRS financial statements are for the period ending on 31 July 20X4. Its date of transition to 
IFRS accounting standards is 1 August 20X1, which is the beginning of the earliest period for which full 
comparative information is included in its first IFRS financial statements. 


International GAAP® 2025 
Chapter 06 
First-time adoption 
311 
Entity C’s most recent financial statements, under its previous GAAP, are for the period from 1 July 20X1 to 
31 December 20X2. Entity C presents its first IFRS financial statements (that include one comparative period) 
for the period ending 31 December 20X3. 
Entity C’s date of transition to IFRS accounting standards is 1 July 20X1, which is the beginning of the 
comparative period included in its first IFRS financial statements. 
How we see it 
While IFRS accounting standards require presentation of at least one comparative period, the 
comparative period is not required to be equal in length to the current period. Thus, the entity’s date 
of transition will be the beginning of the earliest comparative period, irrespective of the length of 
that period. 
However, an entity must disclose the reason why the comparative period is not equal in length and the fact 
that the amounts presented in the financial statements are not entirely comparable. [IAS 1.36]. 
Similarly, it is generally not considered to be a problem if the current or comparative period in an entity’s first 
IFRS financial statements only covers a 52-week period, because IAS 1 does not preclude the practice of 
presenting financial statements for 52-week financial periods. [IAS 1.37]. 
3.2 
Opening IFRS statement of financial position and accounting 
policies 
Extract from IFRS 1 
7 
An entity shall use the same accounting policies in its opening IFRS statement of financial 
position and throughout all periods presented in its first IFRS financial statements. Those 
accounting policies shall comply with each IFRS effective at the end of its first IFRS reporting 
period, except as specified in paragraphs 13-19 and Appendices B-E. 
8 
An entity shall not apply different versions of IFRSs that were effective at earlier dates. An 
entity may apply a new IFRS that is not yet mandatory if that IFRS permits early application. 
… 
9 
The transitional provisions in other IFRSs apply to changes in accounting policies made by an 
entity that already uses IFRSs; they do not apply to a first-time adopter’s transition to IFRSs, 
except as specified in Appendices B-E. 
The fundamental principle of IFRS 1 is to require full retrospective application of IFRS accounting standards 
effective at the end of an entity’s first IFRS reporting period, but with limited exceptions and exemptions for 
the opening IFRS statement of financial position. IFRS 1 requires a first-time adopter to use the same 
accounting policies in its opening IFRS statement of financial position and for all periods presented in its 
first IFRS financial statements. However, this is not always straightforward, since to achieve this, the entity 
complies with each IFRS accounting standard effective at the end of its first IFRS reporting period fully 
retrospectively, after taking into account a number of allowed exemptions from certain IFRS accounting 
standards and mandatory exceptions to retrospective application of other IFRS accounting standards in 
accordance with IFRS 1, as provided in IFRS 1.13-183 and Appendices B-E of IFRS 1 (see section 3.5 
below). [IFRS 1.7, IFRS 1.9, IFRS 1.13, IFRS 1.18]. 
The requirement to apply the same accounting policies to all periods also prohibits a first-time adopter from 
applying previous versions of standards that were effective at earlier dates. [IFRS 1.8]. As well as enhancing 
comparability, the IASB believes that this gives users comparative information that is based on IFRS 
accounting standards that are superior to superseded versions of those standards and avoids unnecessary 
costs. [IFRS 1.BC11]. 
For similar reasons, IFRS 1 also permits an entity to choose to apply either the current standard or a new 
standard that is not yet mandatory if that standard allows early application. [IFRS 1.8]. Whichever standard is 
selected it would need to be applied consistently throughout the periods presented in its first IFRS financial 
statements on a retrospective basis, unless IFRS 1 provides an exemption or an exception that permits or 
requires otherwise. [IFRS 1.BC11A]. Figure 3-2 below summarises these requirements in IFRS 1. 
 
3 
IFRS 1.7 refers to paragraphs 13-19 as in the extract above, but paragraph 19 has been deleted.

312 
Figure 3-2: 
Timeline for the first IFRS financial statements 
 
Depending on the end of its first IFRS reporting period, there could be a choice over which version of a 
particular standard to apply. 
Illustration 3-2: 
Prohibition from applying superseded standards 
Entity A’s first IFRS reporting period ends on 31 December 2025 and its date of transition to IFRS 
accounting standards is 1 January 2024 as it presents financial statements including one comparative 
period. How does Entity A apply Lack of Exchangeability (Amendments to IAS 21 The Effects of Changes in 
Foreign Exchange Rates), which are effective for annual periods beginning on or after 1 January 2025, in its 
first IFRS financial statements? 
IFRS 1 requires Entity A to apply the amendments to IAS 21 because they are effective at the end of its first 
IFRS reporting period. IFRS 1 prohibits an entity from applying a superseded version of IAS 21 to transactions 
that occurred before 1 January 2025 while applying the amended standard in the periods beginning on or 
after 1 January 2025. Entity A must apply the above-mentioned amendments to IAS 21 fully retrospectively 
for all periods presented. [IFRS 1.7, IFRS 1.8]. 
IFRS 1 sets out requirements for the recognition, derecognition, classification and measurement of assets 
and liabilities in an entity’s opening statement of financial position as follows: [IFRS 1.10] 
Extract from IFRS 1 
10 
Except as described in paragraphs 13-19 and Appendices B-E, an entity shall, in its opening 
IFRS statement of financial position: 
(a) 
recognise all assets and liabilities whose recognition is required by IFRSs; 
(b) 
not recognise items as assets or liabilities if IFRSs do not permit such recognition; 
(c) 
reclassify items that it recognised in accordance with previous GAAP as one type of 
asset, liability or component of equity, but are a different type of asset, liability or 
component of equity in accordance with IFRSs; and 
(d) 
apply IFRSs in measuring all recognised assets and liabilities. 
Any change in accounting policies on adoption of IFRS accounting standards could cause changes in the 
amounts recorded under previous GAAP in respect of events and transactions that occurred before the 
date of transition to IFRS accounting standards. The effects of these changes are recognised at the date of 
transition to IFRS accounting standards in retained earnings or, if appropriate, in another category of 
equity. [IFRS 1.11]. For example, an entity that applies the revaluation model under IAS 16 Property, Plant 
and Equipment (see Chapter 19 at section 6) in its first IFRS financial statements would recognise the 
difference between cost (or deemed cost) and the revalued amount of property, plant and equipment in a 
revaluation reserve as a separate component of equity. [IFRS 1.IG10]. By contrast, an entity that had applied 
a revaluation model under its previous GAAP, but decided to apply the cost model under IAS 16, would 
reallocate the revaluation reserve to retained earnings or a separate component of equity not described as 
a revaluation reserve (see section 7.4.3 below). 


International GAAP® 2025 
Chapter 06 
First-time adoption 
313 
A first-time adopter is under no obligation to ensure that its IFRS accounting policies are similar to or as 
close as possible to its previous GAAP accounting policies. Therefore, for example, a first-time adopter 
could adopt the IAS 16 revaluation model despite the fact that it applied a cost model under its previous 
GAAP or vice versa. However, a first-time adopter would need to take into account the guidance in IAS 8 to 
ensure that its choice of accounting policy results in information that is relevant and reliable. [IAS 8.8, 
IAS 8.10, IAS 8.11, IAS 8.12]. 
The requirement to prepare an opening IFRS statement of financial position and ‘reset the clock’ at that 
date poses a number of challenges for first-time adopters. Even a first-time adopter that already applies a 
standard that is directly based on IFRS accounting standards might need to or decide to restate items in its 
opening IFRS statement of financial position (see section 2.3.1 above). For example, an entity applying a 
property, plant and equipment standard under previous GAAP that is based on IAS 16 could decide to use a 
deemed cost exemption for certain of its assets as allowed by IFRS 1. 
3.3 
Fair value and deemed cost 
Some exemptions in IFRS 1 refer to ‘fair value’ and ‘deemed cost’, which the standard defines as follows: 
[IFRS 1 Appendix A] 
• 
Deemed cost: 
An amount used as a surrogate for cost or depreciated cost at a given date. Subsequent depreciation 
or amortisation assumes that the entity had initially recognised the asset or liability at the given date 
and that its cost was equal to the deemed cost. 
• 
Fair value: 
The price that would be received to sell an asset or paid to transfer a liability in an orderly 
transaction between market participants at the measurement date (see Chapter 15 and IFRS 13 Fair 
Value Measurement). 
The fair values determined by a first-time adopter reflect the conditions that existed at the date for which 
they were determined, i.e., the first-time adopter does not apply hindsight in measuring the fair value at an 
earlier date. 
3.4 
Transitional provisions in other standards 
The transitional provisions in other standards only apply to entities that already report under IFRS accounting 
standards. Therefore, a first-time adopter is not able to apply those transitional provisions (unless specified by 
the requirements in IFRS 1.14-17 and Appendices B-E of IFRS 1). [IFRS 1.9, IFRS 1.12, IFRS 1.13, IFRS 1.18]. The 
exceptions and the exemptions to this general rule are covered in the later parts of this chapter that deal with 
the exceptions to the retrospective application of other IFRS accounting standards (see section 4 below) and 
the exemptions from other IFRS accounting standards (see section 5 below). 
It is important to note that the transition rules for first-time adopters and entities that already report under 
IFRS accounting standards could differ significantly. 
The IASB considers “case by case when it issues a new IFRS whether a first-time adopter should apply that 
IFRS retrospectively or prospectively. The Board expects that retrospective application will be appropriate 
in most cases, given its primary objective of comparability over time within a first-time adopter’s first 
IFRS financial statements. However, if the Board concludes in a particular case that prospective application 
by a first-time adopter is justified, it will amend the IFRS on first-time adoption of IFRSs.” [IFRS 1.BC14]. 
IAS 8 allows exceptions from retrospective application for entities that cannot apply a requirement after 
making every reasonable effort to do so. There is no such relief in IFRS 1. The IFRS Interpretations 
Committee (IFRS IC) agreed “that there were potential issues, especially with respect to ‘old’ items, such as 
property, plant and equipment. However, those issues could usually be resolved by using one of the 
transition options available in IFRS 1.” (See section 3.5 below).4 For example, an entity could elect to use 
fair value as deemed cost at the date of transition to IFRS accounting standards if an entity is unable to 
apply IAS 36 Impairment of Assets on a fully retrospective basis (see section 7.12 below). Therefore no 
‘impracticability relief’ was added to the standard for first-time adopters. The transition options usually 
involve using certain surrogate values as deemed cost and are discussed in section 5.4 below. 
 
4 
IFRIC Update, October 2004, available on the IFRS Foundation’s website.

3.5 
Departures from full retrospective application 
Extract from IFRS 1 
12 
This IFRS establishes two categories of exceptions to the principle that an entity’s opening 
IFRS statement of financial position shall comply with each IFRS: 
(a) 
paragraphs 14-17 and Appendix B prohibit retrospective application of some aspects of 
other IFRSs. 
(b) 
Appendices C-E grant exemptions from some requirements of other IFRSs. 
IFRS 1 provides for mandatory exceptions and optional exemptions as follows. [IFRS 1.12]. 
Mandatory exceptions: IFRS 1 prohibits retrospective application of IFRS accounting standards in some 
areas, particularly where this would require judgements by management about past conditions after the 
outcome of a particular transaction is already known. The mandatory exceptions in the standard cover the 
following situations: [IFRS 1.12(a), IFRS 1.13, IFRS 1.14, IFRS 1.B1] 
(a) Estimates (see section 4.2 below) 
(b) Derecognition of financial assets and financial liabilities (see section 4.3 below) 
(c) Hedge accounting (see sections 4.4 to 4.7 below) 
(d) Non-controlling interests (see section 4.8 below) 
(e) Classification and measurement of financial instruments (see section 4.9 below) 
(f) Impairment of financial assets (see section 4.10 below) 
(g) Embedded derivatives (see section 4.11 below) 
(h) Government loans (see section 4.12 below) 
(i) Insurance contracts (see section 4.13 below) 
(j) Deferred tax related to leases and decommissioning, restoration and similar liabilities (see 
section 4.14 below) 
The reasoning behind most of the exceptions is that retrospective application of IFRS accounting standards 
in these situations could easily result in an unacceptable use of hindsight. [IFRS 1.BC12(b)]. This could lead to 
arbitrary or biased restatements, which would be neither relevant nor reliable. 
Optional exemptions: In addition to the mandatory exceptions, IFRS 1 grants limited optional exemptions 
from the general requirement of full retrospective application of the standards in force at the end of an 
entity’s first IFRS reporting period, considering the fact that the cost of complying with them would be likely 
to exceed the benefits to users of financial statements. [IFRS 1.BC12(a)]. The standard provides exemptions in 
relation to: [IFRS 1.12(b), IFRS 1.18, IFRS 1 Appendix C, IFRS 1.D1] 
(a) Business combinations and acquisitions of associates and joint arrangements (see section 5.2 below) 
(b) Share-based payment transactions (see section 5.3 below) 
(c) Deemed cost (see section 5.4 below) 
(d) Leases (see section 5.5 below) 
(e) Cumulative translation differences (see section 5.6 below) 
(f) Investments in subsidiaries, joint ventures and associates (see section 5.7 below) 
(g) Assets and liabilities of subsidiaries, associates and joint ventures (see section 5.8 below) 
(h) Compound financial instruments (see section 5.9 below) 
(i) Designation of previously recognised financial instruments (see section 5.10 below) 
(j) Fair value measurement of financial assets or financial liabilities at initial recognition (see 
section 5.11 below) 
(k) Decommissioning liabilities included in the cost of property, plant and equipment (see section 5.12 below) 
(l) Financial assets or intangible assets accounted for in accordance with IFRIC 12 Service Concession 
Arrangements (see section 5.13 below) 
(m) Borrowing costs (see section 5.14 below) 
(n) Extinguishing financial liabilities with equity instruments (see section 5.15 below) 
(o) Severe hyperinflation (see section 5.16 below) 
(p) Joint arrangements (see section 5.17 below) 
(q) Stripping costs in the production phase of a surface mine (see section 5.18 below) 


International GAAP® 2025 
Chapter 06 
First-time adoption 
315 
(r) Designation of contracts to buy or sell a non-financial item (see section 5.19 below) 
(s) Revenue from contracts with customers (see section 5.20 below) 
(t) Foreign currency transactions and advance consideration (see section 5.21 below) 
(u) Regulatory deferral accounts (see section 5.22 below). This exemption is dealt with in IFRS 14 
Regulatory Deferral Accounts, rather than IFRS 1. [IFRS 14.5]. 
In addition to the above, IFRS 1 grants certain exemptions that are intended to have short-term 
applications. These are included in Appendix E to the standard. [IFRS 1 Appendix E]. At the time of writing, 
there are no short-term exemptions that apply where an entity’s first IFRS reporting period begins on or 
after 1 January 2025 (and therefore the requirements of Appendix E are not discussed in this chapter). 
First-time adopters should monitor developments for any new short-term exemptions that could be issued 
after the publication of this edition. 
It is specifically prohibited under IFRS 1 to apply exemptions by analogy to other items. [IFRS 1.18]. 
How we see it 
Application of the transition exemptions is entirely optional except for IFRS 1.D16 and D17 (see 
section 5.8 below), i.e., a first-time adopter can pick and choose the exemptions that it wants to apply. 
Importantly, the IASB did not establish a hierarchy of exemptions. Therefore, we believe when an item is 
covered by more than one exemption, a first-time adopter has a free choice in determining the order in 
which it applies the exemptions. 
Illustration 3-3 below provides an example. 
Illustration 3-3: 
Order of application of exemptions 
Entity A acquired a building in a business combination. If Entity A were to apply the business combinations 
exemption described in section 5.2 below, it would, at the date of transition to IFRS accounting standards, 
recognise the building at the acquisition date value net of subsequent depreciation and impairment of CU120. 
However, if it were to use the fair value as the deemed cost of the building it would have to recognise it at 
CU150. Which value does Entity A use? 
Entity A can choose whether it wants to recognise the building at CU120 or CU150 in its opening 
IFRS statement of financial position. The fact that Entity A uses the business combinations exemption does not 
prohibit it from also applying the ‘fair value as deemed cost’ exemption in relation to the same assets. Also, 
Entity A is not required to apply the ‘fair value as deemed cost’ exemption to all assets or to all similar assets 
as entities can choose to which assets they want to apply this exemption (see section 5.4 below).

4 
Exceptions to the retrospective application of other IFRS 
accounting standards 
4.1 
Introduction 
IFRS 1 provides a number of mandatory exceptions that specifically prohibit retrospective application of 
some aspects of other IFRS accounting standards as listed in section 3.5 above. Each of the exceptions is 
explained in detail below. 
4.2 
Estimates 
Extract from IFRS 1 
14 
An entity’s estimates in accordance with IFRSs at the date of transition to IFRSs shall be 
consistent with estimates made for the same date in accordance with previous GAAP (after 
adjustments to reflect any difference in accounting policies), unless there is objective 
evidence that those estimates were in error. 
The above requirement, [IFRS 1.14], has the effect that an entity must not apply hindsight and make ‘better’ 
estimates when it prepares its first IFRS financial statements. This also means that an entity is not allowed 
to consider subsequent events that provide evidence of conditions that existed at that date, but that came 
to light after the date its previous GAAP financial statements were finalised. If an estimate made under 
previous GAAP requires adjustment because of new information after the relevant date, an entity treats 
this information in the same way as a non-adjusting event after the reporting period under IAS 10 Events 
after the Reporting Period. [IFRS 1.BC84]. Effectively, the IASB wishes to prevent entities from using hindsight 
to ‘clean up’ their balance sheets as part of the preparation of the opening IFRS statement of financial 
position. In addition, the exception also ensures that a first-time adopter need not conduct a search for, and 
change the accounting for, events that might have otherwise qualified as adjusting events. 
IFRS 1 provides the following guidance on estimates: 
• 
When previous GAAP required estimates of similar items for the date of transition to IFRS accounting 
standards, an entity can be in one of the following two positions: [IFRS 1.IG3] 
• 
Its previous GAAP accounting policy was consistent with IFRS accounting standards, in which case 
the estimates under IFRS accounting standards need to be consistent with those made for that 
date under previous GAAP, unless there is objective evidence that those estimates were in error 
under IAS 8. [IAS 8.5]. 
• 
Its previous GAAP accounting policy was not consistent with IFRS accounting standards, in which 
case it adjusts the estimates only for the difference in accounting policies (unless there is objective 
evidence that those estimates were in error under IAS 8). [IAS 8.5]. 
In both situations, it accounts for the revisions to those estimates in the period in which it makes the 
revisions in the same way as a non-adjusting event after the reporting period under IAS 10. [IFRS 1.15, 
IFRS 1.IG3, IAS 10.10]. 
• 
When an entity needs to make estimates under IFRS accounting standards at the date of transition to 
IFRS accounting standards that were not required under its previous GAAP, those estimates must 
reflect conditions that existed at that date. This is consistent with the distinction in IAS 10 between 
adjusting events and non-adjusting events after the reporting period. In particular, estimates of market 
prices, interest rates or foreign exchange rates reflect market conditions at that date. [IFRS 1.16, 
IFRS 1.IG3]. Entities that are preparing for transition to IFRS accounting standards need to consider 
gathering the data required for the estimate at the date of transition to IFRS accounting standards to 
make the transition easier and to ensure that hindsight is not incorporated into the estimate. 
The requirements above apply both to estimates made in respect of the date of transition to IFRS 
accounting standards and to those in respect of any comparative period presented, in which case the 
references to ‘the date of transition to IFRS accounting standards’ above are replaced by references to ‘the 
end of that comparative period’. [IFRS 1.17]. 


International GAAP® 2025 
Chapter 06 
First-time adoption 
317 
Figure 4-1 below shows the decision-making process that an entity needs to apply in dealing with estimates 
at the date of transition to IFRS accounting standards and during any of the comparative periods included 
in its first IFRS financial statements. 
Figure 4-1: 
The treatment of estimates in the first IFRS financial statements 
 
The general prohibition in IFRS 1 on the use of hindsight in making estimates about past transactions does 
not override the requirements in other IFRS accounting standards that base classifications or 
measurements on circumstances existing at a particular date, e.g., the distinction between finance leases 
and operating leases for a lessor. [IFRS 1.IG4]. 
IFRS 1 requires an entity that is unable to determine whether a particular portion of an adjustment is a 
transition adjustment or a change in estimate, to treat that portion as a change in accounting estimate 
under IAS 8.32-38, with appropriate disclosures as required by IAS 8.39-40. [IFRS 1.IG58B, IAS 8.36, IAS 8.37, 
IAS 8.39, IAS 8.40]. The distinction between changes in accounting policies and changes in accounting 
estimates is discussed in detail in Chapter 3 at section 4.2. 
If a first-time adopter concludes that estimates under previous GAAP were made in error, it distinguishes 
the correction of those errors from changes in accounting policies in its reconciliations from previous GAAP 
to IFRS accounting standards (see section 6.3.1 below). [IFRS 1.26]. 


International GAAP® 2025 
Chapter 06 
First-time adoption 
318 
Illustration 4-1 below demonstrates how an entity deals with estimates on the date of transition to IFRS 
accounting standards and in comparative periods included in its first IFRS financial statements. 
Illustration 4-1: 
Application of IFRS 1 to estimates 
Entity A’s first IFRS financial statements have a reporting date of 31 December 20X3 and include comparative 
information for one year. In its previous GAAP financial statements for 31 December 20X2, Entity A accounted 
for its pension plan on a cash basis. However, under IAS 19 Employee Benefits the plan is classified as a defined 
benefit plan and actuarial estimates are required. 
Entity A will need to make estimates under IFRS accounting standards at the relevant date that reflect 
conditions that existed at the relevant date. This means, for example, that Entity A’s: 
• 
Discount rates at 1 January 20X2 (date of transition to IFRS accounting standards) and 31 December 
20X2 for the pension plan reflect market conditions at those dates 
• 
Actuarial assumptions at 1 January 20X2 and 31 December 20X2 about future employee turnover rates 
do not reflect conditions that arose after those dates, such as a significant increase in estimated 
employee turnover rates as a result of a redundancy plan in 20X3 [IFRS 1.IG Example 1] 
Entity B accounted for inventories at the lower of cost and net realisable value under its previous GAAP. Entity 
B’s accounting policy is consistent with the requirements of IAS 2 Inventories. Under previous GAAP, the goods 
were accounted for at a price of CU1.25/kg. Due to changes in market circumstances, Entity B ultimately could 
only sell the goods in the following period for CU0.90/kg. 
Assuming that Entity B’s estimate of the net realisable value was not in error, it will account for the goods at 
CU1.25/kg upon transition to IFRS accounting standards and will make no adjustments because the estimate 
was not in error and its accounting policy was consistent with IFRS accounting standards. The effect of selling 
the goods for CU0.90/kg will be reflected in the period in which they were sold. 
Entity C’s first IFRS financial statements have a reporting date of 31 December 20X3 and include comparative 
information for one year. In its previous GAAP financial statements for 31 December 20X1, Entity C accounted 
for a provision of CU150,000 in connection with a court case. Entity C’s accounting policy was consistent with 
the requirements of IAS 37 Provisions, Contingent Liabilities and Contingent Assets, except for the fact that 
Entity C did not discount the provision for the time value of money. The discounted value of the provision at 
31 December 20X1 would have been CU135,000. The case was settled for CU190,000 during 20X2. 
In its opening IFRS statement of financial position at 1 January 20X2, Entity C will measure the provision at 
CU135,000. IFRS 1 does not permit an entity to adjust the estimate itself, unless it was in error, but does 
require an adjustment to reflect the difference in accounting policies. The unwinding of the discount and the 
adjustment due to the under-provision will be included in the comparative statement of profit or loss and other 
comprehensive income for 20X2. 
Entity D’s first IFRS financial statements have a reporting date of 31 December 20X3 and include comparative 
information for one year. In its previous GAAP financial statements for 31 December 20X2, Entity D did not 
recognise a provision for a court case arising from events that occurred in September 20X2. When the court 
case was concluded on 30 June 20X3, Entity D was required to pay CU1,000,000 and paid this on 10 July 
20X3. 
In preparing its comparative statement of financial position at 31 December 20X2, the treatment of the court 
case at that date depends on the reason why Entity D did not recognise a provision under its previous GAAP at 
that date. 
Scenario 1 - Previous GAAP was consistent with IAS 37. At the date of preparing its 20X2 financial 
statements, Entity D concluded that the recognition criteria were not met. In this case, Entity D’s assumptions 
under IFRS accounting standards are required to be consistent with its assumptions under previous GAAP. 
Therefore, Entity D does not recognise a provision at 31 December 20X2 and the effect of settling the court 
case is reflected in the 20X3 statement of profit or loss and other comprehensive income. 
Scenario 2 - Previous GAAP was not consistent with IAS 37. Therefore, Entity D develops estimates under 
IAS 37, which requires that an entity determines whether a present obligation exists at the end of the 
reporting period by taking account of all available evidence, including any additional evidence provided by 
events after the end of the reporting period. Similarly, under IAS 10, the resolution of a court case after the 
end of the reporting period is an adjusting event if it confirms that the entity had a present obligation at that 
date. In this instance, the resolution of the court case confirms that Entity D had a liability in September 20X2 
(when the events occurred that gave rise to the court case). Therefore, Entity D recognises a provision at 
31 December 20X2. Entity D measures that provision by discounting the CU1,000,000 paid on 10 July 20X3 
to its present value, using a discount rate that complies with IAS 37 and reflects market conditions at 
31 December 20X2. [IFRS 1.IG Example 1]. 
Some of the potential consequences of applying IAS 37 resulting in changes in the way an entity accounts 
for provisions are addressed in section 7.13 below. 


International GAAP® 2025 
Chapter 06 
First-time adoption 
319 
4.3 
Derecognition of financial assets and financial liabilities 
Extract from IFRS 1 
Derecognition of financial assets and financial liabilities 
B2 
Except as permitted by paragraph B3, a first-time adopter shall apply the derecognition 
requirements in IFRS 9 prospectively for transactions occurring on or after the date of 
transition to IFRSs. For example, if a first-time adopter derecognised non-derivative financial 
assets or non-derivative financial liabilities in accordance with its previous GAAP as a result of a 
transaction that occurred before the date of transition to IFRSs, it shall not recognise those 
assets and liabilities in accordance with IFRSs (unless they qualify for recognition as a result of 
a later transaction or event). 
B3 
Despite paragraph B2, an entity may apply the derecognition requirements in IFRS 9 
retrospectively from a date of the entity’s choosing, provided that the information needed to 
apply IFRS 9 to financial assets and financial liabilities derecognised as a result of past 
transactions was obtained at the time of initially accounting for those transactions. 
IFRS 1’s requirements on derecognition of financial assets and financial liabilities, [IFRS 1.B2, IFRS 1.B3], will 
effectively prevent most first-time adopters from restating transactions that occurred before the date of 
transition to IFRS accounting standards. 
A first-time adopter that derecognised non-derivative financial assets and financial liabilities before the 
date of transition to IFRS accounting standards and chose not to apply the derecognition requirements in 
IFRS 9 Financial Instruments retrospectively from an earlier date does not recognise these items under IFRS 
accounting standards even if they meet the IFRS 9 recognition criteria. [IFRS 1.IG53]. An entity does not 
recognise financial assets and financial liabilities in its opening statement of financial position that do not 
qualify for recognition in accordance with IFRS 9, or have already qualified for derecognition in accordance 
with IFRS 9. [IFRS 1.IG54]. 
Some arrangements for the transfers of assets, particularly securitisations, could last for some time, with 
the result that transfers could be made both before and after (or on) the date of transition to IFRS 
accounting standards under the same arrangement. IFRS 1 clarifies that transfers made under such 
arrangements fall within the first-time adoption exception only if they occurred before the date of transition 
to IFRS accounting standards (assuming the entity does not choose to apply IFRS 1.B3). [IFRS 1.B3]. 
Transfers on or after the date of transition to IFRS accounting standards are subject to the full 
requirements of IFRS 9. [IFRS 1.IG53]. 
However, an entity could have derecognised, under its previous GAAP, non-derivative financial assets and 
financial liabilities when they were transferred to a structured entity as part of a securitisation programme, 
which is considered to be a controlled entity under IFRS 10 Consolidated Financial Statements. 
How we see it 
IFRS 1 contains no specific exceptions and exemptions to retrospective application of IFRS 10 except for 
those for non-controlling interests (see section 4.8 below). Accordingly, its consolidation requirements 
are applied fully retrospectively by first-time adopters. 
Therefore, in this case, those assets and liabilities will be re-recognised on transition to IFRS accounting 
standards by way of the application of IFRS 10 rather than through application of IFRS 9. Of course, if the 
structured entity itself then subsequently achieved derecognition of those assets and liabilities under the 
entity’s previous GAAP (other than by transfer to another structured entity or member of the entity’s 
group), then the items remain derecognised on transition. 


International GAAP® 2025
