First-time adoption 
320 
4.4 
Hedge accounting: general 
Extract from IFRS 1 
Hedge accounting 
B4 
As required by IFRS 9, at the date of transition to IFRSs an entity shall: 
(a) 
measure all derivatives at fair value; and 
(b) 
eliminate all deferred losses and gains arising on derivatives that were reported in 
accordance with previous GAAP as if they were assets or liabilities. 
B5 
An entity shall not reflect in its opening IFRS statement of financial position a hedging relationship 
of a type that does not qualify for hedge accounting in accordance with IFRS 9 (for example, many 
hedging relationships where the hedging instrument is a stand-alone written option or a net 
written option; or where the hedged item is a net position in a cash flow hedge for another risk 
than foreign currency risk). However, if an entity designated a net position as a hedged item in 
accordance with previous GAAP, it may designate as a hedged item in accordance with IFRSs an 
individual item within that net position, or a net position if that meets the requirements in 
paragraph 6.6.1 of IFRS 9, provided that it does so no later than the date of transition to IFRSs. 
B6 
If, before the date of transition to IFRSs, an entity had designated a transaction as a hedge but 
the hedge does not meet the conditions for hedge accounting in IFRS 9, the entity shall apply 
paragraphs 6.5.6 and 6.5.7 of IFRS 9 to discontinue hedge accounting. Transactions entered 
into before the date of transition to IFRSs shall not be retrospectively designated as hedges. 
In sections 4.4 to 4.7 below, we discuss the hedge accounting treatment for first-time adopters under 
IFRS 9. [IFRS 1.B4, IFRS 1.B5, IFRS 1.B6]. 
First-time adoption issues relating to hedge accounting in the opening IFRS statement of financial position 
are discussed in section 4.5 below and subsequent measurement issues are discussed in section 4.6 below. 
Hedge accounting is dealt with comprehensively in Chapter 49. 
Summary: 
On transition: 
• 
A first-time adopter separately accounts for all derivatives as assets or liabilities measured at fair value 
(regardless of the previous accounting treatment). [IFRS 1.B4(a)]. However, deferred losses and gains 
arising on derivatives that were reported in accordance with previous GAAP as if they were assets and 
liabilities are eliminated. [IFRS 1.B4(b)]. See section 4.5.1 below. 
• 
A first-time adopter can only reflect a hedging relationship that still exists in its opening IFRS statement 
of financial position if it was designated as such on or before the date of transition to IFRS accounting 
standards (i.e., hedges cannot be designated retrospectively). [IFRS 1.B5, IFRS 1.B6, IFRS 1.IG60]. In our 
view, such designation could be made under previous GAAP or in anticipation of adoption of IFRS 
accounting standards. See section 4.5.2 below. 
• 
A first-time adopter can only reflect a hedging relationship of a type that qualifies for hedge 
accounting in accordance with IFRS 9, which consists only of eligible hedging instruments and 
eligible hedged items as per IFRS 9.6.4.1(a). Previous GAAP hedge documentation and effectiveness 
assessments that were not fully compliant with the criteria in IFRS 9.6.4.1(b)-(c) do not preclude the 
hedging relationship from qualifying for hedge accounting under IFRS 9 (see section 4.4.1 below). 
[IFRS 1.B5, IFRS 9.6.4.1]. However, unless updated so that the qualifying criteria for hedge accounting 
are fully met on or before the date of transition to IFRS accounting standards, the entity will need to 
follow the hedge discontinuation requirements in IFRS 9.6.5.6-6.5.7 immediately after transition. 
[IFRS 1.B6, IFRS 9.6.5.6, IFRS 9.6.5.7, IFRS 1.IG60]. 
• 
If the hedging relationship is of a type that qualifies for hedge accounting under IFRS 9, the entity must 
reflect the hedge accounting under previous GAAP in the opening IFRS statement of financial position 
by applying the implementation guidance in IFRS 1 on fair value or cash flow hedges, as at the date of 
transition. [IFRS 1.IG60A, IFRS 1.IG60B]. See sections 4.5.3 to 4.5.6 below. 
• 
If the hedging relationship is not of a type that qualifies for hedge accounting under IFRS 9, an entity is 
required to remove that relationship from its opening IFRS statement of financial position (except for the 
exception relating to certain net positions designated as a hedged item under previous GAAP - see 
section 4.5.2 below). [IFRS 1.B5, IFRS 9.6.4.1(a)]. Any adjustments to the carrying amounts of hedged items 
or deferrals of hedging gains or losses are, therefore, eliminated. See sections 4.5.1 and 4.5.2 below. 
• 
When the hedging instrument is no longer outstanding at the date of transition to IFRS accounting 
standards and, under previous GAAP, the entity had already recognised its result in profit or loss 
without amortisation, no restatement is required. See Figure 4-2 below. 


International GAAP® 2025 
Chapter 06 
First-time adoption 
321 
Subsequent accounting: 
• 
Immediately after transition (i.e., after the date of the opening IFRS statement of financial position), 
the entity follows the normal hedge accounting provisions of IFRS 9 prospectively. If the hedge 
reflected in the opening statement of financial position does not fully comply with the qualifying criteria 
for hedge accounting in IFRS 9 (including hedge documentation and hedge effectiveness) the entity 
applies IFRS 9.6.5.6-6.5.7 to discontinue hedge accounting prospectively. [IFRS 1.IG60, IFRS 1.B6, 
IFRS 9.6.4.1, IFRS 9.6.5.6, IFRS 9.6.5.7]. See section 4.6 below. 
Figure 4-2 below sets out a decision chart for the treatment of hedge accounting in the opening 
IFRS statement of financial position and subsequent periods. Hedge accounting in the opening 
IFRS statement of financial position is discussed in section 4.5 below and hedge accounting after transition 
to IFRS accounting standards is dealt with in section 4.6 below. 
Figure 4-2: 
Treatment of hedge accounting in the first IFRS financial statements 
 


International GAAP® 2025 
Chapter 06 
First-time adoption 
322 
4.4.1 
Paragraphs B5 and B6 of IFRS 1 when applying IFRS 9 
IFRS 1.B5 and IFRS 1.B6 refer to a ‘hedging relationship of a type that does not qualify for hedge accounting’ 
and the ‘conditions for hedge accounting in IFRS 9’ when specifying the recognition (or otherwise) of hedging 
relationships in the opening IFRS statement of financial position and subsequent accounting, respectively. 
[IFRS 1.B5, IFRS 1.B6]. However, IFRS 9 does not set out ‘hedging relationship types’ or ‘conditions for hedge 
accounting’. Instead, it sets out qualifying criteria which consist of (a) eligibility, (b) formal designation and 
documentation, and (c) hedge effectiveness. [IFRS 9.6.4.1]. When IFRS 9 was issued, the wording in IFRS 1.B5 
and IFRS 1.B6 was not updated for IFRS 9, and IFRS 1 was unclear as to whether the same two-step approach 
above (i.e., ‘type’ test and ‘condition’ test) continues to apply. 
How we see it 
The IASB did not intend to change the requirements of IFRS 1 for recognising a hedging relationship in 
the opening IFRS statement of financial position or the subsequent accounting upon issuance of IFRS 9. 
Therefore, we believe that the reference to a ‘hedging relationship of a type that does not qualify for 
hedge accounting’ in IFRS 1.B5 relates only to the qualifying criterion in IFRS 9.6.4.1(a) (i.e., eligible 
hedging instruments and hedged items) and the ‘conditions for hedge accounting’ in IFRS 1.B6 relate to 
the qualifying criteria in IFRS 9.6.4.1(b)-(c). The discussions in sections 4.4 above to 4.7 below are based 
on this view. 
The Annual improvements to IFRS accounting standards issued in July 2024 clarified the above position. 
The amendments added a cross-reference to IFRS 9.6.4.1(a) from IFRS 1.B5 to clarify that the phrase 
‘hedging relationship of a type that does not qualify for hedge accounting’ in paragraph B5 of IFRS 1 relates 
only to the ‘eligibility’ of a hedging relationship (i.e., there are eligible hedging instruments and eligible 
hedged items). Also, the phrase ‘conditions for hedge accounting’ in IFRS 1.B6 was replaced with ‘qualifying 
criteria for hedge accounting in IFRS 9.6.4.1(b)-(c)’. The amendments to IFRS 1 must be applied for annual 
reporting periods beginning on or after 1 January 2026 and early application is permitted. [IFRS 1(2026).B5, 
IFRS 1(2026).B6, IFRS 9.6.4.1] 
4.4.2 
Applicability of IAS 39 hedge requirements 
A transition requirement of IFRS 9 for hedge accounting permits an entity already applying IFRS 
accounting standards to choose as its accounting policy to continue to apply the hedge accounting 
requirements of IAS 39 Financial Instruments: Recognition and Measurement to all of its hedging 
relationships instead of the requirements in Chapter 6 of IFRS 9. [IFRS 9.7.2.21]. First-time adopters do not 
have this choice because IFRS 1 prohibits first-time adopters from applying the transition provisions in 
other IFRS accounting standards, except as specified in Appendices B-E. [IFRS 1.9]. Further, IFRS 1.B4-B6 
contain mandatory exceptions for hedge accounting which do not refer to the transition requirements of 
IFRS 9, and the basis for conclusion attached to IFRS 9 under the heading “Transition related to the 
hedge accounting requirements” states “The IASB decided not to change the requirements of IFRS 1 for 
hedge accounting. The IASB noted that a first-time adopter would need to look at the entire population of 
possible hedging relationships and assess which ones would meet the qualifying criteria of the new hedge 
accounting model.” [IFRS 9.BC7.52]. 
How we see it 
Accordingly, a first-time adopter applies IFRS 9’s requirements on hedge accounting. This is in 
contrast to an entity already applying IFRS accounting standards, who would be permitted to continue 
to apply IAS 39 hedge accounting. Nevertheless, in our view, first-time adopters are permitted to 
apply IAS 39 hedge accounting to a fair value hedge of the interest rate exposure of a portfolio of 
financial assets or financial liabilities (and only to such a hedge) (see Chapter 49 at section 10.2). 
This is because this is permitted by Chapter 6 of IFRS 9, rather than being a transitional provision 
included in Chapter 7 of IFRS 9. 


International GAAP® 2025 
Chapter 06 
First-time adoption 
323 
4.5 
Hedge accounting in the opening IFRS statement of financial 
position 
4.5.1 
Measurement of derivatives and elimination of deferred gains and losses 
Under its previous GAAP, an entity’s accounting policies might have included a number of accounting 
treatments for derivatives that formed part of a hedging relationship. For example, accounting policies 
might have included those where the derivative was: 
• 
Not explicitly recognised as an asset or liability (e.g., in the case of a forward contract used to hedge an 
expected but uncontracted future transaction) 
• 
Recognised as an asset or liability but at an amount different from its fair value (e.g., a purchased 
option recognised at its original cost, perhaps less amortisation; or an interest rate swap accounted for 
by accruing the periodic interest payments and receipts) 
• 
Subsumed within the accounting for another asset or liability (e.g., a foreign currency denominated 
monetary item and a matching forward contract or swap accounted for as a ‘synthetic’ functional 
currency denominated monetary item) 
Whatever the previous accounting treatment, a first-time adopter must account for derivatives in the 
opening IFRS statement of financial position as assets or liabilities measured at fair value in accordance 
with IFRS 9 (for the treatment of embedded derivatives, see section 4.11 below). [IFRS 1.B4(a)]. 
All derivatives are measured at fair value through profit or loss, other than those that are financial 
guarantee contracts, a commitment to provide a loan at a below-market interest rate, a loan commitment 
that is subject to the impairment requirements of IFRS 9, or those that are designated and effective as 
hedging instruments. Accordingly, the difference between the previous carrying amount of these 
derivatives (which may have been zero) and their fair value is recognised as an adjustment of the balance of 
retained earnings at the date of transition to IFRS accounting standards. [IFRS 1.IG58A]. If an entity is unable 
to determine whether a particular portion of an adjustment is a transition adjustment (i.e., a change in 
accounting policy) or a change in estimate, it must treat that portion as a change in accounting estimate, 
with the appropriate disclosures in accordance with IAS 8. [IFRS 1.IG58B, IAS 8.36, IAS 8.37, IAS 8.39, IAS 8.40]. The 
distinction between changes in accounting policies and changes in accounting estimates is discussed in 
detail in Chapter 3 at section 4.2. 
Hedge accounting policies under an entity’s previous GAAP might also have included one or both of the 
following accounting treatments: 
• 
Derivatives were measured at fair value but, to the extent they were regarded as hedging future 
transactions, the gain (or loss) arising was reported as a liability (or asset) such as deferred (or 
accrued) income. 
• 
Realised gains or losses arising on the termination of a previously unrecognised derivative used in a 
hedging relationship (such as an interest rate swap hedging a borrowing) were included in the 
statement of financial position as deferred or accrued income and amortised over the remaining term 
of the hedged exposure. 
In all cases, an entity is required to eliminate deferred gains and losses arising on derivatives that were 
reported in accordance with previous GAAP as if they were assets or liabilities. [IFRS 1.B4(b)]. In contrast to 
adjustments made to restate derivatives at fair value, IFRS 1’s implementation guidance does not specify in 
general terms how to deal with adjustments to eliminate deferred gains or losses, i.e., whether they are 
taken to retained earnings or a separate component of equity. 
How we see it 
We believe that the requirement to eliminate deferred gains and losses does not extend to those that 
have been included in the carrying amount of other assets or liabilities that will continue to be recognised 
under IFRS accounting standards. For example, under an entity’s previous GAAP, the carrying amount of 
non-financial assets such as inventories or property, plant and equipment might have included the 
equivalent of a basis adjustment (i.e., hedging gains or losses were considered an integral part of the 
asset’s cost). Of course, entities also need to consider any other provisions of IFRS 1 that apply to those 
hedged items, e.g., whether any of the exemptions such as those for business combinations (see 
section 5.2 below) or deemed cost (see section 5.4 below) will be used. 


International GAAP® 2025 
Chapter 06 
First-time adoption 
324 
4.5.2 
Hedging relationships reflected in the opening IFRS statement of financial 
position 
IFRS 1 states that a first-time adopter must not reflect a hedging relationship in its opening 
IFRS statement of financial position if that hedging relationship is of a type that does not qualify for 
hedge accounting under IFRS 9 (see section 4.4.1 above). [IFRS 9.6.4.1(a)]. As examples of this, IFRS 1 
cites many hedging relationships where the hedging instrument is a stand-alone written option or a net 
written option; or where the hedged item is a net position in a cash flow hedge for another risk than 
foreign currency risk. [IFRS 1.B5]. Previous GAAP hedge documentation and effectiveness that were not 
fully compliant with the criteria in IFRS 9.6.4.1(b)-(c) do not mean that the hedging relationship is of a 
type that does not qualify for hedge accounting under IFRS 9. [IFRS 9.6.4.1(b), IFRS 9.6.4.1(c)]. (See 
section 4.6 below for requirements for post-transition date hedge accounting). 
However, if an entity designated a net position as a hedged item under its previous GAAP, it could 
designate as a hedged item under IFRS accounting standards an individual item within that net position, or 
a net position that meets the requirements in IFRS 9.6.6.1, provided that it does so no later than the date 
of transition to IFRS accounting standards. [IFRS 1.B5, IFRS 9.6.6.1]. In other words, such designation would 
allow the hedging relationship to be reflected in the opening IFRS statement of financial position. 
On the other hand, a hedging relationship designated under an entity’s previous GAAP is reflected in its 
opening IFRS statement of financial position if that hedging relationship consists of eligible hedging 
instruments and eligible hedged items in IFRS 9.6.4.1(a), regardless of whether or not the hedge 
designation, documentation and effectiveness criteria for hedge accounting under IFRS 9.6.4.1(b)-(c) are 
met on the date of transition. [IFRS 1.B5, IFRS 9.6.4.1]. 
4.5.2.A 
Prohibition on retrospective designation 
A first-time adopter is not permitted to designate hedges retrospectively in relation to transactions entered 
into before the date of transition to IFRS accounting standards. [IFRS 1.B6]. Instead, it must apply the 
requirements prospectively. 
In the Basis for Conclusions, it is explained that: 
“… it is unlikely that most entities would have adopted IAS 39’s criteria for (a) documenting hedges at 
their inception and (b) testing the hedges for effectiveness, even if they intended to continue the same 
hedging strategies after adopting IAS 39. Furthermore, retrospective designation of hedges (or 
retrospective reversal of their designation) could lead to selective designation of some hedges to 
report a particular result.” [IFRS 1.BC75]. 
While this paragraph refers to IAS 39’s criteria, the IASB has since reinforced the above basis for 
conclusion when it issued IFRS 9, which says the following in its Basis for Conclusions: 
“To the extent that an entity wants to apply hedge accounting, those hedging relationships should be 
documented on or before the transition date. This is consistent with the transition requirements for 
existing users of IFRS and the existing transition requirements of IFRS 1 …”. [IFRS 9.BC7.52]. 
4.5.2.B 
Designation in anticipation of adoption of IFRS accounting standards 
If a first-time adopter, in anticipation of the adoption of IFRS accounting standards, decides to designate a 
transaction as a hedge under IFRS 9 and completes the required documentation sometime before the date 
of transition to IFRS accounting standards, some have questioned whether upon adoption of IFRS 
accounting standards, hedge accounting is applied prior to the date of transition. 
How we see it 
In our view, as long as the hedge is properly designated and documented in accordance with IFRS 9.6.4.1 
prior to the date of transition, the first-time adopter applies hedge accounting from the date that it met 
the requirements. As explained in section 4.5.2.A above, a first-time adopter cannot designate a 
transaction retrospectively as a hedge under IFRS accounting standards. 


International GAAP® 2025 
Chapter 06 
First-time adoption 
325 
4.5.3 
Reflecting cash flow hedges in the opening IFRS statement of financial 
position 
A first-time adopter could have deferred gains and losses on a cash flow hedge of a forecast transaction 
under its previous GAAP. If, at the date of transition to IFRS accounting standards, the hedged forecast 
transaction is not highly probable, but is expected to occur, the entire deferred gain or loss is recognised in 
the cash flow hedge reserve within equity. [IFRS 1.IG60B]. This is consistent with the treatment required for 
deferred gains or losses on cash flow hedges applicable after the transition to IFRS accounting standards 
(see Chapter 49 at section 7.2.1 and section 8.3.2). [IFRS 9.6.5.11(d), IFRS 9.6.5.12(a)]. 
How we see it 
The standard is not explicit as to how an entity deals with such a hedge if, at the date of transition to 
IFRS accounting standards, the forecast transaction is highly probable. However, it would make no 
sense if the hedge of a transaction that is no longer highly probable but is still expected to occur were 
required to be reflected in the cash flow hedge reserve within equity in the opening IFRS statement of 
financial position as described above, but the hedge of the highly probable forecast transaction were 
not. Therefore, a cash flow hedge is reflected in the opening IFRS statement of financial position in the 
way set out above if the hedged item is a forecast transaction that is highly probable (see 
Illustration 4-2 below). 
It also follows that a cash flow hedge of the variability in cash flows attributable to a particular risk 
associated with a recognised asset or liability (such as all or some future interest payments on variable rate 
debt) is reflected in the opening IFRS statement of financial position. To do otherwise would allow an entity 
to choose not to designate (in accordance with IFRS 9) certain cash flow hedges, say those that are in a loss 
position, until one day after its date of transition, thereby allowing associated hedging losses to bypass 
profit or loss completely. However, this would effectively result in the retrospective de-designation of 
hedges to achieve a desired result, thereby breaching the general principle of IFRS 1 (i.e., a first-time 
adopter cannot designate a hedging relationship retrospectively). 
If, at the date of transition to IFRS accounting standards, the forecast transaction was not expected to 
occur, consistent with the requirements of IFRS 9.6.5.6-6.5.7 and IFRS 9.6.5.12(b), a first-time adopter 
reclassifies any related deferred gains and losses that are not expected to be recovered into retained 
earnings. [IFRS 1.IG60B, IFRS 9.6.5.6, IFRS 9.6.5.7, IFRS 9.6.5.12(b)]. 
Illustration 4-2: 
Unrecognised gains and losses on existing cash flow hedge 
Entity A has the euro (€) as its functional currency. In September 20X1, it entered into a forward currency 
contract to sell US dollars (US$) for euros in twelve months to hedge US$ denominated sales that it forecasts 
are highly probable to occur in September 20X2. Entity A prepares its first IFRS financial statements and its 
date of transition to IFRS accounting standards is 1 January 20X2. The historical cost of the forward contract 
is €nil and at the date of transition to IFRS accounting standards it had a positive fair value of €100. 
Case 1: Gains and losses deferred 
Under Entity A’s previous GAAP, until the sales occurred the forward contract was recognised in the statement 
of financial position at its fair value and the resulting gain or loss was deferred in the statement of financial 
position as a liability or an asset. When the sale occurred, any deferred gain or loss was recognised in profit or 
loss as an adjustment to the revenue recognised on the hedged sale. 
This relationship must be reflected in Entity A’s opening IFRS statement of financial position whether or not 
the hedge designation, documentation and effectiveness criteria for hedge accounting under 
IFRS 9.6.4.1(b)-(c) are met on the date of transition to IFRS accounting standards: the deferred gain is 
reclassified to the cash flow hedge reserve within equity and there is no adjustment to the carrying amount 
of the forward contract. [IFRS 1.IG60B]. 
Case 2: Gains and losses unrecognised 
Under Entity A’s previous GAAP, the contract was not recognised in the statement of financial position. When 
the sale occurred, any unrecognised gain or loss was recognised in profit or loss as an offset to the revenue 
recognised on the hedged sale. 
How we see it 
Although Case 2 is more problematic, we consider that it is accounted for in the same way as Case 1. 
The difference between the previous carrying amount of a derivative and its fair value would be 
recognised in the cash flow hedge reserve within equity. 
  


International GAAP® 2025 
Chapter 06 
First-time adoption 
326 
Many existing hedging relationships that were acceptable under previous GAAP do not meet the full hedge 
accounting requirements of IFRS 9.6.4.1 although the hedging relationships are of a type that qualify for 
hedge accounting under IFRS 9.6.4.1(a) and are therefore reflected in the opening IFRS statement of 
financial position. Also, in many such cases hedge ineffectiveness has not previously been assessed or 
separately recognised in profit or loss. For cash flow hedges, ineffectiveness is not recognised in opening 
retained earnings but is deferred, as part of the cash flow hedge reserve within equity. As noted above, the 
entire unrecognised portion of the fair value of the hedging instrument is recognised in the cash flow hedge 
reserve in equity in the opening IFRS statement of financial position. There is no reference to the effective 
portion. [IFRS 1.IG60B]. 
4.5.4 
Reflecting fair value hedges in the opening IFRS statement of financial 
position 
If a first-time adopter has, under its previous GAAP, deferred or not recognised gains and losses on a fair 
value hedge of a hedged item that is not measured at fair value, the entity adjusts the carrying amount of 
the hedged item at the date of transition to IFRS accounting standards. The adjustment, which is essentially 
the effective part of the hedge that was not recognised in the carrying amount of the hedged item under 
the previous GAAP, is calculated as the lower of: [IFRS 1.IG60A] 
(a) That portion of the cumulative change in the fair value of the hedged item that was not recognised 
under previous GAAP 
And 
(b) That portion of the cumulative change in the fair value of the hedging instrument which, under 
previous GAAP, was either (i) not recognised or (ii) deferred in the statement of financial position as an 
asset or liability 
Therefore, an entity could be required to recognise some ineffectiveness in retained earnings in the 
opening statement of financial position for fair value hedges, if the cumulative change in the fair value of 
the hedged item is lower than the unrecognised cumulative change in fair value of the hedging instrument. 
However, if the hedged item fair value adjustment on the date of transition to IFRS accounting standards is 
restricted by the change in fair value of the hedging instrument (i.e., where the cumulative change in the 
fair value of the hedged item is higher than the cumulative change in fair value of the hedging instrument), 
any ineffectiveness is not recognised on the date of transition. 
4.5.5 
Reflecting foreign currency net investment hedges in the opening 
IFRS statement of financial position 
IFRS 1 does not provide explicit guidance on reflecting foreign currency net investment hedges in the 
opening IFRS statement of financial position. 
How we see it 
Since IFRS 9 requires that ongoing IFRS reporting entities account for foreign currency net investment 
hedges similarly to cash flow hedges, it follows that the first-time adoption provisions regarding cash flow 
hedges (see section 4.5.3 above) also apply to hedges of foreign currency net investments. 
Specifically, the entire deferred or unrecognised gain or loss on the hedging instrument is recognised in the 
currency translation adjustment on transition. This will result in some previously deferred or unrecognised 
gains or losses on hedging instruments not being recognised in profit or loss until the foreign operation is 
disposed of. 
IFRS 1 allows a first-time adopter to apply the exemption to reset the cumulative translation difference for 
all foreign operations to zero (see section 5.6 below); however, the standard does not explicitly state that 
this exemption extends to the gain or loss on the hedging instruments for net investment hedges. 
How we see it 
We believe that the requirement to reset the cumulative translation differences also applies to related 
gains and losses on hedging instruments (see section 5.6.1 below). Therefore, a first-time adopter that 
applies the exemption to reset cumulative translation differences to zero recognises those pre-transition 
gains and losses on the hedging instruments in the opening balance of retained earnings to avoid a 
disparity between the treatment of the gains and losses on the hedged item and the hedging instrument. 
Those pre-transition gains and losses on the hedging instruments that were recognised in retained 
earnings will not be reclassified to profit or loss upon disposal of a foreign operation. 


International GAAP® 2025 
Chapter 06 
First-time adoption 
327 
4.5.6 
‘Costs of hedging’ (where excluded from the hedging relationship under 
previous GAAP) 
The time value of options, forward points of forward contracts and foreign currency basis spreads 
(collectively referred to as ‘costs of hedging’) might not have been accounted for in the manner permitted 
by IFRS 9. For instance, they might have been amortised under previous GAAP, regardless of whether there 
was a transaction-related or a time-period related hedged item. Also, the amount deferred under previous 
GAAP might not represent the ‘aligned’ value of ‘costs of hedging’ if the critical terms of the hedging 
instrument are not fully aligned with those of the hedged item (see Chapter 49 at section 7.5.1.A). 
By separately accruing the option time value or forward points, for example, under previous GAAP and only 
locking in the intrinsic or spot value of the hedged item, an entity has made a clear intention that only the 
intrinsic or spot value forms part of the hedge. Therefore, the special cash flow hedging rules in 
IFRS 1.IG60B (see section 4.5.3 above) apply purely to the full unrecognised intrinsic or spot value. 
[IFRS 1.IG60B]. Similarly, if the ‘costs of hedging’ had been excluded from a fair value hedging relationship, 
they would not form part of the adjustment to the hedging instrument taken to retained earnings required 
by IFRS 1.IG60A (see section 4.5.4 above). [IFRS 1.IG60A]. 
How we see it 
IFRS 1 does not provide specific guidance on how to account for ‘costs of hedging’ excluded from a 
hedging relationship on transition to IFRS accounting standards. In our view, the same approach as 
applied to cash flow hedges (i.e., IFRS 1.IG60B) is also applied to the ‘costs of hedging’. 
Accordingly, any previously unrecognised amount of the fair value of the option time value, forward 
points or foreign currency basis spread would be recognised in a separate component of equity 
recognised at the date of transition. Therefore, the ‘costs of hedging’ excluded from the hedging 
relationship under previous GAAP are neither recognised in the cash flow hedge reserve nor in retained 
earnings. The same accounting applies to ‘costs of hedging’ excluded from cash flow and fair value 
hedging relationships. 
In many cases, the amount of ‘costs of hedging’ that was amortised or deferred under previous GAAP and is 
recognised as a separate component of equity on transition includes accumulated differences due to the 
different accounting treatments under previous GAAP compared to IFRS 9 (rather than, say, representing 
only the aligned time value of the option, forward element or foreign currency basis spread). Any such 
accumulated differences are included in the amount deferred under previous GAAP and recognised in equity. 
If the ‘costs of hedging’ were not excluded from the hedging relationship under previous GAAP, the above 
accounting does not apply but instead they give rise to ineffectiveness. The treatment of ineffectiveness for 
cash flow hedges, fair value hedges and foreign currency net investment hedges in the opening 
IFRS statement of financial position is explained in sections 4.5.3 to 4.5.5 above. 
4.6 
Hedge accounting: subsequent treatment 
IFRS 1’s implementation guidance explains that hedge accounting can be applied prospectively only from 
the date the hedging relationship is fully designated and documented. In other words, after the date of 
transition to IFRS accounting standards, hedge accounting under IFRS 9 can be applied only if the qualifying 
criteria in IFRS 9.6.4.1 are met. [IFRS 9.6.4.1, IFRS 9.6.5.1]. Therefore, if the hedging instrument is still held at 
the date of transition to IFRS accounting standards, the designation and documentation of the hedging 
relationship must be completed (and the effectiveness requirements met) on or before that date if the 
hedging relationship is to qualify for hedge accounting from that date. [IFRS 1.IG60]. For further discussion on 
measuring ineffectiveness, see Chapter 49 at section 7.4. 
4.6.1 
Discontinuation of a hedging relationship 
Before the date of transition to IFRS accounting standards an entity might have designated as a hedge a 
transaction under previous GAAP that is of a type that qualifies for hedge accounting under IFRS 9, which 
consists only of eligible hedging instruments and eligible hedged items in IFRS 9.6.4.1(a), but does not 
meet other qualifying criteria in IFRS 9 (i.e., IFRS 9.6.4.1 (b)-(c)) at the date of transition to IFRS 
accounting standards. [IFRS 9.6.4.1]. In this case, the entity follows the general requirements in 
IFRS 9.6.5.6-6.5.7 for discontinuing hedge accounting subsequent to the date of transition to IFRS 
accounting standards - these are dealt with in Chapter 49 at section 8.3. [IFRS 1.B6, IFRS 9.6.5.6, IFRS 9.6.5.7]. 


International GAAP® 2025 
Chapter 06 
First-time adoption 
328 
For cash flow hedges, any net cumulative gain or loss that was reclassified to the cash flow hedge reserve 
on the date of transition to IFRS accounting standards (see section 4.5.3 above) remains there until: 
[IFRS 1.IG60B] 
(a) The forecast transaction subsequently results in the recognition of a non-financial asset or non-
financial liability 
(b) The forecast transaction affects profit or loss 
Or 
(c) Subsequently, circumstances change and the forecast transaction is no longer expected to occur, in 
which case any related net cumulative gain or loss that had been reclassified to the cash flow hedge 
reserve on the date of transition to IFRS accounting standards is reclassified to profit or loss 
The requirements above do little more than reiterate the general requirements of IFRS 9, i.e., that hedge 
accounting can only be applied prospectively if the qualifying criteria are met, and entities are expected to 
experience few interpretative problems in dealing with this aspect of the hedge accounting requirements. 
4.6.2 
Ineffectiveness of cash flow and foreign currency net investment hedges in 
continuing hedging relationships 
Hedge accounting in accordance with IFRS 9 is applied prospectively from the date the hedge accounting is 
fully designated and documented. [IFRS 1.IG60]. Accounting for ongoing cash flow hedges and foreign 
currency net investment hedges is discussed in more detail in Chapter 49 at sections 7.2 and 7.3. 
As noted in sections 4.5.3 and 4.5.5 above, ineffectiveness relating to cash flow and foreign currency net 
investment hedges reflected in the opening statement of financial position is not recognised in the opening 
retained earnings but is deferred, as part of the cash flow hedge reserve (or cumulative translation reserve) 
within equity. The entity does not recognise any past ineffectiveness deferred in equity on transition in 
profit or loss as ineffectiveness in subsequent periods. Rather, the entity measures ineffectiveness in 
subsequent periods by reference to cumulative changes in the fair value of the hedging instrument and 
hedged item subsequent to the date of transition (if that is the date from which the hedging relationship 
qualifies under IFRS 9). In order to capture the appropriate cumulative change in fair value of the hedged 
item, where a hypothetical derivative is used to measure ineffectiveness, the ineffectiveness recognised in 
profit or loss on the continuing hedging relationship is determined by reference to the hypothetical 
derivative where terms were set at inception of the hedging relationship, rather than on the date of 
transition to IFRS accounting standards. This means that the fair value movements arising on the hedged 
item which are used in calculating the ongoing adjustments required to the cash flow hedge reserve derive 
from the hypothetical derivative whose terms were set at the inception of the hedging relationship 
(designated under previous GAAP). 
Any ineffectiveness deferred in equity on transition (as part of, say, the cash flow hedge reserve) will 
remain in equity until (a) the forecast transaction subsequently results in the recognition of a non-financial 
asset or non-financial liability (when the amount deferred in equity is included directly in the initial cost or 
other carrying amount of the asset or liability), (b) the forecast transaction affects profit or loss, or (c) 
subsequently circumstances change and the forecast transaction is no longer expected to occur, in which 
case the amount is reclassified from equity to profit or loss. 
How we see it 
This deferral of ineffectiveness on transition implies a discrepancy between the profit or loss impact of 
the hedged item and the amounts reclassified from cash flow hedge reserves under IFRS 9.6.5.11(d) and 
IFRS 9.6.5.12. Therefore, when the hedged item affects several periods (e.g., a hedge of variable 
interest on a debt instrument), it is necessary to recognise this mismatch appropriately over the hedging 
period. For a net investment hedge, the amounts accumulated in the cumulative translation reserve are 
only reclassified to profit or loss on disposal or, in certain circumstances, partial disposal of the foreign 
operation in accordance with IAS 21 (see Chapter 16 at section 6.6 and Chapter 49 at section 7.3). 
In certain cases, no deferral of ineffectiveness will occur, (e.g., where on transition the change in fair 
value of the hedged item exceeds the change in fair value of the hedging instrument). In those 
circumstances, subsequent changes in fair value of the hedging instrument that are effective are 
deferred in the cash flow hedge reserve or for a foreign currency net investment hedge, the cumulative 
translation reserve. 


International GAAP® 2025 
Chapter 06 
First-time adoption 
329 
4.6.3 
Ineffectiveness of fair value hedges in continuing hedging relationships 
Hedge accounting in accordance with IFRS 9 is applied prospectively from the date the hedge accounting is 
fully designated and documented. [IFRS 1.IG60]. Accounting for ongoing fair value hedges is discussed in 
more detail in Chapter 49 at section 7.1. 
As noted in section 4.5.4 above, if the cumulative change in the fair value of the hedged item is lower than 
the unrecognised cumulative change in fair value of the hedging instrument, ineffectiveness relating to fair 
value hedges is recognised in retained earnings on transition. However, if the hedged item’s fair value 
adjustment on transition is restricted by the change in fair value of the hedging instrument (i.e., where the 
cumulative change in the fair value of the hedged item is higher than the cumulative change in fair value of 
the hedging instrument), any ineffectiveness is not recognised on transition. 
How we see it 
The ineffectiveness on a fair value hedge not recognised on transition is not subsequently recognised in 
profit or loss through the next revaluation of the hedged item. Illustration 4-3 below demonstrates how 
an entity treats the ineffectiveness not recognised on transition subsequently. 
 
Illustration 4-3: 
Ineffectiveness of fair value hedge on transition 
At the date of transition to IFRS accounting standards, the cumulative change in the fair value of the hedged 
item is CU100,000 whereas the cumulative change in fair value of the hedging instrument is CU80,000 (and 
therefore, the fair value adjustment to the hedged item is restricted to the change in fair value of the hedging 
instrument, i.e., CU80,000). Following the date from when the hedging relationship is designated under 
IFRS 9, subsequent changes in the fair value of the hedged item attributable to the hedged risk and changes in 
the fair value of the hedging instrument would be recognised in profit or loss (without reference to the 
adjustment made to the hedged item on transition. See also Case 1 in Illustration 4-5 in section 4.7 below for 
an example of adjustments required on transition for fair value hedges). [IFRS 9.6.5.8]. 
As time passes, the mechanics of fair value hedge accounting would normally result in an automatic unwinding 
of 100% of the cumulative fair value changes attributable to the hedged risk so that the carrying amount of the 
hedged item would, at any point in time, reflect fair value adjustments attributable to the hedged risk that are 
consistent with the remaining term of the hedging relationship. However, because at the date of transition, 
some of the cumulative changes in the fair value of the hedged item were not recognised, total fair value 
changes unwound, at any point in time, would outpace the cumulative adjustments applied to the hedged item 
up to that point. In this example, the hedged item carrying amount at maturity would differ from its nominal 
amount by CU20,000 since the original adjustment on transition was CU80,000 but the subsequent 
cumulative net change in fair value that would be recognised in profit or loss would be CU100,000. In order to 
deal with this difference, the CU20,000 would need to be amortised over the term of the hedge in profit or 
loss. For interest-bearing hedged items, this would typically be achieved using the effective interest rate 
method. [IFRS 9.6.5.10]. 
4.6.4 
‘Costs of hedging’ (where excluded from the hedging relationship under 
previous GAAP) 
As explained in section 4.5.6 above, ‘costs of hedging’ that were amortised or deferred under previous 
GAAP, are recognised as a separate component of equity rather than retained earnings on transition, 
including any accumulated difference in the amount deferred under previous GAAP and the amount in 
accordance with IFRS accounting standards. 
IFRS 1 does not provide specific guidance on how to account for ‘costs of hedging’, including the difference 
arising on transition in subsequent periods. 
How we see it 
In our view, the same approach as that described earlier for cash flow hedges (see section 4.6.2 above) is 
applied to the ‘costs of hedging’. In cases where there is a transaction-related hedged item, any amount 
of ‘costs of hedging’ on transition deferred in equity will be: (a) included directly in the initial cost or 
other carrying amount of a non-financial asset or a non-financial liability on its initial recognition, or (b) 
reclassified to profit or loss as a reclassification adjustment in the same period or periods during which 
the hedged expected future cash flows affect profit or loss, depending on whether the hedged item 
results in a non-financial item or not. If there is a time-period related hedged item, the difference will be 
amortised on a systematic and rational basis over the remaining hedging periods. 


International GAAP® 2025 
Chapter 06 
First-time adoption 
330 
If the first-time adopter excludes ’costs of hedging’ from the designation of the hedging relationship 
prospectively under IFRS 9, and the entity is required (in the case of time value of options) or chooses (in 
the case of the forward element of forward contracts and foreign currency basis spreads) to apply the 
‘costs of hedging’ guidance in IFRS 9, the ‘costs of hedging’ will subsequently be accounted for in 
accordance with IFRS 9.6.5.15 and IFRS 9.6.5.16 and IFRS 9.B6.5.29-B6.5.39. [IFRS 9.6.5.15, IFRS 9.6.5.16, 
IFRS 9.B6.5.29-B6.5.39]. If the first-time adopter chooses not to apply the ‘costs of hedging’ guidance to the 
forward element of forward contracts or foreign currency basis spread, movements in the fair value of the 
forward element or foreign currency basis spread are recognised in profit or loss. 
If the first-time adopter does not exclude the ‘costs of hedging’ from the designation of the hedging 
relationship prospectively under IFRS 9, normal hedge accounting is applied but this designation will give 
rise to ineffectiveness to be accounted for in profit or loss. Any amounts deferred in equity for ‘costs of 
hedging’ at the date of transition (see section 4.5.6 above) will be accounted for as described in the ‘How 
we see it’ above. 
See Chapter 49 at section 7.5 for further discussion of accounting for ‘cost of hedging’ under IFRS 9. 
4.7 
Hedge accounting: Illustrations 
Illustrations 4-4 and 4-5 below demonstrate the guidance considered in sections 4.5 to 4.6 above. 
Illustration 4-4: 
Pre-transition cash flow hedges 
Case 1: All hedge accounting qualifying criteria met from date of transition and thereafter 
In 20X0, Entity A borrowed CU10m from a bank. The terms of the loan provide that a floating rate coupon (based 
on a specified index) plus 2% is payable quarterly in arrears and the principal is repayable after 10 years. In 
20X3, Entity A decided to ‘fix’ its coupon payments for the remainder of the term of the loan by entering into a 
seven-year pay-fixed, receive-floating interest rate swap. The swap has a notional amount of CU10m and the 
floating leg resets quarterly based on the same floating rate index as the loan. 
In Entity A’s final financial statements prepared under its previous GAAP, the swap was clearly identified as a 
hedging instrument in a hedge of the loan and was accounted for as such. The fair value of the swap was not 
recognised in Entity A’s statement of financial position and the periodic interest settlements were accrued and 
recognised as an adjustment to the loan interest expense. On 1 January 20X7, Entity A’s date of transition to 
IFRS accounting standards, the loan and the swap were still in place and the swap had a positive fair value of 
CU1m and a CUnil carrying amount. In addition, Entity A met all the qualifying criteria in IFRS 9 to permit the 
use of hedge accounting for this arrangement throughout 20X7 and 20X8. 
In its opening IFRS statement of financial position Entity A would: 
• 
Recognise the interest rate swap as an asset at its fair value of CU1m 
• 
Credit CU1m to a separate component of equity, to be reclassified to profit or loss as the hedged 
transactions (future interest payments on the loan) affect profit or loss 
In addition, hedge accounting would be applied throughout 20X7 and 20X8. 
Case 2: Hedge terminated prior to date of transition 
The facts are as in Case 1 except that in April 20X6 Entity A decided to terminate the hedge and the interest 
rate swap was settled for its then positive fair value of CU1.5m. Under its previous GAAP, Entity A’s stated 
accounting policy in respect of terminated hedges was to defer any realised gain or loss on terminated hedging 
instruments where the hedged exposure remained. These gains or losses would be recognised in profit or loss 
at the same time as gains or losses on the hedged exposure. At the end of December 20X6, Entity A’s 
statement of financial position included a liability (unamortised gain) of CU1.4m. 
In its opening IFRS statement of financial position Entity A would: 
• 
Remove the deferred gain of CU1.4m from the statement of financial position 
• 
Credit CU1.4m to a separate component of equity, to be reclassified to profit or loss as the hedged 
transactions (future interest payments on the loan) affect profit or loss 
How we see it 
IFRS 1 does not explicitly address hedges terminated prior to the date of transition to IFRS accounting 
standards. In our view, because the terminated hedge relates to a transaction that has an ongoing risk 
exposure, the provisions of IFRS 9 on hedge discontinuance are applied to this relationship. 
  
g 


International GAAP® 2025
