Illustration 4-5: 
Existing fair value hedges 
Case 1: All hedge accounting qualifying criteria met from date of transition and thereafter 
On 15 November 20X1, Entity B entered into a forward contract to sell 50,000 barrels of crude oil to hedge all 
changes in the fair value of certain inventory. Entity B prepares its first IFRS financial statements and its date 
of transition to IFRS accounting standards is 1 January 20X2. The historical cost of the forward contract is 
CUnil and at the date of transition to IFRS accounting standards the forward had a negative fair value of CU50. 
In Entity B’s final financial statements prepared under its previous GAAP, the forward contract was clearly 
identified as a hedging instrument in a hedge of the inventory and was accounted for as such. The contract 
was recognised in the statement of financial position as a liability at its fair value and the resulting loss was 
deferred in the statement of financial position as an asset. In the period between 15 November 20X1 and 
1 January 20X2, the fair value of the inventory increased by CU47. In addition, Entity B met all the qualifying 
criteria in IFRS 9 to permit the use of hedge accounting for this arrangement throughout 20X2 until the 
forward expired. 
In its opening IFRS statement of financial position Entity B would: 
• 
Continue to recognise the forward contract as a liability at its fair value of CU50 
• 
Derecognise the CU50 deferred loss asset on the forward contract 
• 
Recognise the crude oil inventory at its historical cost plus CU47 (the lower of the change in fair value of 
the crude oil inventory, CU47, and that of the forward contract, CU50) 
• 
Record the net adjustment of CU3 in retained earnings 
In addition, hedge accounting would be applied throughout 20X2 until the forward contract expired. 
Case 2: All hedge accounting qualifying criteria met from date of transition and thereafter 
In 20X0 Entity C borrowed CU10m from a bank. The terms of the loan provide that a coupon of 8% is payable 
quarterly in arrears and the principal is repayable after 10 years. In 20X3, Entity C decided to alter its coupon 
payments for the remainder of the term of the loan by entering into a seven-year pay-floating, receive-fixed 
interest rate swap. The swap has a notional amount of CU10m and the floating leg resets quarterly based on a 
specified index. 
In Entity C’s final financial statements prepared under its previous GAAP, the swap was clearly identified as a 
hedging instrument in a hedge of the loan and accounted for as such. The fair value of the swap was not 
recognised in Entity C’s statement of financial position and the periodic interest settlements on the swap were 
accrued and recognised as an adjustment to the loan interest expense. 
On 1 January 20X7, Entity C’s date of transition to IFRS accounting standards, the loan and the swap were still 
in place and the swap had a negative fair value of CU1m and a CUnil carrying amount. The cumulative change 
in the fair value of the loan attributable to changes in the floating interest rate was CU1.1m, although this 
change was not recognised in Entity C’s statement of financial position because the loan was accounted for at 
amortised cost. In addition, Entity C met all the qualifying criteria in IFRS 9 to permit the use of hedge 
accounting for this arrangement throughout 20X7 and 20X8. 
In its opening IFRS statement of financial position Entity C would: 
• 
Recognise the interest rate swap as a liability at its fair value of CU1m 
• 
Reduce the carrying amount of the loan by CU1m (the lower of the change in its fair value attributable to 
the hedged risk, CU1.1m, and that of the interest rate swap, CU1m) to CU9m 
In addition, hedge accounting would be applied throughout 20X7 and 20X8. 
Case 3: Hedge terminated prior to date of transition 
The facts are as in Case 2 above except that, in April 20X6, Entity C decided to terminate the fair value hedge 
and the interest rate swap was settled for its then negative fair value of CU1.5m. Under its previous GAAP, 
Entity C’s stated accounting policy in respect of terminated hedges was to defer any gain or loss on the 
hedging instrument as a liability or an asset where the hedged exposure remained and this gain or loss was 
recognised in profit or loss at the same time as the hedged exposure. At the end of December 20X6 the 
unamortised loss recognised as an asset in Entity C’s statement of financial position was CU1.4m. The 
cumulative change through April 20X6 in the fair value of the loan attributable to changes in the floating 
interest rate that had not been recognised was CU1.6m. 
In its opening IFRS statement of financial position, Entity C would: 
• 
Remove the deferred loss asset of CU1.4m from the statement of financial position 
• 
Reduce the carrying amount of the loan by CU1.4m (the lower of the change in its fair value attributable 
to the hedged risk, CU1.6m, and the change in value of the interest rate swap that was deferred in the 
statement of financial position, CU1.4m) 
The CU1.4m adjustment to the loan would be amortised to profit or loss over its remaining term. 


International GAAP® 2025 
Chapter 06 
First-time adoption 
332 
Case 4: Documentation completed after the date of transition 
The facts are as in Case 2 above except that, at the date of transition, Entity C had not prepared 
documentation that would allow it to apply hedge accounting under IFRS 9. Hedge documentation was 
subsequently prepared as a result of which the hedge qualified for hedge accounting with effect from the 
beginning of July 20X7 and through 20X8. 
As in Case 2, in its opening IFRS statement of financial position Entity C would: 
• 
Recognise the interest rate swap as a liability at its fair value of CU1m 
• 
Reduce the carrying amount of the loan by CU1m (the lower of the change in its fair value attributable to 
the hedged risk, CU1.1m, and that of the interest rate swap, CU1m), because the loan was clearly 
identified as a hedged item 
For the period from January 20X7 to June 20X7, hedge accounting would not be available. Accordingly, the 
interest rate swap would be remeasured to its fair value and any gain or loss would be recognised in profit 
or loss with no offset from remeasuring the loan. The CU1m adjustment to the loan would be amortised to 
profit or loss over its remaining term (the amortisation could begin as soon as an adjustment exists and 
must begin no later than when the hedged item ceases to be adjusted for hedging gains and losses). With 
effect from July 20X7 hedge accounting would be applied prospectively. 
4.8 
Non-controlling interests 
A first-time adopter must apply IFRS 10 retrospectively, except for the following requirements that apply 
prospectively from its date of transition to IFRS accounting standards: [IFRS 1.B7] 
(a) The requirement that total comprehensive income is attributed to the owners of the parent and to 
the non-controlling interests even if this results in the non-controlling interests having a deficit 
balance [IFRS 10.B94] 
(b) The requirements on accounting for changes in the parent’s ownership interest in a subsidiary that do 
not result in a loss of control [IFRS 10.23, IFRS 10.B96] 
(c) The requirements on accounting for a loss of control over a subsidiary, and the related requirements in 
IFRS 5 Non-current Assets Held for Sale and Discontinued Operations to classify all assets and liabilities 
of that subsidiary as held for sale [IFRS 10.B97, IFRS 10.B98, IFRS 10.B99, IFRS 5.8A] 
However, if a first-time adopter restates any business combination that occurred prior to its date of 
transition to comply with IFRS 3 Business Combinations it must also apply IFRS 10, including the above 
requirements, from that date onwards (see section 5.2.2 below). [IFRS 1.C1]. 
4.9 
Classification and measurement of financial instruments 
4.9.1 
Classification of financial assets 
IFRS 9 requires a financial asset to be measured at amortised cost if it meets two tests that deal with the 
nature of the business model that holds the financial asset and the contractual cash flow characteristics of 
the financial asset. [IFRS 9.4.1.2]. Also, the standard requires a financial asset to be measured at fair value 
through other comprehensive income if certain conditions are met. [IFRS 9.4.1.2A]. These conditions for 
classification of financial assets are described in detail in Chapter 44 at sections 1 and 1.1. 
IFRS 1.B8 requires a first-time adopter to assess whether a financial asset meets the conditions on the basis 
of the facts and circumstances that exist at the date of transition to IFRS accounting standards. [IFRS 1.B8]. The 
resulting classifications are applied retrospectively. [IFRS 1.IG56]. 
4.9.1.A 
Assessment of a modified time value of money element 
If it is impracticable to assess a modified time value of money element under IFRS 9.B4.1.9B-B4.1.9D on 
the basis of the facts and circumstances that exist at the date of transition to IFRS accounting standards, 
the first-time adopter is required to assess the contractual cash flow characteristics of that financial 
asset on the basis of the facts and circumstances that existed at that date of transition without taking 
into account the requirements related to the modification of the time value of money element in 
IFRS 9.B4.1.9B-B4.1.9D. 
In this case, the entity also applies IFRS 7.42R and discloses the carrying amount at the reporting date of the 
financial assets - whose contractual cash flow characteristics have been assessed based on the facts and 
circumstances that existed at the date of transition without taking into account the requirements related to 
the modification of the time value of money element in IFRS 9.B4.1.9B-B4.1.9D - until those financial assets 
are derecognised (see section 6.4.2 below). [IFRS 1.B8A, IFRS 7.42R, IFRS 9.B4.1.9B, IFRS 9.B4.1.9C, IFRS 9.B4.1.9D]. 


International GAAP® 2025 
Chapter 06 
First-time adoption 
333 
4.9.1.B 
Assessment of whether the fair value of a prepayment feature is insignificant 
Similarly, if it is impracticable to assess whether the fair value of a prepayment feature is insignificant 
under IFRS 9.B4.1.12(c) on the basis of the facts and circumstances that exist at the date of transition to 
IFRS accounting standards, IFRS 1.B8B requires an entity to assess the contractual cash flow 
characteristics of that financial asset on the basis of the facts and circumstances that existed at the date of 
transition without taking into account the exception for prepayment features in IFRS 9.B4.1.12 (see 
Chapter 44 at section 5.4.4.B). 
In this case, the entity also applies IFRS 7.42S and discloses the carrying amount at the reporting date of 
the financial assets - whose contractual cash flow characteristics have been assessed based on the facts 
and circumstances that existed at the date of transition without taking into account the exception for 
prepayment features in IFRS 9.B4.1.12 - until those financial assets are derecognised (see section 6.4.2 
below). [IFRS 1.B8B, IFRS 7.42S, IFRS 9.B4.1.12]. 
4.9.2 
Measurement of financial instruments measured at amortised cost 
For those financial assets and financial liabilities measured at amortised cost in the opening 
IFRS statement of financial position, an entity determines the gross carrying amount of the financial 
assets and the amortised cost of the financial liabilities on the basis of circumstances existing when the 
assets and liabilities first satisfied the recognition criteria in IFRS 9. However, if the entity acquired those 
financial assets and financial liabilities in a past business combination to which the entity elects not to 
apply IFRS 3 retrospectively, their carrying amount in accordance with previous GAAP immediately 
following the business combination is their deemed cost in accordance with IFRS accounting standards at 
that date. [IFRS 1.C4(e), IFRS 1.IG57]. 
IFRS 1.B8C states that if it is impracticable (as defined in IAS 8) for an entity to apply retrospectively the 
effective interest method in IFRS 9, the fair value of the financial asset or the financial liability at the date 
of transition to IFRS accounting standards will be the new gross carrying amount of that financial asset or 
the new amortised cost of that financial liability at the date of transition. [IFRS 1.B8C, IAS 8.5]. 
4.9.3 
Transition adjustments 
An entity treats an adjustment to the carrying amount of a financial asset or financial liability as a 
transition adjustment to be recognised in the opening balance of retained earnings (or another 
component of equity, as appropriate) at the date of transition to IFRS accounting standards only to the 
extent that it results from adopting IFRS 9. [IFRS 1.IG58A]. If an entity is unable to determine whether a 
particular portion of the adjustment is a transition adjustment or a change in estimate, it treats that 
portion as a change in accounting estimate in accordance with IAS 8, with appropriate disclosures, 
[IFRS 1.IG58B], (see section 4.2 above). 
If an investment had been measured at fair value under previous GAAP with revaluation gains recognised 
outside profit or loss but the investment is classified as at fair value through profit or loss under IFRS 9, the 
pre-IFRS 9 revaluation gain that had been recognised outside profit or loss is reclassified into retained 
earnings on initial application of IFRS 9. If, on initial application of IFRS 9, the investment is measured at 
fair value through other comprehensive income in accordance with IFRS 9.4.1.2A or is designated at fair 
value through other comprehensive income in accordance with IFRS 9.5.7.5, then the pre-IFRS 9 
revaluation gain is recognised in a separate component of equity. Subsequently, the entity recognises gains 
and losses on these financial assets in accordance with IFRS 9. [IFRS 1.IG59, IFRS 9.4.1.2A, IFRS 9.5.7.5]. 
4.10 Impairment of financial assets 
IFRS 1.B8D requires a first-time adopter to apply the impairment requirements in Section 5.5 of IFRS 9 
retrospectively subject to IFRS 1.B8E-B8G. [IFRS 1.B8D, IFRS 1.B8E, IFRS 1.B8F, IFRS 1.B8G]. 
At the date of transition to IFRS accounting standards, IFRS 1.B8E requires a first-time adopter to use 
reasonable and supportable information that is available without undue cost or effort (see Chapter 47 at 
section 5.9) to determine the credit risk at the date that the financial instruments were initially recognised 
(or, for loan commitments and financial guarantee contracts, the date that the entity became a party to the 
irrevocable commitment in accordance with IFRS 9.5.5.6) and compare that to the credit risk at the date of 
transition. IFRS 1.B8E references IFRS 9.B7.2.2-B7.2.3 which set out similar transitional rules on adoption 
of IFRS 9 for existing IFRS reporters. [IFRS 1.B8E, IFRS 9.5.5.6, IFRS 9.B7.2.2, IFRS 9.B7.2.3]. 


International GAAP® 2025 
Chapter 06 
First-time adoption 
334 
In order to determine the loss allowance on financial instruments initially recognised (or loan 
commitments or financial guarantee contracts to which the entity became a party to the contract) prior 
to the date of transition to IFRS accounting standards, both on the transition and until the derecognition 
of those items, an entity is required to consider information that is relevant in determining or 
approximating the credit risk at initial recognition. In order to determine or approximate the initial credit 
risk, an entity could consider internal and external information, including portfolio information, in 
accordance with IFRS 9.B5.5.1-B5.5.6 (see Chapter 47 at section 6). [IFRS 9.B5.5.1-B5.5.6, IFRS 9.B7.2.3]. 
A first-time adopter is allowed to apply the following guidance to determine whether there has been a 
significant increase in credit risk since initial recognition: [IFRS 1.B8F] 
(a) The requirements (on financial instruments determined to have low credit risk) in IFRS 9.5.5.10 and 
IFRS 9.B5.5.22-B5.5.24 (see Chapter 47 at section 6.4.1) [IFRS 9.5.5.10, IFRS 9.B5.5.22, IFRS 9.B5.5.23, 
IFRS 9.B5.5.24] 
(b) The rebuttable presumption in IFRS 9.5.5.11 (that there has been a significant increase in credit risk 
since initial recognition) for contractual payments that are more than 30 days past due if an entity will 
apply the impairment requirements by identifying significant increases in credit risk since initial 
recognition for those financial instruments on the basis of past due information (see Chapter 47 at 
section 6.2.2) [IFRS 9.5.5.11] 
A first-time adopter would not be required to determine whether there has been a significant increase in 
credit risk if that would require undue cost or effort. In such a case, IFRS 1.B8G requires a first-time 
adopter to recognise a loss allowance at an amount equal to lifetime expected credit losses at each 
reporting date until that financial instrument is derecognised (unless that financial asset is low credit risk at 
a reporting date, in which case, IFRS 1.B8F(a) applies and, consequently, the entity is allowed to assume 
that the credit risk has not increased significantly since initial recognition (see Chapter 47 at 
section 6.4.1)). [IFRS 1.B8F(a), IFRS 1.B8G, IFRS 9.5.5.10, IFRS 9.B5.5.22-B5.5.24]. 
4.11 Embedded derivatives 
When IFRS 9 requires an entity to separate an embedded derivative from a host contract, [IFRS 9.4.3.3], the 
initial carrying amounts of the components at the date the instrument first satisfied the recognition criteria 
in IFRS 9 must reflect circumstances that existed at that date. If the initial carrying amounts of the 
embedded derivative and host contract cannot be determined reliably, the entire combined contract is 
designated at fair value through profit or loss. [IFRS 1.IG55, IFRS 9.4.3.6]. 
IFRS 1.B9 requires a first-time adopter to assess whether an embedded derivative is required to be 
separated from the host contract and accounted for as a derivative based on conditions that existed at the 
later of the date it first became a party to the contract and the date a reassessment is required by 
IFRS 9.B4.3.11. [IFRS 1.B9, IFRS 9.B4.3.11]. It should be noted that IFRS 9 does not permit embedded 
derivatives to be separated from host contracts that are financial assets (see Chapter 42 at section 3). 
4.12 Government loans 
It is common practice in certain countries for the government to grant loans to entities at below-market 
interest rates, for example, in order to promote economic development. A first-time adopter might not have 
recognised and measured such loans in its previous GAAP financial statements on a basis that complies with 
IFRS accounting standards. IAS 20 Accounting for Government Grants and Disclosure of Government 
Assistance requires such loans to be recognised initially at fair value with the effect of the below-market 
interest rate separately accounted for as a government grant (see Chapter 25 at section 3.4). [IAS 20.10A]. 
The IASB has provided transition relief to first-time adopters in the form of an exception that requires the 
first-time adopter to classify government loans received as a financial liability or an equity instrument in 
accordance with IAS 32 Financial Instruments: Presentation and to apply the requirements in IFRS 9 and 
IAS 20 prospectively to government loans existing at the date of transition to IFRS accounting standards 
(except as permitted by IFRS 1.B11, which is discussed below). Therefore, a first-time adopter does not 
recognise the corresponding benefit of the government loan at a below-market rate of interest as a 
government grant. A first-time adopter that did not, under its previous GAAP, recognise and measure a 
government loan at a below-market rate of interest on a basis consistent with IFRS accounting standards 
requirements will instead use its previous GAAP carrying amount of the loan at the date of transition to 
IFRS accounting standards as the carrying amount of the loan in the opening IFRS statement of financial 
position and apply IFRS 9 to the measurement of such loans after the date of transition to IFRS accounting 
standards. [IFRS 1.B10]. 


International GAAP® 2025 
Chapter 06 
First-time adoption 
335 
Alternatively, IFRS 1.B11 allows an entity to apply the requirements in IFRS 9 and IAS 20 retrospectively to 
any government loan originated before the date of transition to IFRS accounting standards, provided the 
information needed to do so had been obtained when it initially accounted for the loan under previous 
GAAP. [IFRS 1.B11]. 
The requirements and guidance set out in IFRS 1.B10-B11 (which are discussed above) do not preclude an 
entity from using the exemptions in IFRS 1.D19-D19C to designate previously recognised financial 
instruments at fair value through profit or loss (see section 5.10 below). [IFRS 1.B12, IFRS 1.D19, IFRS 1.D19A, 
IFRS 1.D19B, IFRS 1.D19C]. 
Illustration 4-6 below demonstrates the accounting for a government loan with a below-market interest rate 
that is not restated from its previous GAAP amount. [IFRS 1.IG Example 12, IFRS 1.IG66]. 
Illustration 4-6: 
Government loan with below-market interest rate 
A government provides loans at a below-market rate of interest to fund the purchase of manufacturing equipment. 
Entity A’s date of transition to IFRS accounting standards is 1 January 20X2. 
In 20X0, Entity A received a loan of CU100,000 at a below-market rate of interest from the government. 
Under its previous GAAP, Entity A accounted for the loan as equity and the carrying amount was CU100,000 
at the date of transition. The amount repayable at 1 January 20X5 will be CU103,030. 
No other payment is required under the terms of the loan and there are no future performance conditions 
attached to it. The information needed to measure the fair value of the loan was not obtained at the time it 
was initially accounted for. 
The loan meets the definition of a financial liability in accordance with IAS 32. Entity A therefore reclassifies it 
from equity to liability. It also uses the previous GAAP carrying amount of the loan at the date of transition as 
the carrying amount of the loan in the opening IFRS statement of financial position. It calculates the effective 
interest rate starting at 1 January 20X2 as 1% based on the carrying amount of the loan at the date of 
transition and the amount repayable at maturity. The opening balance of CU100,000 will accrete to 
CU103,030 at 31 December 20X4 and interest of CU1,000, CU1,010 and CU1,020 will be charged to interest 
expense in each of the three years ended 31 December 20X2, 20X3 and 20X4. 
4.13 Insurance contracts 
An entity must apply the transition provisions in IFRS 17.C1-C24 and IFRS 17.C28 to contracts within the scope 
of IFRS 17 Insurance Contracts (see Chapter 51 at section 17). The references in those paragraphs in IFRS 17 to 
‘the transition date’ must be read as ‘the date of transition to IFRS accounting standards’. [IFRS 1.B13]. 
This is because the Board saw no reason to give different transition approaches to first-time adopters of 
IFRS accounting standards from other entities. This means that the Board has amended IFRS 1 to require 
use of the modified retrospective approach or the fair value approach in IFRS 17 when retrospective 
application of IFRS 17 is impracticable, as defined by IAS 8. However, the Board decided not to give any 
additional relief on the restatement of comparative amounts from that already in IFRS 1. [IFRS 17.BC407]. 
4.14 Deferred tax related to leases and decommissioning, restoration 
and similar liabilities 
IAS 12 Income Taxes provides an exception (known as the ‘initial recognition exception’) from recognising 
the deferred tax on temporary differences arising on initial recognition of an asset or liability in a 
transaction that (a) is not a business combination; (b) at the time of the transaction, affects neither 
accounting profit nor taxable profit (or tax loss); and (c) at the time of the transaction does not give rise to 
equal taxable and deductible temporary differences (see Chapter 29 at section 7.3). [IAS 12.15, IAS 12.24]. 
For example, at the commencement date of a lease, a lessee typically recognises a lease liability and the 
corresponding amount as part of the cost of a right-of-use asset. Depending on the applicable tax law, equal 
taxable and deductible temporary differences could arise on initial recognition of the asset and liability in 
such a transaction. If so, the initial recognition exception does not apply and any resulting deferred tax 
liability and asset is recognised. [IAS 12.22A]. It is important to note that IAS 12.15(b)(iii) and IAS 12.24(c) 
refer to ‘equal taxable and deductible temporary differences’, rather than ‘equal deferred tax assets and 
liabilities’. For example, the deferred tax assets and liabilities recognised could differ due to the application 
of different tax rates or because the deferred tax assets are not recoverable. [IAS 12.BC86, IAS 12.BC88]. 


International GAAP® 2025 
Chapter 06 
First-time adoption 
336 
Retrospective application would require entities to retrospectively assess whether each lease and 
decommissioning obligation gave rise to equal taxable and deductible temporary differences on initial 
recognition, which might have occurred many years before. Instead, first-time adopters are required to 
recognise deferred tax for all temporary differences associated with leases and decommissioning 
obligations existing at the date of transition to IFRS accounting standards. [IAS 12.BC95]. 
Therefore, notwithstanding the initial recognition exception in IAS 12.15 and IAS 12.24, at the date of 
transition to IFRS accounting standards, a first-time adopter must recognise a deferred tax asset (to the 
extent that it is probable that taxable profit will be available against which the deductible temporary 
difference can be utilised) and a deferred tax liability for all deductible and taxable temporary differences 
associated with: [IFRS 1.B14]. 
(a) Right-of-use assets and lease liabilities 
(b) Decommissioning, restoration and similar liabilities and the corresponding amounts recognised as part 
of the cost of the related asset 
However, the initial recognition exception still applies fully retrospectively to transactions other than leases 
and decommissioning obligations. 


International GAAP® 2025 
Chapter 06 
First-time adoption 
337 
5 
Optional exemptions from the requirements of certain IFRS 
accounting standards 
5.1 
Introduction 
As noted in section 3.5 above, IFRS 1 grants limited optional exemptions from the general requirement of 
full retrospective application of the standards effective at the end of an entity’s first IFRS reporting period. 
[IFRS 1.7, IFRS 1.12(b)]. Each of these exemptions, which are set out in Appendices C and D of IFRS 1, is 
explained in detail below. 
5.2 
Business combinations and acquisitions of associates and joint 
arrangements 
The business combinations exemption in IFRS 1 is probably the single most important exemption in the 
standard, as it permits a first-time adopter not to restate business combinations that occurred prior to its 
date of transition to IFRS accounting standards. The detailed guidance on the application of the business 
combinations exemption is contained in Appendix C to IFRS 1 and is organised in the sections as follows: 
[IFRS 1 Appendix C] 
(a) Scope of the business combinations exemption in IFRS 1 - and application of the exemption to 
acquisitions of associates and joint arrangements (see section 5.2.1 below) 
(b) Option to restate business combinations retrospectively (see section 5.2.2 below) 
(c) Classification of business combinations (see section 5.2.3 below) 
(d) Assets and liabilities to be recognised in the opening IFRS statement of financial position (see 
section 5.2.4 below) 
(e) Restatement of goodwill (see section 5.2.5 below) 
(f) Currency adjustments to goodwill (see section 5.2.6 below) 
(g) Previously unconsolidated subsidiaries (see section 5.2.7 below) 
(h) Previously consolidated entities that are not subsidiaries (see section 5.2.8 below) 
(i) Measurement of deferred taxes and non-controlling interests (see section 5.2.9 below) 
(j) Transition accounting for contingent consideration (see section 5.2.10 below) 
How we see it 
In our view, the business combinations exemption applies only to business combinations that occurred 
before the date of transition to IFRS accounting standards and only to business combinations as defined 
under IFRS 3 (see section 5.2.1 below). Therefore, it does not apply to a transaction that, for example, 
IFRS accounting standards treat as an acquisition of an asset (see section 5.2.1.A below). 
5.2.1 
Scope of the business combinations exemption in IFRS 1 
As noted above, the business combinations exemption applies only to business combinations as defined 
under IFRS 3. Therefore, a first-time adopter needs to consider whether past transactions would qualify as 
business combinations under IFRS 3 which defines a business combination as: 
“A transaction or other event in which an acquirer obtains control of one or more businesses. Transactions 
sometimes referred to as ‘true mergers’ or ‘mergers of equals’ are also business combinations as that term 
is used in this IFRS.” [IFRS 3 Appendix A]. 
The definition of a business is “An integrated set of activities and assets that is capable of being 
conducted and managed for the purpose of providing goods or services to customers, generating 
investment income (such as dividends or interest) or generating other income from ordinary activities.” 
[IFRS 3 Appendix A]. In addition, IFRS 3.3 states that “If the assets acquired are not a business, the 
reporting entity shall account for the transaction or other event as an asset acquisition.” (See 
section 5.2.1.A below). [IFRS 3.3]. Distinguishing a business combination from an asset acquisition is 
described in detail in Chapter 10 at section 3. 


International GAAP® 2025 
Chapter 06 
First-time adoption 
338 
In October 2012, the IASB issued Investment Entities: Amendments to IFRS 10, IFRS 12 and IAS 27 (see 
section 5.8.5 below). Amongst other changes, the amendment also stated that Appendix C of IFRS 1, which 
deals with the business combinations exemption, is applied only to business combinations within the scope 
of IFRS 3. [IFRS 1 Appendix C, IFRS 3.2A]. 
How we see it 
In our view, the purpose of this change was to preclude the application of Appendix C to the acquisition 
by an investment entity, as defined in IFRS 10, of an investment in subsidiary that is required to be 
measured at fair value through profit or loss. 
Therefore, we believe that the exemption is still available to transactions that meet the definition of a 
business combination under IFRS 3, even if the business combination is outside the scope of IFRS 3 (such 
as a combination of entities or businesses under common control). 
However, the business combinations exemption does not apply to transactions which are not ‘business 
combinations’ as defined in IFRS 3 (even if the transactions were accounted for as business combinations 
under previous GAAP). Examples of transactions that are not business combinations would include asset 
acquisitions (see section 5.2.1.A below) or continuations (such as where a new holding company is inserted 
above an existing group). A first-time adopter which had such transactions under previous GAAP 
retrospectively applies IFRS accounting standards applicable to those transactions. If transactions which 
are not business combinations as defined in IFRS 3 are combinations of entities under common control, 
there is no specific guidance under IFRS accounting standards on accounting for them. We discuss this 
issue in Chapter 11 at sections 1.2, 1.3, 2 and 4. 
5.2.1.A 
Asset acquisitions 
Because IFRS 3 provides such a specific definition of a business combination (as described in Chapter 10 at 
section 3), it is possible that under some national GAAPs, transactions that are not business combinations 
under IFRS 3, e.g., asset acquisitions, could have been accounted for as if they were business combinations. If 
so, a first-time adopter will need to restate such transactions, as discussed in Illustration 5-1 below. 
Illustration 5-1: 
Acquisition of assets 
Entity A acquired a company that held a single asset at the time of acquisition. That company had no 
employees and the asset itself was not in use at the date of acquisition. Entity A accounted for the transaction 
under its previous GAAP using the purchase method, which resulted in goodwill. Can Entity A apply the 
business combinations exemption to the acquisition of this asset? 
If Entity A concludes that the asset is not a business as defined in IFRS 3, it will not be able to apply the business 
combinations exemption. Instead, Entity A accounts for such transaction as an asset acquisition (see Chapter 10 
at section 2.2.2). Entity A could consider applying other applicable optional exemptions under IFRS 1. 
 
How we see it 
If, in Illustration 5-1 above, the entity had accounted for the transaction as an asset acquisition rather 
than a business combination under previous GAAP, it would need to determine whether the transaction 
meets the definition of a business combination in IFRS 3 in order to qualify for use of the exemption. 
5.2.1.B 
Associates and joint arrangements 
Extract from IFRS 1 
C5 
The exemption for past business combinations also applies to past acquisitions of investments 
in associates, interests in joint ventures and interests in joint operations in which the activity of 
the joint operation constitutes a business, as defined in IFRS 3. Furthermore, the date selected 
for paragraph C1 applies equally for all such acquisitions. 
IFRS 1 extends the exemption for past business combinations to past acquisitions of investments in 
associates, interests in joint ventures and interests in joint operations (in which the activity of the joint 
operation constitutes a business, as defined in IFRS 3). The date selected for the first restatement of 
business combinations will also be applied to the restatement of these other acquisitions. [IFRS 1.C5]. 


International GAAP® 2025 
Chapter 06 
First-time adoption 
339 
5.2.2 
Option to restate business combinations retrospectively 
Extract from IFRS 1 
C1 
A first-time adopter may elect not to apply IFRS 3 retrospectively to past business 
combinations (business combinations that occurred before the date of transition to IFRSs). 
However, if a first-time adopter restates any business combination to comply with IFRS 3, it 
shall restate all later business combinations and shall also apply IFRS 10 from that same date. 
For example, if a first-time adopter elects to restate a business combination that occurred on 
30 June 20X6, it shall restate all business combinations that occurred between 30 June 
20X6 and the date of transition to IFRSs, and it shall also apply IFRS 10 from 30 June 20X6. 
A first-time adopter must account for business combinations occurring after its date of transition to IFRS 
accounting standards under IFRS 3, i.e., any business combinations during the comparative periods 
presented need to be restated in accordance with IFRS 3. An entity accounts for a business combination 
under common control based on its accounting policy as determined under IFRS accounting standards (see 
Chapter 11 at section 3). This is because such transactions are outside the scope of IFRS 3. [IFRS 3.2(c)]. 
A first-time adopter is allowed to elect not to apply IFRS 3 retrospectively to business combinations 
occurring before the date of transition to IFRS accounting standards. However, if a first-time adopter does 
restate a business combination occurring prior to its date of transition to IFRS accounting standards to 
comply with IFRS 3 it must also restate any subsequent business combinations under IFRS 3 and apply 
IFRS 10 from that date onwards. [IFRS 1.C1]. In other words, as shown on the timeline at Figure 5-1 below, a 
first-time adopter is allowed to choose any date in the past from which it wants to account for all business 
combinations under IFRS 3 and would not restate business combinations that occurred prior to such date. It 
must be noted that a first-time adopter availing itself of this option is required to apply the version of 
IFRS 3 effective at the end of its first IFRS reporting period to any retrospectively restated business 
combinations. [IFRS 1.7]. 
Appendix C to IFRS 1 sets out the accounting consequences for business combinations not restated in 
accordance with IFRS 3 (see sections 5.2.3 to 5.2.10 below). [IFRS 1.C2, IFRS 1.C3, IFRS 1.C4]. However, the 
exemptions set out in IFRS 1.C2-C3 from applying IAS 21 to goodwill and fair value adjustments are also 
available for business combinations that are restated in accordance with IFRS 3. [IFRS 1.C2, IFRS 1.C3]. The 
various options for applying IAS 21 to goodwill and fair value adjustments are explained further in 
section 5.2.6 below. 
If any pre-transition business combinations are restated, the first-time adopter must also apply IFRS 10’s 
requirements on: the attribution of total comprehensive income to the owners of the parent and to the 
non-controlling interests; accounting for changes in the parent’s ownership interest in a subsidiary 
without loss of control; and accounting for a loss of control of a subsidiary (and the related IFRS 5 
requirements on classification of the disposal group as ‘held for sale’) from the date of the earliest 
business combination that is restated (see section 4.8 above). [IFRS 1.B7, IFRS 1.C1]. 
Figure 5-1: 
Application of the optional business combination exemption 
 
Even if a first-time adopter elects not to restate certain business combinations, it might still need to restate 
the carrying amounts of the acquired assets and assumed liabilities, as described in section 5.2.4 below. 
How we see it 
Although there is no restriction that prevents retrospective application by a first-time adopter of IFRS 3 
to all past business combinations, in our view, a first-time adopter does not restate business 
combinations under IFRS 3 that occurred before the date of transition to IFRS accounting standards 
when this would require use of hindsight.

entity that chose to restate certain business combinations that occurred prior to its date of transition to 
IFRS accounting standards.

5.2.3 
Classification of business combinations 
IFRS 3 mandates a business combination to be accounted for either as an acquisition or reverse acquisition. 
[IFRS 3.4, IFRS 3.5(a), IFRS 3.6, IFRS 3.B19]. An entity’s previous GAAP could be based on a different definition of, for 
example, a business combination, an acquisition, a uniting of interests (or merger) or a reverse acquisition. 
An important benefit of the exemption not to apply IFRS 3 retrospectively to pre-transition business 
combinations is that a first-time adopter keeps the same classification (as in its previous GAAP financial 
statements) for business combinations not restated under IFRS 3, [IFRS 1.C4(a)]. For example, a business 
combination that was accounted for as a merger (or uniting of interests) using the pooling of interests method 
under an entity’s previous GAAP is not reclassified and accounted for under the acquisition method. Similarly, 
a business combination that was accounted for as an acquisition by the legal acquirer under the entity’s 
previous GAAP continues to be classified as such even if it would be accounted for as a reverse acquisition by 
the acquiree under IFRS 3. 
However, an entity could still elect to restate pre-transition business combinations retrospectively in 
accordance with IFRS 3, if it so wishes without using hindsight (see section 5.2.2 above). [IFRS 1.C1]. 


International GAAP® 2025 
Chapter 06 
First-time adoption 
342 
5.2.3.A 
Business combinations under common control 
As discussed in section 5.2.1 above, we believe that Appendix C of IFRS 1 applies to a business combination 
under common control, provided it meets the definition of a business combination under IFRS 3, even 
though it is outside of the scope of IFRS 3. [IFRS 1 Appendix C, IFRS 3.B1]. Therefore, a first-time adopter could 
use the exemption to not restate a business combination under common control occurring before the date 
of transition to IFRS accounting standards retrospectively. If a first-time adopter does not apply the 
exemption, it accounts for a business combination under common control based on its IFRS accounting 
policy, as discussed in Chapter 11 at section 3. 
Illustration 5-2: 
Business combination under common control 
Entity B, which is a first-time adopter under IFRS accounting standards, acquired Entity C in a business 
combination under common control that it accounted for as a pooling of interests under its previous GAAP 
prior to the date of transition to IFRS accounting standards. Entity B has the option to elect to use the business 
combinations exemption. 
Case 1: Entity B uses the business combinations exemption 
Entity B is required to apply the same classification as in its previous GAAP financial statements in accordance 
with IFRS 1.C4(a). [IFRS 1.C4(a)]. Entity B must also follow all of the other provisions of IFRS 1.C4. [IFRS 1.C4]. 
Case 2: Entity B does not use the business combinations exemption 
Entity B accounts for the business combination applying the accounting policy it has chosen for such business 
combinations under IFRS accounting standards. If Entity B applies the acquisition method to business 
combinations involving entities under common control (where the transaction has substance - see Chapter 11 
at section 3.1), the business combination would be retrospectively restated in accordance with IFRS 3. 
Conversely, if the Entity B’s accounting policy for such business combinations is to use the pooling of interests 
method, the business combination would be retrospectively restated if there were differences between the 
accounting treatment under previous GAAP compared to the accounting policy adopted under IFRS accounting 
standards in applying the pooling of interests method.

Assets and liabilities to be recognised in the opening IFRS statement of 
financial position

If a first-time adopter does not apply IFRS 3 retrospectively to a past business combination, this 
has the following consequences for that business combination: 
(a)

The first-time adopter shall recognise all its assets and liabilities at the date of transition 
to IFRSs that were acquired or assumed in a past business combination, other than: 
(i) 
some financial assets and financial liabilities derecognised in accordance with 
previous GAAP (see paragraph B2); and 
(ii) 
assets, including goodwill, and liabilities that were not recognised in the acquirer’s 
consolidated statement of financial position in accordance with previous GAAP and 
also would not qualify for recognition in accordance with IFRSs in the separate 
statement of financial position of the acquiree (see (f)-(i) below). 
The first-time adopter shall recognise any resulting change by adjusting retained earnings 
(or, if appropriate, another category of equity), unless the change results from the 
recognition of an intangible asset that was previously subsumed within goodwill (see (g)(i)

(c) 
… 
In its opening IFRS statement of financial position, a first-time adopter is required to recognise all assets 
acquired and liabilities assumed in a past business combination, with the exception of: [IFRS 1.C4(b)] 
• 
Certain financial assets and liabilities that were derecognised in accordance with previous GAAP and 
that fall under the derecognition exception (see section 4.3 above) [IFRS 1.B2] 
• 
Assets (including goodwill) and liabilities that were not recognised in the acquirer’s consolidated 
statement of financial position under its previous GAAP and that also would not qualify for recognition 
under IFRS accounting standards in the separate statement of financial position of the acquiree (see 
section 5.2.4.B and Illustration 5-4 below) [IFRS 1.C4(f), IFRS 1.C4(g), IFRS 1.C4(h), IFRS 1.C4(i)]

an asset or liability under IFRS accounting standards (see section 5.2.4.A and Illustration 5-3 below).

5.2.4.A 
Assets and liabilities to be excluded 
Extract from IFRS 1 
C4 
If a first-time adopter does not apply IFRS 3 retrospectively to a past business combination, this 
has the following consequences for that business combination: 
(a) 
… 
(c) 
The first-time adopter shall exclude from its opening IFRS statement of financial position 
any item recognised in accordance with previous GAAP that does not qualify for 
recognition as an asset or liability under IFRSs. The first-time adopter shall account for 
the resulting change as follows: 
(i) 
the first-time adopter may have classified a past business combination as an 
acquisition and recognised as an intangible asset an item that does not qualify for 
recognition as an asset in accordance with IAS 38 Intangible Assets. It shall 
reclassify that item (and, if any, the related deferred tax and non-controlling 
interests) as part of goodwill (unless it deducted goodwill directly from equity in 
accordance with previous GAAP, see (g)(i) and (i) below). 
(ii) 
the first-time adopter shall recognise all other resulting changes in retained 
earnings.2 
(d) 
… 
2 
Such changes include reclassifications from or to intangible assets if goodwill was not recognised in accordance with previous 
GAAP as an asset. This arises if, in accordance with previous GAAP, the entity (a) deducted goodwill directly from equity or (b) 
did not treat the business combination as an acquisition. 
If the first-time adopter recognised under its previous GAAP items that do not qualify for recognition under 
IFRS accounting standards, these must be excluded from the opening IFRS statement of financial position. 
An intangible asset, acquired as part of a business combination, that does not qualify for recognition as an 
asset under IAS 38 is required to be derecognised along with the related deferred tax and non-controlling 
interests (if any), with an offsetting change to goodwill, unless the entity previously deducted goodwill 
directly from equity under its previous GAAP (see section 5.2.5 below). 
All other changes resulting from derecognition of such assets and liabilities are accounted for as 
adjustments of retained earnings or another category of equity, if appropriate. [IFRS 1.C4(c)]. For example: 
• 
Any restructuring provisions recognised under previous GAAP and which remain at the date of 
transition to IFRS accounting standards will need to be assessed against the IFRS recognition criteria. If 
the criteria are not met, then the provisions must be reversed against retained earnings. 
• 
If an entity has deferred transaction costs (for which the services were received) relating to a business 
combination that has not been finalised under previous GAAP at the date of transition to IFRS 
accounting standards, these deferred transaction costs would need to be recognised in retained 
earnings as they do not qualify for deferral under IFRS accounting standards. 
• 
Assets and liabilities of a structured entity required to be consolidated under previous GAAP but that 
does not qualify for consolidation under IFRS 10 would be deconsolidated. 
Illustration 5-3 below, which is based on one within the implementation guidance in IFRS 1, illustrates the 
exclusion of a restructuring provision that did not comply with IFRS accounting standards from the opening 
statement of financial position. [IFRS 1.IG Example 3]. 
Illustration 5-3: 
Restructuring provision 
Background 
Entity D’s first IFRS financial statements are for a period that ends on 31 December 20X3 and include 
comparative information for 20X2 only. It chooses not to restate previous business combinations under 
IFRS 3. On 1 July 20X1, Entity D acquired 100% of Entity E. Under its previous GAAP, Entity D recognised an 
(undiscounted) restructuring provision of CU100 that would not have qualified as an identifiable liability under 
IFRS 3. The recognition of this restructuring provision increased goodwill by CU100. At 1 January 20X2 (date 
of transition to IFRS accounting standards), Entity D: 
(a) Had paid restructuring costs of CU60 
(b) Estimated that it would pay further costs of CU40 in 20X2 and that the effects of discounting were 
immaterial. At 1 January 20X2, those further costs did not qualify for recognition as a provision under 
IAS 37. 
This Illustration ignores any income tax effects of the adjustments. 


International GAAP® 2025 
Chapter 06 
First-time adoption 
344 
Application of requirements 
In its opening IFRS statement of financial position, Entity D: 
(a) Does not recognise a restructuring provision in respect of the outstanding CU40 provision under previous 
GAAP. The adjustment is recognised in retained earnings. [IFRS 1.C4(c)]. 
(b) Does not adjust the amount assigned to goodwill. However, Entity D tests the goodwill for impairment 
under IAS 36 and recognises any resulting impairment loss as an adjustment to retained earnings. 
[IFRS 1.C4(g)]. 
5.2.4.B 
Recognition of assets and liabilities 
Extract from IFRS 1 
C4 
If a first-time adopter does not apply IFRS 3 retrospectively to a past business combination, this 
has the following consequences for that business combination: 
(a) 
… 
(f) 
If an asset acquired, or liability assumed, in a past business combination was not 
recognised in accordance with previous GAAP, it does not have a deemed cost of zero in 
the opening IFRS statement of financial position. Instead, the acquirer shall recognise and 
measure it in its consolidated statement of financial position on the basis that IFRSs 
would require in the statement of financial position of the acquiree. … 
(g) 
… 
For example, if the acquirer had not recognised an assumed provision under its previous GAAP that still exists 
at the date of transition to IFRS accounting standards, the acquirer recognises that provision at that date 
unless IAS 37 would prohibit its recognition in the financial statements of the acquiree. [IFRS 1.C4(f), IAS 37.27]. 
The change resulting from the recognition of such assets and liabilities is accounted for as an adjustment of 
retained earnings or another category of equity, if appropriate. However, if the change results from the 
recognition of an intangible asset that was previously subsumed within goodwill, it is accounted for as an 
adjustment of that goodwill (and of the related deferred tax and non-controlling interests, if any) (see 
section 5.2.5 below). [IFRS 1.C4(b), IFRS 1.C4(g)(i), IFRS 1.C4(k)]. 
How we see it 
Intangible assets acquired as part of a business combination that were not recognised under a first-time 
adopter’s previous GAAP will rarely be recognised in the opening IFRS statement of financial position 
because either: (1) they cannot be capitalised in the acquiree’s own statement of financial position under 
IAS 38; or (2) capitalisation would require the use of hindsight which is not permitted under IAS 38 (see 
section 7.14 below). 
Illustration 5-4 below discusses some examples in which certain assets were not recognised under previous GAAP. 
Illustration 5-4: 
Items not recognised under previous GAAP 
Entity F acquired Entity G but did not recognise Entity G’s right-of-use assets and internally generated customer 
lists under its previous GAAP. 
Upon first-time adoption of IFRS accounting standards, Entity F recognises the right-of-use assets in its opening 
IFRS statement of financial position using the amounts that Entity G would recognise in its separate statement of 
financial position. The resulting adjustment to the net assets at the date of transition is reflected in retained 
earnings; goodwill is not restated to reflect the net assets that would have been recognised at the date of 
acquisition (see section 5.2.5 below). However, Entity F does not recognise the customer lists in its opening 
IFRS statement of financial position, because Entity G is not permitted to capitalise internally generated customer 
lists under IAS 38. Any value that might have been attributable to the customer lists would remain subsumed in 
goodwill in Entity F’s opening IFRS statement of financial position. [IFRS 1.C4(b)(ii), IFRS 1.C4(f)]. 
Entity H acquired Entity I but did not recognise Entity I’s brand name as a separate intangible asset under its 
previous GAAP. 
Upon first-time adoption of IFRS accounting standards, Entity H will not recognise Entity I’s brand name in its 
opening IFRS statement of financial position because Entity I would not have been permitted under IAS 38 to 
recognise it as an asset in its own separate statement of financial position. Again, any value that might have been 
attributable to the brand name would remain subsumed in goodwill in Entity H’s opening IFRS statement of 
financial position. [IFRS 1.C4(b)(ii), IFRS 1.C4(f)].

Illustration 5-5: 
Lease in which the acquiree was a lessee not capitalised in accordance with

Entity J’s date of transition to IFRS accounting standards is 1 January 20X2. Entity J acquired Entity K

financial statements in accordance with IFRS accounting standards, it would recognise lease liabilities of 
CU300,000 and right-of-use assets of CU250,000 at 1 January 20X2. This Illustration ignores any 
income tax effects of the adjustments. 
Entity J has elected not to apply the transition reliefs in IFRS 1.D9 and IFRS 1.D9B to D9E. [IFRS 1.D9, 
IFRS 1.D9B, IFRS 1.D9C, IFRS 1.D9D, IFRS 1.D9E]. In its consolidated opening IFRS statement of financial position,
