CU50,000 to retained earnings. [IFRS 1.C4(b)(ii), IFRS 1.C4(f)]. 
5.2.4.C 
Previous GAAP carrying amount as deemed cost 
Extract from IFRS 1 
C4 
If a first-time adopter does not apply IFRS 3 retrospectively to a past business combination, this 
has the following consequences for that business combination: 
(a) 
… 
(e) 
Immediately after the business combination, the carrying amount in accordance with 
previous GAAP of assets acquired and liabilities assumed in that business combination 
shall be their deemed cost in accordance with IFRSs at that date. If IFRSs require a cost-
based measurement of those assets and liabilities at a later date, that deemed cost shall 
be the basis for cost-based depreciation or amortisation from the date of the business 
combination. 
(f) 
… 
IFRS 1 does not specifically define ‘immediately after the business combination’, but it is commonly 
understood that this takes account of the final determination of the purchase price allocation and final 
completion of purchase accounting. In other words, a first-time adopter would not use the provisionally 
determined fair values of assets acquired and liabilities assumed in applying the business combinations 
exemption, as explained in Illustration 5-6 below. [IFRS 1.C4(e)]. 
Illustration 5-6: 
Provisionally determined fair values 
Entity L acquired Entity M in August 20X1 and made a provisional assessment of Entity M’s identifiable net assets 
in its 31 December 20X1 consolidated financial statements under its previous GAAP. In its 31 December 20X2 
consolidated financial statements - its last financial statements under previous GAAP - Entity L completed the 
initial accounting for the business combination and adjusted the provisional values of the identifiable net assets 
and the corresponding goodwill. Upon first-time adoption of IFRS accounting standards, Entity L elects not to 
restate past business combinations in accordance with IFRS 3. 
In preparing its opening IFRS statement of financial position as of 1 January 20X2, Entity L uses the adjusted 
carrying amounts of the identifiable net assets as determined in its 20X2 financial statements as the deemed 
cost at the date of the business combination rather than the provisional carrying amounts of the identifiable net 
assets and goodwill shown in the 20X1 financial statements. 
IFRS 1 is silent as to whether the relevant carrying amounts of the identifiable net assets and goodwill are 
those that appeared in the financial statements drawn up immediately before the date of transition to IFRS 
accounting standards or any restated balance appearing in a later set of previous GAAP accounts. 


International GAAP® 2025 
Chapter 06 
First-time adoption 
346 
How we see it 
Since the adjustments that were made to provisional fair values of the identifiable net assets and goodwill 
under previous GAAP effectively resulted in a restatement of the balances at the date of transition to 
IFRS accounting standards in a manner that is consistent with the approach permitted by IFRS 
accounting standards, it is, in our view, appropriate to reflect those adjustments in the opening 
IFRS statement of financial position. Moreover, this remains the case even if such adjustments to 
goodwill and the identifiable net assets at the acquisition date would have been made prospectively under 
previous GAAP rather than restating prior periods as would be required under IFRS 3. 
Since the adjustments are effectively made as at the date of transition to IFRS accounting standards, it is 
also appropriate to use the window period permitted by previous GAAP provided that this does not 
extend into the first IFRS reporting period. This is because any restatements in that period can only be 
made in accordance with IFRS 3. In effect, the phrase ‘immediately after the business combination’ in 
IFRS 1.C4(e) is interpreted as including a window period allowed by the previous GAAP that ends at the 
earlier of the end of the window period and the beginning of the first IFRS reporting period. 
Although the use of cost-based measurements under previous GAAP might be considered inconsistent with 
the requirements of IFRS accounting standards for assets and liabilities that were not acquired in a business 
combination, the IASB did not identify any situations in a business combination in which it would not be 
acceptable to bring forward cost-based measurements made under previous GAAP. [IFRS 1.BC36]. For 
example, assume an entity that adopts IFRS accounting standards with a date of transition to IFRS 
accounting standards of 1 January 20X2 and, as required, applies IFRS 3 to business combinations 
occurring on or after 1 January 20X2. Under the entity’s previous GAAP, it acquired a business in 20X0 
and the purchase accounting resulted in negative goodwill. At that time, the negative goodwill was 
eliminated by reducing the amounts assigned to long-lived assets (PP&E and intangible assets) on a pro rata 
basis. In this situation, the negative goodwill adjustment to PP&E and intangible assets is ‘grandfathered’ 
and is not adjusted in the opening IFRS statement of financial position. The negative goodwill adjustment to 
PP&E and intangible assets was part of the original purchase accounting and is not a subsequent 
measurement difference between completing the purchase price allocation and 1 January 20X2 and 
therefore the adjustment forms part of the deemed cost of PP&E and intangible assets. 
By contrast, if under previous GAAP, the entity had recognised amortisation of intangible assets from the 
date of acquisition in a way that was not in compliance with IAS 38, it is not ‘grandfathered’ under the 
business combination exemption. The amortisation therefore is amended to the amount that complies with 
IFRS accounting standards on transition, as explained in Illustration 5-7 below (note that the adjusted 
carrying amount is also required to be tested for impairment if there are impairment indicators pursuant to 
the requirements of IAS 36 at the date of transition; see section 7.12 below). [IFRS 1.C4(c), IFRS 1.BC37]. 
Illustration 5-7: 
Items measured on a cost basis 
Entity N applies the business combination exemption under IFRS 1. In a business combination Entity N 
acquired property, plant and equipment, inventory and accounts receivable. Under its previous GAAP, Entity N 
initially measured these assets at cost (i.e., their fair values at the date originally acquired). 
Upon adoption of IFRS accounting standards, Entity N determines that its accounting policy for these assets 
under its previous GAAP complied with the requirements of IFRS accounting standards. Therefore, property, 
plant and equipment, inventory and accounts receivable are not adjusted but recognised in the opening 
IFRS statement of financial position at their carrying amounts under the previous GAAP. 
In addition to the above, Entity N recognised a brand name as an intangible asset acquired in the business 
combination under its previous GAAP. Entity N initially measured the intangible asset at its cost of CU100 and 
amortised it over a period of 10 years (being the maximum period of amortisation under previous GAAP). The 
intangible asset had a carrying amount of CU80 at the date of transition to IFRS accounting standards under 
its previous GAAP. The intangible asset is not amortised in accordance with IAS 38, as it was concluded that it 
has an indefinite useful life. There are no indicators of impairment and the intangible asset’s recoverable 
amount exceeds CU100. 
Entity N recognises the intangible asset at its original cost under its previous GAAP of CU100 in the opening 
IFRS statement of financial position (reflecting the fact that under IAS 38, the intangible asset would not have 
been amortised since it had an indefinite life). The subsequent measurement not in accordance with IFRS 
accounting standards is not reflected. 


International GAAP® 2025 
Chapter 06 
First-time adoption 
347 
5.2.4.D 
In-process research and development 
IFRS 1 makes it clear that in-process research and development (IPR&D) that was included within goodwill 
under an entity’s previous GAAP is not recognised separately upon transition to IFRS accounting standards 
unless it qualifies for recognition under IAS 38 in the statement of financial position of the acquiree. 
[IFRS 1.C4(h)(i)]. However, IFRS 1 is silent on the treatment of IPR&D that was identified separately by an 
entity under the business combinations accounting standard of its previous GAAP, but which was 
immediately written off to profit or loss. 
There are two possible scenarios as follows: 
(a) If previous GAAP required IPR&D to be written off as an integral part of the business combination 
accounting under that GAAP then the carrying amount of IPR&D ‘immediately after the business 
combination’ would be zero. 
(b) If that write-off was not an integral part of the business combination accounting (e.g., the previous 
GAAP merely required accelerated amortisation) then the carrying amount ‘immediately after the 
business combination’ would be the amount allocated to IPR&D by the business combinations standard 
under previous GAAP. 
How we see it 
While we understand that there could be different views, it is our view that IFRS 1 does not allow 
reinstatement of the amount of IPR&D previously written off, where previous GAAP required IPR&D to be 
written off as an integral part of the business combination accounting (scenario (a) above). 
On the other hand, where the write-off of IPR&D under previous GAAP was not an integral part of the 
business combination (scenario (b) above), we believe that reinstatement of the amount of IPR&D that 
was written off is appropriate. 
The above distinction could be largely irrelevant if the business combination took place several years before 
the transition to IFRS accounting standards because, in practice, the IPR&D could have been amortised fully 
or impaired before the date of transition. 
5.2.4.E 
Subsequent measurement under IFRS accounting standards not based on cost 
IFRS accounting standards require subsequent measurement of some assets and liabilities on a basis other 
than original cost, such as fair value for certain financial instruments or on specific measurement bases for 
share-based payments (IFRS 2) and employee benefits (IAS 19). Even if a first-time adopter does not apply 
IFRS 3 retrospectively to a business combination, such assets and liabilities must be measured on that 
other basis in its opening IFRS statement of financial position. Any change in the carrying amount of those 
assets and liabilities is accounted for as an adjustment of retained earnings, or other appropriate category 
of equity, rather than as an adjustment of goodwill. [IFRS 1.C4(d)]. Illustration 5-8 below discusses the 
treatment of assets measured at fair value under IFRS accounting standards. 
Illustration 5-8: 
Items not measured at original cost 
Entity O acquired in a business combination a trading portfolio of equity securities and a number of investment 
properties. Under its previous GAAP, Entity O initially measured these assets at historical cost (i.e., at their 
fair values at the date of original acquisition). 
Upon adoption of IFRS accounting standards, Entity O measures the trading portfolio of equity securities and 
the investment properties at fair value in its opening IFRS statement of financial position. The resulting 
adjustment to these assets at the date of transition is reflected in retained earnings. 


International GAAP® 2025 
Chapter 06 
First-time adoption 
348 
5.2.4.F 
Illustration of recognition and measurement requirements 
Illustration 5-9 below, which is based on one within the implementation guidance in IFRS 1, demonstrates 
many of the requirements discussed above. [IFRS 1.IG Example 2]. 
Illustration 5-9: 
Business combination example 
Background 
Entity P’s first IFRS financial statements are for a reporting period that ends on 31 December 20X3 and 
include comparative information for 20X2 only. On 1 July 20X0, Entity P acquired 100% of Entity Q. Under its 
previous GAAP, Entity P: 
(a) Classified the business combination as an acquisition by Entity P 
(b) Measured the assets acquired and liabilities assumed at the following amounts under previous GAAP at 
1 January 20X2 (date of transition to IFRS accounting standards) 
(i) 
Identifiable assets less liabilities for which IFRS accounting standards require cost-based 
measurement at a date after the business combination: CU200 (with a tax base of CU150 and an 
applicable tax rate of 30%) 
(ii) Pension liability (for which the present value of the defined benefit obligation measured under IAS 19 
is CU130 and the fair value of plan assets is CU100): CUnil (because Entity P used a pay-as-you-go 
cash method of accounting for pensions under its previous GAAP). The tax base of the pension 
liability is also CUnil 
(iii) Goodwill: CU180 
(c) Did not, at the date of acquisition, recognise deferred tax arising from temporary differences associated 
with the identifiable assets acquired and liabilities assumed 
Application of requirements 
In its opening (consolidated) IFRS statement of financial position, Entity P decides to apply the business 
combination exemption and as a result: 
(i) 
Classifies the business combination as an acquisition by Entity P even if the business combination would 
have qualified under IFRS 3 as a reverse acquisition by Entity Q. [IFRS 1.C4(a)]. 
(ii) Does not adjust the accumulated amortisation of goodwill. Entity P tests the goodwill for impairment 
under IAS 36 and recognises any resulting impairment loss, based on conditions that existed at the date 
of transition to IFRS accounting standards. If no impairment exists, the carrying amount of the goodwill 
remains at CU180. [IFRS 1.C4(g)]. 
(iii) For those net identifiable assets acquired for which IFRS accounting standards require cost-based 
measurement at a date after the business combination, treats their carrying amount under previous GAAP 
immediately after the business combination as their deemed cost at that date. [IFRS 1.C4(e)]. 
(iv) Does not restate the accumulated depreciation and amortisation of the net identifiable assets in (iii) 
above, unless the depreciation methods and rates under previous GAAP result in amounts that differ 
materially from those required under IFRS accounting standards (for example, if they were adopted solely 
for tax purposes and do not reflect a reasonable estimate of the asset’s useful life under IFRS accounting 
standards). If no such restatement is made, the carrying amount of those assets in the opening 
IFRS statement of financial position equals their carrying amount under previous GAAP at the date of 
transition to IFRS accounting standards (CU200). [IFRS 1.IG7]. 
(v) If there is any indication that identifiable assets are impaired, tests those assets for impairment, under 
IAS 36, based on conditions that existed at the date of transition to IFRS accounting standards (see 
section 7.12 below). 
(vi) Recognises the pension liability, and measures it, at the present value of the defined benefit obligation 
(CU130) less the fair value of the plan assets (CU100), giving a carrying amount of CU30, with a 
corresponding debit of CU30 to retained earnings. [IFRS 1.C4(d)]. 
(vii) Recognises a net deferred tax liability of CU6 (CU20 at 30%) arising from: 
(a) The taxable temporary difference of CU50 (CU200 less CU150) associated with the identifiable 
assets acquired and non-pension liabilities assumed 
Less 
(b) The deductible temporary difference of CU30 (CU30 less CUnil) associated with the pension liability 
Entity P recognises the resulting increase in the deferred tax liability as a deduction from retained 
earnings. [IFRS 1.C4(b), IFRS 1.C4(k)]. If a taxable temporary difference arises from the initial recognition of 
the goodwill, Entity P does not recognise the resulting deferred tax liability. [IAS 12.15(a)]. 


International GAAP® 2025 
Chapter 06 
First-time adoption 
349 
5.2.5 
Restatement of goodwill 
Under the business combinations exemption, a first-time adopter takes the carrying amount of goodwill 
under its previous GAAP at the date of transition to IFRS accounting standards as a starting point and only 
adjusts it as follows: [IFRS 1.C4(g)] 
(a) Goodwill is increased by the carrying amount of any intangible asset acquired in a business combination 
under its previous GAAP (less any related deferred tax and non-controlling interests) that does not 
meet the recognition criteria under IFRS accounting standards. The first-time adopter accounts for the 
change in classification prospectively and does not, for example, reverse the cumulative amortisation 
on the item that it recognised as an intangible asset under its previous GAAP (see section 5.2.4.A 
above). [IFRS 1.C4(c)(i), IFRS 1.C4(k)]. 
(b) Goodwill is decreased if a first-time adopter is required to recognise an intangible asset under IFRS 
accounting standards that was subsumed in goodwill under its previous GAAP. It adjusts deferred tax and 
non-controlling interests accordingly (see section 5.2.4.B above). [IFRS 1.C4(b)(ii), IFRS 1.C4(f), IFRS 1.C4(k)]. 
(c) Goodwill must be tested for impairment at the date of transition to IFRS accounting standards in 
accordance with IAS 36 regardless of whether there is any indication that the goodwill could be 
impaired (see Chapter 21 at section 2); any resulting impairment loss is recognised in retained earnings 
unless IAS 36 requires it to be recognised in a revaluation surplus (see Chapter 21 at section 11). The 
impairment test must be based on conditions at the date of transition to IFRS accounting standards. 
Application of the above guidance can sometimes be more complicated than expected as is shown in 
Illustration 5-10 below. 
Illustration 5-10: 
Recognition and derecognition of acquired intangible assets 
Before its date of transition to IFRS accounting standards, Entity R acquired an online retailer, Entity S. Under 
its previous GAAP, Entity R recognised an intangible asset of CU1,200 related to ‘deferred marketing costs’, 
which does not meet the recognition criteria under IFRS accounting standards. Entity R also acquired customer 
relationships with a fair value of CU900 that do meet the recognition criteria under IFRS 3, but which it did not 
recognise as an intangible asset under its previous GAAP. 
Upon adoption of IFRS accounting standards, Entity R is required to derecognise the ‘deferred marketing 
costs’ intangible asset and increase the carrying amount of goodwill for a corresponding amount. It also 
adjusts deferred tax and non-controlling interests accordingly (with a corresponding adjustment to goodwill). 
[IFRS 1.C4(c)(i), IFRS 1.C4(g)(i), IFRS 1.C4(k)]. Nevertheless, the customer relationship intangible asset that is 
subsumed in goodwill cannot be recognised as its carrying amount in the statement of financial position of the 
acquiree, Entity S, would have been nil under IFRS accounting standards and therefore remains subsumed 
within goodwill. [IFRS 1.C4(b)(ii), IFRS 1.C4(g)(i), IFRS 1.C4(f)]. 
In economic terms it might be contended that the ‘deferred marketing costs’ intangible asset in the 
Illustration above comprises the value that would have been attributable under IFRS accounting standards 
to the acquired customer relationships. However, unless Entity R concluded that not recognising the 
customer relationship intangible asset was an error under its previous GAAP, it would not be able to 
recognise the customer relationship intangible asset upon adoption of IFRS accounting standards. 
Under IFRS 1, assets acquired and liabilities assumed in a business combination prior to the date of 
transition to IFRS accounting standards are not necessarily valued on a basis that is consistent with IFRS 
accounting standards. This can lead to ‘double counting’ in the carrying amount of assets and goodwill as is 
shown in Illustration 5-11 below. 


International GAAP® 2025 
Chapter 06 
First-time adoption 
350 
Illustration 5-11: 
Impairment testing of goodwill on first-time adoption 
Entity T acquired a business before its date of transition to IFRS accounting standards. The cost of acquisition 
was CU530 and Entity T allocated the purchase price as follows: 
  
CU  
Properties, at carry-over cost 
450  
Liabilities, at amortised cost 
(180) 
Goodwill 
260  
Purchase price 
530  
The goodwill under Entity T’s previous GAAP relates entirely to the properties that had a fair value at the date 
of acquisition that was significantly in excess of their value on a carry-over cost basis. In Entity T’s opening 
IFRS statement of financial position the same assets, liabilities and goodwill are valued as follows: 
  
CU  
Properties, at fair value 
750  
Liabilities, at amortised cost 
(180) 
Provisional IFRS goodwill  
 (before impairment test) 
260  
Total carrying amount 
830  
Entity T used the option to measure the properties at fair value at its date of transition in its opening 
IFRS statement of financial position. [IFRS 1.D5]. However, IFRS 1 does not permit goodwill to be adjusted to 
reflect the extent to which the increase in fair value relates to other assets recognised at the time of the 
acquisition. The total carrying amount of the acquired net assets including goodwill of CU830 might now 
exceed the recoverable amount. When Entity T tests the ‘provisional IFRS goodwill’ for impairment on first-
time adoption of IFRS accounting standards, the recoverable amount of the business is determined to be 
CU620. Accordingly, it will have to recognise an impairment of goodwill of CU210 and disclose this impairment 
under IFRS 1. [IFRS 1.C4(g)(ii), IFRS 1.24(c)]. 
In some cases, the write-off will completely eliminate the goodwill and thereby any ‘double counting’. However, 
in this particular case, the remaining goodwill of CU50 in truth represents goodwill that was internally 
generated between the date of acquisition and the date of transition to IFRS accounting standards. 
The IASB accepted that IFRS 1 does not prevent the implicit recognition of internally generated goodwill 
that arose after the date of the business combination. It concluded that attempts to exclude such internally 
generated goodwill would be costly and lead to arbitrary results. [IFRS 1.BC39]. 
The business combinations exemption also applies to associates, interests in joint ventures and interests in 
joint operations (in which the activity of the joint operation constitutes a business, as defined in IFRS 3) 
(see section 5.2.1.B above). [IFRS 1.C5]. 
However, IAS 28 Investments in Associates and Joint Ventures states the goodwill that forms part of the 
carrying amount of the net investment in an associate or interest in a joint venture is not tested for 
impairment separately by applying the requirements for impairment testing goodwill in IAS 36. Instead, the 
entire carrying amount of the investment is tested for impairment in accordance with IAS 36 as a single 
asset. [IAS 28.42]. 
How we see it 
In our view, investments in associates and joint arrangements are tested for impairment as at the date of 
transition to IFRS accounting standards if they include an element of goodwill. However, the goodwill 
embedded in the amount of an investment in an associate or an interest in a joint venture will not be 
subject to a separate impairment test. Rather the entire carrying amount of the investment is tested for 
impairment following the requirements of IAS 36. 
In some cases, there might have been an impairment of an investment in an associate or joint venture 
accounted using the equity method under previous GAAP. 


International GAAP® 2025 
Chapter 06 
First-time adoption 
351 
How we see it 
In testing the investment in an associate or joint venture for impairment as at the date of transition to 
IFRS accounting standards, in our view, an investor does not reverse a previous GAAP impairment that 
was recognised separately on the notional goodwill element embedded in the investment. However, if the 
previous GAAP impairment had been recognised as a reduction of the entire investment (without 
attribution to any particular embedded goodwill element), the first-time adopter is able to reverse such 
impairment if it is assessed to no longer be necessary. 
This is discussed in the two scenarios set out in Illustration 5-12 below: 
Illustration 5-12: 
Previous GAAP impairment of goodwill embedded in equity method investment 
in associate or joint venture 
Scenario 1 - Impairment was recognised on the notional goodwill element embedded in the investment under 
previous GAAP 
On 1 January 20X0, Entity U acquired an investment in Entity V, which it accounts for using the equity method 
(the investment would qualify as an associate under IFRS accounting standards). The cost of investment was 
CU1,500 compared to V’s identifiable net assets of CU500; therefore, notional goodwill of CU1,000 was 
included in the carrying value of the investment at that time. The previous GAAP required the entity to: 
• 
Amortise the notional goodwill of the associate on a straight-line basis over 10 years. 
• 
Test the investment accounted using the equity method for impairment at the investment level. 
• 
Allocate and recognise the impairment loss (if any) against notional goodwill. 
• 
Not reverse goodwill impairments (including those on notional goodwill). Consequently, goodwill 
impairments recognised under previous GAAP affected future amortisation of goodwill. 
Therefore, under its previous GAAP, Entity U tested its investment in Entity V for impairment and recognised 
an impairment loss of CU400 in the year ended 31 December 20X1 after amortisation of CU200 was 
recognised. This reduced the notional goodwill to CU400, which Entity U amortises over the remaining 8 years 
(CU50 annually). By its date of transition to IFRS accounting standards, 1 January 20X4, the notional goodwill 
had been amortised by a further 2 years × CU50 (CU100), reducing the notional goodwill to CU300. Net assets 
are unchanged since acquisition, leaving the investment with a carrying value of CU800. 
Entity U applies the exemption from retrospective restatement for past acquisitions of investments in 
associates. [IFRS 1.C5]. Therefore, at 1 January 20X4, its date of transition, Entity U also tests the investment 
for impairment in accordance with IAS 36. At 1 January 20X4, the value of the investment in Entity V has 
recovered, and is CU1,500 based on its current listed share price. Under this scenario, the previous 
impairment to notional goodwill is not reversed, since the use of the business combination exemption as it 
applies to associates means that the goodwill determined under the previous GAAP acquisition accounting 
together with the subsequent accounting up to the date of transition is effectively grandfathered in a similar 
way to a subsidiary’s goodwill under the business combinations exemption. [IFRS 1.C4(g), IFRS 1.C4(h)]. 
Therefore, the carrying value of the notional goodwill as determined under previous GAAP becomes the 
embedded notional goodwill at transition unless specifically required to be adjusted. 
Scenario 2 - Impairment was recognised at the investment level under previous GAAP 
The same as Scenario 1, except that Entity U’s previous GAAP impairment test was performed at the 
investment level, using a test similar to that required under IAS 36. Because of applying this approach, when 
Entity U recognised an impairment loss of CU400 in the year ended 31 December 20X1, the carrying amount 
of the investment was CU900. Similar to Scenario 1, at 1 January 20X4, the value of the investment in 
Entity V has recovered and is CU1,500 based on its current listed share price. 
Under this scenario, the previous impairment of notional goodwill, which is embedded in the full amount of the 
investment, is reversed. However, the impairment can only be reversed up to the pre-impairment investment 
carrying amount (using the equity method) that results from the application of the business combination exemption. 


International GAAP® 2025 
Chapter 06 
First-time adoption 
352 
5.2.5.A 
Prohibition of other adjustments to goodwill 
IFRS 1 prohibits restatement of goodwill for most other adjustments reflected in the opening 
IFRS statement of financial position. Therefore, a first-time adopter electing not to apply IFRS 3 
retrospectively is not permitted to make any adjustments to goodwill other than those described in 
section 5.2.5 above. [IFRS 1.C4(h)]. For example, a first-time adopter cannot restate the carrying amount 
of goodwill: 
(i) To exclude in-process research and development acquired in that business combination (unless the 
related intangible asset would qualify for recognition under IAS 38 in the statement of financial 
position of the acquiree) 
(ii) To adjust previous amortisation of goodwill 
(iii) To reverse adjustments to goodwill that IFRS 3 would not permit, but were made under previous GAAP 
because of adjustments to assets and liabilities between the date of the business combination and the 
date of transition to IFRS accounting standards 
Differences between the goodwill amount in the opening IFRS statement of financial position and that in the 
financial statements under previous GAAP can arise, for example, because: 
(a) Goodwill needs to be restated as a result of a retrospective application of IAS 21 (see section 5.2.6 below) 
(b) Goodwill in relation to previously unconsolidated subsidiaries will have to be recognised (see 
section 5.2.7 below) 
(c) Goodwill in relation to transactions that do not qualify as business combinations under IFRS accounting 
standards must be derecognised (see section 5.2.1 above) 
And 
(d) ‘Negative goodwill’ that had been included within goodwill under previous GAAP is derecognised under 
IFRS accounting standards (see section 5.2.5.B below) 
Illustration 5-13 below demonstrates the adjustments required to goodwill, where applicable, for certain items. 
Illustration 5-13: 
Adjusting goodwill 
Entity W acquired Entity X but under its previous GAAP it did not recognise the following items: 
• 
Entity X’s customer lists which had a fair value of CU1,100 at the date of the acquisition and CU1,500 at 
the date of transition to IFRS accounting standards. 
• 
Deferred tax liabilities related to the fair value adjustment of Entity X’s property, plant and equipment, 
which amounted to CU9,500 at the date of the acquisition and CU7,800 at the date of transition to IFRS 
accounting standards. 
What adjustment does Entity W make to goodwill to account for the customer lists and deferred tax liabilities 
at its date of transition to IFRS accounting standards, if the business combination exemption is taken? 
As explained in section 5.2.4.B above, Entity W cannot recognise the customer lists (internally generated 
intangible assets of acquiree) when it uses the business combinations exemption. Accordingly, Entity W does 
not adjust goodwill for the customer lists. [IFRS 1.C4(b)(ii), IFRS 1.C4(f)]. 
Entity W must recognise, under IAS 12, the deferred tax liability at its date of transition to IFRS accounting 
standards because there is no exemption from recognising deferred taxes under IFRS 1. However, Entity W is 
not permitted to adjust goodwill for the deferred tax liability that would have been recognised at the date of 
acquisition. Instead, Entity W recognises the deferred tax liability of CU7,800 with a corresponding charge to 
retained earnings or other category of equity, if appropriate. [IFRS 1.C4(b), IFRS 1.C4(h), IFRS 1.C4(k)]. 
5.2.5.B 
Derecognition of negative goodwill 
IFRS 1 does not specifically address accounting for negative goodwill recognised under a previous GAAP. 
How we see it 
We believe negative goodwill is derecognised by a first-time adopter because it is not permitted to 
recognise items as assets or liabilities if IFRS accounting standards do not permit such recognition. 
Negative goodwill clearly does not meet the definition of a liability under the IASB’s Conceptual Framework, 
[CF 4.26], and its recognition is not permitted under IFRS 3. While not directly applicable to a first-time 
adopter, the transitional provisions of IFRS 3 specifically required that any negative goodwill is 
derecognised upon adoption of IFRS 3. [IFRS 3.B69(e)]. 


International GAAP® 2025 
Chapter 06 
First-time adoption 
353 
5.2.5.C 
Goodwill previously deducted from equity 
If a first-time adopter deducted goodwill from equity under its previous GAAP then it does not recognise 
that goodwill in its opening IFRS statement of financial position. Also, it does not reclassify that goodwill to 
profit or loss if it disposes of the subsidiary or if the investment in the subsidiary becomes impaired. 
[IFRS 1.C4(i)(i)]. Effectively, under IFRS accounting standards such goodwill ceases to exist, as is shown in 
Illustration 5-14 below, which is based on the implementation guidance in IFRS 1. [IFRS 1.IG Example 5]. 
Illustration 5-14: 
Goodwill deducted from equity and treatment of related intangible assets 
Entity Y acquired a subsidiary before the date of transition to IFRS accounting standards. Under its previous 
GAAP, Entity Y: 
(a) Recognised goodwill as an immediate deduction from equity 
(b) Recognised an intangible asset of the subsidiary that does not qualify for recognition as an asset under 
IAS 38 
(c) Did not recognise an intangible asset of the subsidiary that would qualify under IAS 38 for recognition as 
an asset in the financial statements of the subsidiary. The subsidiary held the asset at the date of its 
acquisition by Entity Y and at the date of transition to IFRS accounting standards. 
In its opening IFRS statement of financial position, Entity Y: 
(a) Does not recognise the goodwill, as it did not recognise the goodwill as an asset under previous GAAP 
[IFRS 1.C4(i)] 
(b) Does not recognise the intangible asset that does not qualify for recognition as an asset under IAS 38. 
Because Entity Y deducted goodwill from equity under its previous GAAP, the elimination of this intangible 
asset (and, if applicable, any related adjustments to deferred tax and non-controlling interests) reduces 
retained earnings (see section 5.2.4.A above) [IFRS 1.C4(c)(ii), IFRS 1.C4(k)] 
And 
(c) Recognises the intangible asset that qualifies under IAS 38 for recognition as an asset in the financial 
statements of the subsidiary, even though the amount assigned to it under previous GAAP in Entity Y’s 
consolidated financial statements was nil (see section 5.2.4.B above). [IFRS 1.C4(b)(ii), IFRS 1.C4(f)]. The 
recognition criteria in IAS 38 include the availability of a reliable measurement of cost, [IAS 38.21, 
IFRS 1.IG45-48], and Entity Y measures the asset at cost less accumulated depreciation and less any 
accumulated impairment losses identified under IAS 36 (see section 7.12 below). Because Entity Y 
deducted goodwill from equity under its previous GAAP, the recognition of this intangible asset (and, if 
applicable, any related adjustments to deferred tax and non-controlling interests) increases retained 
earnings. [IFRS 1.C4(c)(ii), IFRS 1.C4(k)]. However, if this intangible asset had been subsumed in goodwill 
recognised as an asset under previous GAAP, Entity Y would have decreased the carrying amount of that 
goodwill accordingly (and, if applicable, adjusted deferred tax and non-controlling interests) (see 
section 5.2.5 above). [IFRS 1.C4(b), IFRS 1.C4(f), IFRS 1.C4(g)(i), IFRS 1.C4(k)]. 
The prohibition on reinstating goodwill that was deducted from equity could have a significant impact on 
first-time adopters that hedge their foreign net investments, as demonstrated in Illustration 5-15 below. 
Illustration 5-15: 
Goodwill related to foreign net investments 
Entity Z, which uses the euro (€) as its functional currency, acquired a subsidiary in the United States whose 
functional currency is the US dollar ($). The goodwill on the acquisition of $2,100 was deducted from equity. 
Under its previous GAAP, Entity Z hedged the currency exposure on the goodwill because it would be required 
to recognise the goodwill as an expense upon disposal of the subsidiary. 
IFRS 1 does not permit reinstatement of goodwill deducted from equity nor does it permit transfer of such 
goodwill to profit or loss upon disposal of the investment in the subsidiary. [IFRS 1.C4(i)(i)]. Under IFRS 
accounting standards, goodwill deducted from equity ceases to exist and Entity Z can no longer hedge the 
currency exposure on that goodwill. Therefore, exchange gains and losses relating to the hedge will no longer 
be classified in currency translation difference but will be recognised in profit and loss after adoption of IFRS 
accounting standards. 
If a first-time adopter deducted goodwill from equity under its previous GAAP, adjustments resulting 
from the subsequent resolution of a contingency affecting the purchase consideration, at or before the 
date of transition to IFRS accounting standards, are required to be recognised in retained earnings. 
[IFRS 1.C4(i)(ii)]. Effectively, the adjustment is being accounted for in the same way as the original goodwill 
that arose on the acquisition, rather than having to be accounted for in accordance with IFRS 3. This 
requirement could affect, for example, the way a first-time adopter accounts for provisional amounts 
relating to business combinations prior to its date of transition to IFRS accounting standards, as shown in 
Illustration 5-16 below. 


International GAAP® 2025 
Chapter 06 
First-time adoption 
354 
Illustration 5-16: 
Adjustments made during measurement period to provisional amounts 
Entity A acquired Entity B on 30 September 20X1. Entity A sought an independent valuation for an item of 
property, plant and equipment acquired in the combination. However, the valuation was not completed by the 
date of transition to IFRS accounting standards (1 January 20X2). Under Entity A’s previous GAAP, any 
goodwill was written off against equity as incurred. Five months after the acquisition date (and after the date 
of transition), Entity A received the independent valuation. 
In preparing its opening IFRS statement of financial position, Entity A uses the adjusted carrying amounts of 
the identifiable net assets (see section 5.2.4.C above) with the corresponding adjustment being recognised in 
retained earnings. 
5.2.6 
Currency adjustments to goodwill 
A first-time adopter need not apply IAS 21 retrospectively to fair value adjustments and goodwill arising in business 
combinations that occurred before the date of transition to IFRS accounting standards. [IFRS 1.C2, IFRS 1.IG21A]. This 
exemption is different from the ‘cumulative translation differences’ exemption, which is discussed in section 5.6 below. 
IAS 21 requires any goodwill arising on the acquisition of a foreign operation and any fair value adjustments to the 
carrying amounts of assets and liabilities arising on the acquisition of that foreign operation to be treated as assets and 
liabilities of the foreign operation. Thus, they are expressed in the functional currency of the foreign operation and are 
translated at the closing rate in accordance with the requirements discussed in Chapter 16 at section 6.5. [IAS 21.47]. 
For a first-time adopter it might be impracticable, especially after a corporate restructuring, to determine 
retrospectively the currency denomination of the goodwill and fair value adjustments required by IAS 21. 
If IAS 21 is not applied retrospectively, a first-time adopter instead treats such fair value adjustments and goodwill as 
assets and liabilities of the entity rather than as assets and liabilities of the acquiree. As a result, those goodwill and fair 
value adjustments are either already expressed in the entity’s functional currency or are non-monetary foreign currency 
items that are reported using the exchange rate applied in accordance with previous GAAP. [IFRS 1.C2]. 
However, a first-time adopter is allowed to choose to apply IAS 21 retrospectively to fair value adjustments and goodwill 
arising in either: [IFRS 1.C1, IFRS 1.C3] 
(a) All business combinations that occurred before the date of transition to IFRS accounting standards 
Or 
(b) All business combinations that the entity elects to restate to comply with IFRS 3 
In practice, the exemption might be of limited use for a number of reasons: 
First, the exemption permits ‘goodwill and fair value adjustments’ to be treated as assets and liabilities of the 
entity rather than as assets and liabilities of the acquiree, but does not clarify whether or not the exemption, if 
taken, must be applied to both goodwill and fair value adjustments. 
How we see it 
We believe that implicit in the exemption is the requirement to treat both the goodwill and the fair value 
adjustments consistently, as the exemption requires to treat them as assets and liabilities of the entity if 
the entity does not apply IAS 21 retrospectively to those fair value adjustments and goodwill. 
However, the IASB apparently did not consider that many first-time adopters, under their previous GAAPs, will 
have treated fair value adjustments as assets or liabilities of the acquiree (so expressed in the foreign operation’s 
functional currency), while at the same time treating goodwill as an asset of the acquirer (so expressed in the 
entity’s functional currency). As noted above, we believe that the exemption requires the same treatment for 
both the goodwill and the fair value adjustments. In such cases, this means that the first-time adopter needs to 
use one of the following approaches: 
(i) 
Goodwill and fair value adjustments treated as assets of the acquired foreign operation (i.e., in the foreign 
operation’s functional currency) 
The goodwill (which was previously expressed as an asset of the first-time adopter) is treated as an asset of 
the foreign operation from the date of acquisition. Therefore, the goodwill and fair value adjustments are 
measured, both initially and subsequently, in the foreign operation’s functional currency, and are reported in 
the opening IFRS statement of financial position using the exchange rate at the date of transition to IFRS 
accounting standards. 
(ii) Goodwill and fair value adjustments treated as separate assets of the first-time adopter 
The fair value adjustments (which were previously expressed in the functional currency of the foreign operation 
acquired) are treated as separate assets (and liabilities) of the first-time adopter from the date of acquisition. 
Therefore, the goodwill and fair value adjustments are measured, both initially and subsequently, in the first-time 
adopter’s functional currency, and are consistently expressed in that currency. If the presentation currency of the 
first-time adopter differs from its functional currency the recomputed functional currency amounts are then 
translated to the presentation currency of the first-time adopter. 


International GAAP® 2025 
Chapter 06 
First-time adoption 
355 
As the exemption under IFRS 1 did not foresee this particular situation, those first-time adopters will need 
to restate either their goodwill or fair value adjustments. In many cases, restatement of goodwill is less 
onerous than restatement of fair value adjustments. 
Secondly, the paragraphs in IFRS 1 that introduce the exemption were drafted at a later date than the rest 
of the Appendix of which they form part. Instead of referring to ‘first-time adopter’ these paragraphs refer 
to ‘entity’. 
How we see it 
In our view, it is clear from the context that ‘entity’ is read as ‘first-time adopter’. This means that the 
exemption only permits goodwill and fair value adjustments to be treated as assets and liabilities of the 
first-time adopter (i.e., the ultimate parent in the reporting group). 
In practice, many groups have treated goodwill and fair value adjustments as assets and liabilities of an 
intermediate parent in the reporting group. Where the intermediate parent has a functional currency that is 
different from that of the ultimate parent who is the first-time adopter or the acquiree, it will be necessary 
to restate goodwill and fair value adjustments. 
The decision to treat goodwill and fair value adjustments as either items denominated in the parent’s or the 
acquiree’s functional currency will also affect the extent to which the net investment in those foreign 
subsidiaries can be hedged (see also section 4.5.5 above). 
5.2.7 
Previously unconsolidated subsidiaries 
A first-time adopter which did not consolidate a subsidiary acquired in a past business combination under 
previous GAAP, when applying the business combinations exemption, measures the carrying amounts of 
the subsidiary’s assets and liabilities in its consolidated financial statements at the date of transition to IFRS 
accounting standards at either: [IFRS 1.IG27(a)] 
(a) If the subsidiary has adopted IFRS accounting standards - the same carrying amounts as in the 
IFRS financial statements of the subsidiary, after adjusting for consolidation procedures and for the 
effects of the business combination in which it acquired the subsidiary. [IFRS 1.D17]. 
Or 
(b) If the subsidiary has not adopted IFRS accounting standards - the carrying amounts that IFRS 
accounting standards would require in the subsidiary’s statement of financial position. [IFRS 1.C4(j)]. 
The deemed cost of goodwill is the difference at the date of transition between: [IFRS 1.C4(j)] 
(i) The parent’s interest in those adjusted carrying amounts 
And 
(ii) The cost in the parent’s separate financial statements of its investment in the subsidiary 
How we see it 
The cost of an investment in a subsidiary in the parent’s separate financial statements will depend on 
which option the parent has taken to measure the cost under IFRS 1 (see section 5.7.2 below). 
A parent can elect to measure its investment in a subsidiary at one of the following amounts as the cost in 
its separate opening IFRS statement of financial position: [IFRS 1.D15] 
(a) Cost determined in accordance with IAS 27 Separate Financial Statements 
Or 
(b) Deemed cost, which is its: 
(i) Fair value at the entity’s date of transition to IFRS accounting standards in its separate financial 
statements 
Or 
(ii) Previous GAAP carrying amount at that date 


International GAAP® 2025 
Chapter 06 
First-time adoption 
356 
A first-time adopter using the business combinations exemption not to apply IFRS 3 retrospectively is 
precluded from calculating what the goodwill would have been at the date of the original acquisition. The 
deemed cost of goodwill is capitalised in the opening IFRS statement of financial position. Illustration 5-17 
below, which is based on one within the implementation guidance in IFRS 1, demonstrates this requirement. 
[IFRS 1.IG Example 6]. 
Illustration 5-17: 
Subsidiary not consolidated under previous GAAP 
Background 
Entity C’s date of transition to IFRS accounting standards is 1 January 20X2. Under its previous GAAP, 
Entity C did not consolidate its 75% interest in Entity D, which it acquired in a business combination on 15 July 
20X0. On 1 January 20X2: 
(a) The cost of Entity C’s investment in Entity D is CU180 
(b) Under IFRS accounting standards, Entity D would measure its assets at CU500 and its liabilities (including 
deferred tax under IAS 12) at CU300. On this basis, Entity D’s net assets are CU200 under IFRS 
accounting standards. 
Application of requirements 
Entity C consolidates Entity D. The consolidated statement of financial position at 1 January 20X2 includes: 
(a) Entity D’s assets at CU500 and liabilities at CU300 
(b) Non-controlling interests of CU50 (25% of [CU500 - CU300]) 
(c) Goodwill of CU30 (cost of CU180 less 75% of [CU500 - CU300]). [IFRS 1.C4(j)]. Entity C tests the goodwill 
for impairment under IAS 36 (see section 7.12 below) and recognises any resulting impairment loss, 
based on conditions that existed at the date of transition to IFRS accounting standards. [IFRS 1.C4(g)(ii)]. 
If the cost of the subsidiary (as measured under IFRS 1, see section 5.7.2 below) is lower than the 
proportionate share of net asset value at the date of transition to IFRS accounting standards, the difference 
is taken to retained earnings, or other category of equity, if appropriate. 
Slightly different rules apply to all other subsidiaries (i.e., those not acquired in a business combination but 
created) that an entity did not consolidate under its previous GAAP, the main difference being that goodwill 
is not recognised in relation to those subsidiaries. [IFRS 1.IG27(c)]. 
How we see it 
Note that this exemption requires the use of the cost of the investment in the separate financial 
statements of the parent prepared using IAS 27. Therefore, if a first-time adopter, in its separate 
financial statements, does not opt to measure its cost of investment in a subsidiary at deemed cost 
(i.e., its fair value or previous GAAP carrying amount at the date of transition), it is required to calculate 
the deemed cost of the goodwill by comparing the cost of the investment (as determined in accordance 
with IAS 27) to its share of the carrying amount of the net assets determined on a different date. 
In the case of a highly profitable subsidiary this could give rise to the following anomaly, as shown in 
Illustration 5-18 below: 
Illustration 5-18: 
Calculation of deemed cost of goodwill 
Entity E acquired Entity F before the date of transition to IFRS accounting standards for CU500. The net assets 
of Entity F would have been CU220 under IFRS accounting standards at the date of acquisition. Entity F makes 
on average an annual net profit of CU60, which it does not distribute to Entity E. 
At the date of transition to IFRS accounting standards, the cost of Entity E’s investment in Entity F is still 
CU500. However, the net assets of Entity F have increased to CU460. Therefore, under IFRS 1, the deemed 
cost of goodwill is CU40. [IFRS 1.C4(j)]. 
The deemed cost of goodwill is much lower than the goodwill that was paid at the date of acquisition 
because Entity F did not distribute its profits. In fact, if Entity F had distributed a dividend to its parent just 
before its date of transition to IFRS accounting standards, the deemed cost of goodwill would have been 
significantly higher. 


International GAAP® 2025

First-time adoption 
357 
5.2.8 
Previously consolidated entities that are not subsidiaries 
A first-time adopter might have consolidated an investment under its previous GAAP that does not meet the 
definition of a subsidiary under IFRS accounting standards. In this case the entity first determines the 
appropriate classification of the investment under IFRS accounting standards and then applies the first-time 
adoption rules in IFRS 1. Generally, such a previously consolidated investment is accounted for as either: 
• 
An associate, a joint venture or a joint operation: 
First-time adopters applying the business combinations exemption must also apply that exemption to 
past acquisitions of investments in associates, interests in joint ventures or interests in joint operations 
in which the activity of the joint operation constitutes a business, as defined in IFRS 3. [IFRS 1.C5]. If the 
business combinations exemption is not applicable or the entity did not acquire (i.e., the entity created) 
the investment in the associate or joint venture, IAS 28 is applied retrospectively unless the entity 
applies the joint arrangements exemption. [IFRS 1.D31]. (See section 5.17 below). 
• 
An investment under IFRS 9 (see section 5.10 below). 
Or 
• 
An executory contract or service concession arrangement: 
There are no first-time adoption exemptions that apply; therefore, IFRS accounting standards are 
applied retrospectively. However, a practical relief is provided for a service concession arrangement if 
retrospective application is impracticable (see section 5.13 below). [IFRS 1.D22]. 
5.2.9 
Measurement of deferred taxes and non-controlling interests 
Deferred tax is calculated based on the difference between the carrying amounts of assets and liabilities 
and their respective tax bases. Therefore, deferred taxes are recalculated after all assets acquired and 
liabilities assumed have been adjusted under IFRS 1. [IFRS 1.C4(k)]. 
IFRS 10 defines non-controlling interest as the “Equity in a subsidiary not attributable, directly or 
indirectly, to a parent.” [IFRS 10 Appendix A]. This definition is discussed in Chapter 8 at section 5.1. Non-
controlling interests are calculated after all assets acquired, liabilities assumed and deferred taxes have 
been adjusted under IFRS 1. [IFRS 1.C4(k)]. 
Any resulting changes in the carrying amount of deferred taxes and non-controlling interests are 
recognised by adjusting retained earnings (or, if appropriate, another category of equity), unless they 
relate to adjustments to intangible assets that are adjusted against goodwill. See Illustration 5-19 below. 
[IFRS 1.IG Example 4]. 
In terms of the treatment of deferred tax in relation to recognised intangible assets, there is a difference 
depending on whether the first-time adopter previously recognised acquired intangible assets in accordance 
with its previous GAAP or whether it had subsumed the intangible asset into goodwill (in both cases, a 
deferred tax liability was not recognised under its previous GAAP but is required to be recognised under 
IFRS accounting standards). In the first case, the first-time adopter must recognise a deferred tax liability 
and adjust non-controlling interests and opening reserves accordingly. [IFRS 1.C4(b), IFRS 1.C4(k), IAS 12.15(b)]. 
By contrast, in the second case it would have to decrease the carrying amount of goodwill, recognise the 
intangible assets and a deferred tax liability and adjust non-controlling interests as necessary, as discussed 
in section 5.2.5 above. [IFRS 1.C4(b), IFRS 1.C4(f), IFRS 1.C4(g)(i), IFRS 1.C4(k), IAS12.15(b)]. The IASB discussed this 
issue in October 2005, but decided not to propose an amendment to address this inconsistency, i.e., the 
difference in the treatment of deferred tax depending on whether acquired intangible assets were 
recognised separately or subsumed within goodwill under previous GAAP.5 
 
5 
IASB Update, October 2005, available on the IFRS Foundation’s website. 


International GAAP® 2025 
Chapter 06 
First-time adoption 
358 
Illustration 5-19 below illustrates how to account for restatement of intangible assets, deferred tax and 
non-controlling interests where previous GAAP requires deferred tax to be recognised. [IFRS 1.IG Example 4]. 
Illustration 5-19: 
Restatement of intangible assets, deferred tax and non-controlling interests 
Entity G’s first IFRS financial statements are for a period that ends on 31 December 20X3 and include 
comparative information for 20X2 only. On 1 July 20X0, Entity G acquired 75% of subsidiary H. Under its 
previous GAAP, Entity G assigned an initial carrying amount of CU200 to intangible assets that would not have 
qualified for recognition under IAS 38. The tax base of the intangible assets was CUnil, giving rise to a deferred 
tax liability (at 30%) of CU60. Entity G measured non-controlling interests as their share of the fair value of the 
identifiable net assets acquired. Goodwill arising on the acquisition was capitalised as an asset in Entity G’s 
consolidated financial statements. 
On 1 January 20X2 (the date of transition to IFRS accounting standards), the carrying amount of the 
intangible assets under previous GAAP was CU160, and the carrying amount of the related deferred tax 
liability was CU48 (30% of CU160). 
Under IFRS 1, Entity G derecognises intangible assets that do not qualify for recognition as separate assets 
under IAS 38, together with the related deferred tax liability of CU48 and non-controlling interests, with a 
corresponding increase in goodwill (see section 5.2.5 above). The related non-controlling interests amount to 
CU28 (25% of CU112 (CU160-CU48)). Entity G makes the following adjustment in its opening IFRS statement 
of financial position: [IFRS 1.C4(g)(i)] 
Dr 
Goodwill 
CU84 
  
Dr 
Deferred tax liability 
CU48 
  
Dr 
Non-controlling interests  
CU28 
  
  
Cr 
Intangible assets 
  
CU160 
Entity G tests the goodwill for impairment under IAS 36 and recognises any resulting impairment loss, based 
on conditions that existed at the date of transition to IFRS accounting standards. [IFRS 1.C4(g)(ii)]. 
5.2.10 
Transition accounting for contingent consideration 
IFRS 1 is silent on accounting for contingent consideration that relates to transactions that are within the 
scope of the business combinations exemption. 
How we see it 
The business combination exemption does not extend to contingent consideration that arose from a 
transaction that occurred before the date of transition to IFRS accounting standards, even if the 
acquisition itself is not restated due to the use of the exemption. Therefore, such contingent 
consideration, other than that classified as equity under IAS 32, is recognised at its fair value at the date 
of transition to IFRS accounting standards, regardless of the accounting under previous GAAP. 
If the contingent consideration was not recognised at fair value at the date of transition to IFRS accounting 
standards under previous GAAP, the resulting adjustment is recognised in retained earnings or other 
category of equity, if appropriate. Subsequent adjustments will be recognised following the provisions of 
IFRS 3. [IFRS 3.40, IFRS 3.58]. 
5.3 
Share-based payment transactions 
IFRS 2 applies to accounting for the acquisition of goods or services in equity-settled share-based payment 
transactions, cash-settled share-based payment transactions and transactions in which the entity or the 
counterparty has the option to choose between settlement in cash or equity. There is no exemption from 
recognising share-based payment transactions that have not yet vested at the date of transition to IFRS 
accounting standards. The exemptions in IFRS 1 clarify that a first-time adopter is not required to apply 
IFRS 2 fully retrospectively to equity-settled share-based payment transactions that have already vested at 
the date of transition to IFRS accounting standards. 


International GAAP® 2025 
Chapter 06 
First-time adoption 
359 
IFRS 1 contains the following exemptions and requirements regarding share-based payment transactions: 
(a) Only if a first-time adopter has disclosed publicly the fair value of its equity instruments, determined at 
the measurement date, as defined in IFRS 2, then it is encouraged to apply IFRS 2 to: [IFRS 1.D2] 
(i) Equity instruments that were granted on or before 7 November 2002 (i.e., the date the IASB 
issued ED 2 Share-based Payment) 
(ii) Equity instruments that were granted after 7 November 2002 but vested before the date of 
transition to IFRS accounting standards. 
Many first-time adopters that did not use fair value-based share-based payment accounting under 
previous GAAP will not have published the fair value of equity instruments granted and are, therefore, 
not allowed to apply IFRS 2 retrospectively to those share-based payment transactions. 
(b) For all grants of equity instruments to which IFRS 2 has not been applied, a first-time adopter must 
still make the disclosures relating to the nature and extent of share-based payments required by 
IFRS 2.44-45. [IFRS 1.D2, IFRS 2.44, IFRS 2.45]. 
(c) If a first-time adopter modifies the terms or conditions of a grant of equity instruments to which IFRS 2 
has not been applied, the entity is not required to apply the requirements of IFRS 2.26-29 if the 
modification occurred before the date of transition to IFRS accounting standards. [IFRS 1.D2, IFRS 2.26, 
IFRS 2.27, IFRS 2.28, IFRS 2.28A, IFRS 2.29]. 
(d) A first-time adopter is encouraged, but not required, to apply IFRS 2 to liabilities arising from 
share-based payment transactions that were settled before the date of transition to IFRS 
accounting standards. [IFRS 1.D3]. 
There are a number of interpretation issues concerning these exemptions and requirements: 
• 
Meaning of ‘disclosed publicly’ under (a) above 
IFRS 1 only permits retrospective application of IFRS 2 if the entity has ‘disclosed publicly’ the fair 
value of the equity instruments concerned, but IFRS accounting standards do not define what is meant 
by ‘disclosed publicly’. 
How we see it 
While IFRS 1 does not specifically require public disclosure of the fair value of an entity’s share-
based payment transactions in its previous financial statements, it is clear that IFRS 1 requires fair 
value to have been published contemporaneously. In addition, the requirements in IFRS 1 to 
disclose publicly the fair value of share-based payment transactions can be met even if the fair 
value is only disclosed in aggregate rather than for individual awards. 
• 
First-time adopters encouraged to apply IFRS 2 under (a) and (d) above 
The ‘date of transition to IFRS accounting standards’ to which those exemptions refer is the first day of 
the earliest comparative period presented in a first-time adopter’s first IFRS financial statements. 
How we see it 
This effectively means that an entity could accelerate the vesting of an award that was otherwise 
due to vest after the date of transition to IFRS accounting standards in order to avoid applying 
IFRS 2 to that award. 
• 
Whether a consistent selection of the exemptions under (a) and (d) above is required 
How we see it 
A first-time adopter can choose which of the exemptions under (a) and (d) it wants to apply, 
i.e., there is no specific requirement to select the exemptions in a consistent manner. 
• 
Meaning of ‘encouraged’ under (a) and (d) above 
Under IFRS 1, a first-time adopter is encouraged, but not required, to apply IFRS 2 to certain 
categories of share-based payment transactions (see (a) and (d) above). IFRS 1 does not specifically 
prohibit a literal reading of ‘encouraged’, which could, for example, allow a first-time adopter to decide 
to apply IFRS 2 only to some share-based payment transactions granted before 7 November 2002. 


International GAAP® 2025 
Chapter 06 
First-time adoption 
360 
How we see it 
We believe that it would generally be acceptable for a first-time adopter to apply IFRS 2 only to 
share-based payment transactions: 
(1) After an earlier date chosen by the entity (e.g., 1 January 2001), while not applying it to earlier 
transactions 
(2) For which fair values were disclosed publicly 
• 
Treatment of modifications, cancellations and settlements under (c) above 
There is a slight ambiguity concerning the interpretation of the exemption under (c) above, because 
IFRS 1.D2 refers only to the modification of awards. This could allow a literal argument that IFRS 1 
does not prescribe any specific treatment when an entity cancels or settles, as opposed to modifying, 
an award to which IFRS 2 has not been applied. However, IFRS 1.D2 also requires an entity to apply 
IFRS 2.26-29 to ‘modified’ awards. These paragraphs deal not only with modification but also with 
cancellation and settlement (see Chapter 30 at section 7); indeed IFRS 2.28-29 are not relevant to 
modifications at all. 
How we see it 
Given the reference to IFRS 2.26-29 in IFRS 1.D2, it is clear that the IASB intended IFRS 2 to be 
applied not only to modifications, but also to any cancellation or settlement of an award to which 
IFRS 2 has not been applied, unless the modification, cancellation or settlement occurs before the 
date of transition to IFRS accounting standards. 
• 
Transactions where the counterparty has a choice of settlement method 
These are not specifically addressed in the first-time adoption rules. 
How we see it 
It therefore appears that, where such transactions give rise to recognition of both an equity 
component and a liability component, the equity component is subject to the transitional rules for 
equity-settled transactions and the liability component to those for cash-settled transactions. This 
could well mean that the liability component of such a transaction is recognised in the financial 
statements, whilst the equity component is not. 
• 
Application of IFRS 2 to cash-settled transactions settled before the date of transition to IFRS 
accounting standards under (d) above 
How we see it 
It is not entirely clear what lies behind the exemption under (d) above, since a first-time adopter 
would never be required to report a share-based payment transaction (or indeed any transaction) 
settled before the date of transition. 
In Practical example 5-3 below, Manulife Financial Corporation (Manulife) made use of the exemption not to 
apply IFRS 2 to share-based payment transactions that were fully vested at the date of transition to IFRS 
accounting standards. The Practical example also illustrates that the share-based payment exemption does 
not apply for awards that were granted after 7 November 2002 and that were still vesting at the date of 
transition to IFRS accounting standards.

First-time adoption

Practical example 5-3: 
Manulife Financial Corporation (2011) 
Canada 
2011 Annual Report [extract] 
Notes to Consolidated Financial Statements [extract] 
 
(a) First-time adoption elections [extract] 
Optional exemptions [extract] 
 
 
5.3.1 
Use of previously published fair values 
There is no explicit requirement in IFRS 1 or IFRS 2 that any voluntary retrospective application of IFRS 2 
must be based on the fair value previously published. This might appear to allow a first-time adopter the 
flexibility of using a different valuation for IFRS 2 purposes than that previously used for disclosure purposes. 
How we see it 
The requirements of IFRS 1 in relation to estimates under previous GAAP (see section 4.2 above) mean 
that the assumptions used, when retrospectively applying IFRS 2, must be consistent with those used in 
the originally disclosed valuation. In our view, the entity will also need to consider the implications of the 
assertion, in effect, that there is more than one fair value for the same transaction. 
5.3.2 
Restatement of costs recognised under previous GAAP 
A first-time adopter could elect to take advantage of the transition exemption in IFRS 1 which allows it not 
to apply IFRS 2 to equity-settled share-based payment transactions that were vested before the date of 
transition to IFRS accounting standards, despite having recognised a cost for those transactions in 
accordance with its previous GAAP. Neither IFRS 1 nor IFRS 2 clearly indicates the appropriate treatment 
of the costs of share-based payment transactions that were recognised under the previous GAAP. 
How we see it 
There are mixed views on this issue in different jurisdictions, some of which are being driven by local regulatory 
expectations. In practice, either of the following approaches is considered acceptable, provided that the 
treatment chosen is disclosed in the financial statements if the previously recognised costs are material: 
• 
Not to change expense previously recognised under previous GAAP for equity instruments subject to 
transitional relief under IFRS 1. 
This approach is consistent with the approach for cash-settled share-based payment transactions that are 
settled prior to the date of transition to IFRS accounting standards, since the entity must reflect the 
reduction in cash that was actually paid (and therefore the reduction in retained earnings) in the first 
IFRS financial statements. 
Or 
• 
To derecognise share-based payment expense under previous GAAP for equity instruments subject to 
transitional relief under IFRS 1. 
In other words, reverse cumulative amounts recognised in the retained earnings and equity accounts 
of the opening IFRS statement of financial position. 


International GAAP® 2025 
Chapter 06 
First-time adoption 
362 
5.4 
Deemed cost 
IFRS 1 requires full retrospective application of IFRS accounting standards effective at the end of a first-
time adopter’s first IFRS reporting period. [IFRS 1.7]. Therefore, in the absence of the deemed cost 
exemption, the requirements of, for example, IAS 16, IAS 38, IAS 40 Investment Property and IFRS 6 
Exploration for and Evaluation of Mineral Resources would have to be applied as if the first-time adopter 
had always applied these standards. This could be quite onerous because: 
• 
These items are long-lived which means that accounting records from the time of acquisition might not 
be available anymore. In the case of formerly state-owned businesses, the required accounting records 
possibly never even existed 
• 
The entity might have revalued the items in the past as a matter of accounting policy or because this 
was required under national law 
Or 
• 
Even if the items were carried at depreciated cost, the accounting policy for recognition and 
depreciation might not have been compliant with IFRS accounting standards 
Given the significance of items like property, plant and equipment in the statement of financial position of 
most first-time adopters and the sheer number of transactions affecting property, plant and equipment, 
restatement is not only difficult but would often also involve huge cost and effort. Therefore, the IASB 
decided to introduce the notion of a ‘deemed cost’ that is not the ‘true’ cost basis of an asset (as required 
by IFRS accounting standards), but a surrogate that is deemed to be a suitable starting point. 
There are seven separate deemed cost exemptions in IFRS 1: 
(a) Fair value or revaluation as deemed cost (see section 5.4.1 below) 
(b) Event-driven fair value measurement as deemed cost (see section 5.4.2 below) 
(c) Deemed cost for oil and gas assets (see section 5.4.3 below) 
(d) Deemed cost for assets used (or previously used) in operations subject to rate regulation (see 
section 5.4.4 below) 
(e) Deemed cost for assets acquired, liabilities assumed and goodwill in a business combination not 
restated under IFRS accounting standards (see section 5.2 above) 
(f) Deemed cost for a right-of-use asset for a lessee based on certain exemptions in IFRS 1 (see 
section 5.5 below) 
(g) Deemed cost in determining the cost of an investment in a subsidiary, joint venture or associate in 
separate financial statements (see section 5.7.2 below) 
5.4.1 
Fair value or revaluation as deemed cost 
To deal with the problem of restatement of long-lived assets upon first-time adoption of IFRS accounting 
standards, IFRS 1 permits a first-time adopter - for the categories of assets listed below - to measure an 
item in its opening IFRS statement of financial position using an amount that is based on its deemed cost 
(see section 5.4.1.A below): [IFRS 1.D5, IFRS 1.D6, IFRS 1.D7] 
• 
Property, plant and equipment, including bearer plants (see section 7.4 below) [IFRS 1.D5, IFRS 1.D6] 
• 
Investment property, if an entity elects to use the cost model in IAS 40. [IFRS 1.D7(a)]. The fact that the 
exemption can only be applied to investment property accounted for under the cost model will not pose 
any problems in practice as the fair value model under IAS 40 requires an entity to measure its 
investment property at fair value at its date of transition to IFRS accounting standards. 
• 
Right-of-use assets under IFRS 16 [IFRS 1.D7(aa)] 
• 
Intangible assets (see section 7.14 below) that meet: [IFRS 1.D7(b)] 
• 
The recognition criteria in IAS 38 (including reliable measurement of original cost) 
And 
• 
The criteria in IAS 38 for revaluation (including the existence of an active market) 
A first-time adopter cannot use this deemed cost approach by analogy for any other assets or for liabilities. 
[IFRS 1.D7]. 
The use of fair value or revaluation as deemed cost for intangible assets will be very limited in practice 
because of the definition of an ‘active market’ in IFRS 13. An active market is defined as: “A market in 
which transactions for the asset or liability take place with sufficient frequency and volume to provide 
pricing information on an ongoing basis.” [IFRS 13 Appendix A]. It is therefore unlikely that a first-time adopter 
will be able to apply this exemption to any intangible assets (see Chapter 18 at section 8.2). 


International GAAP® 2025 
Chapter 06 
First-time adoption 
363 
It is important to note that this exemption does not take classes or categories of assets as its unit of 
measure, but refers to “an item of property, plant and equipment”, and similarly for investment property, 
right-of-use assets under IFRS 16 and intangible assets. [IFRS 1.D5, IFRS 1.D6, IFRS 1.D7]. 
How we see it 
A first-time adopter is allowed to apply the deemed cost exemption to only some of its assets. For 
example, it could apply the exemption only to: 
• 
A selection of properties 
• 
Part of a factory 
Or 
• 
Some of the right-of-use asset leased under a single lease agreement 
This is because IFRS 1.IG12 states that “IAS 16 does not prescribe the unit of measure for recognition of 
an asset, i.e., what constitutes an item of property, plant and equipment. Thus, judgement is required in 
applying the recognition criteria to an entity’s specific circumstances” (see Chapter 19 at section 3.1). 
The IASB argued that it is not necessary to restrict application of the exemption to classes of assets to 
prevent arbitrarily selective revaluations, because IAS 36 “requires an impairment test if there is any 
indication that an asset is impaired. Thus, if an entity uses fair value as deemed cost for assets whose fair 
value is above cost, it cannot ignore indications that the recoverable amount of other assets may have 
fallen below their carrying amount. Therefore, the IFRS does not restrict the use of fair value as deemed 
cost to entire classes of asset.” [IFRS 1.BC45]. 
How we see it 
In our view, it is doubtful that the quality of financial information would benefit from a revaluation of a 
haphazard selection of items of property, plant and equipment as deemed cost. Therefore, a first-time 
adopter needs to exercise judgement in selecting the items to which it believes it is appropriate to apply 
the exemption. 
Practical examples 5-4 and 5-5 below refer to the use of the ‘fair value or revaluation as deemed cost’ exemption. 
Practical example 5-4: 
Suncor Energy Inc. (2011) 
Canada

NOTES TO THE CONSOLIDATED FINANCIAL STATEMENTS [extract]
