5.6.1 
Gains and losses arising on related hedges 
IFRS 1 is not entirely clear whether this exemption extends to similar gains and losses arising on related 
hedges. Paragraph D13, which contains the exemption, explains that a first-time adopter need not comply 
with ‘these requirements.’ [IFRS 1.D13]. The requirements referred to are those summarised in IFRS 1.D12 
which explain that IAS 21 requires an entity: [IFRS 1.D12] 
(a) To recognise some translation differences in other comprehensive income and accumulate these in a 
separate component of equity 
And 
(b) On disposal of a foreign operation, to reclassify the cumulative translation difference for that foreign 
operation (including, if applicable, gains and losses on related hedges) from equity to profit or loss as 
part of the gain or loss on disposal 
The problem arises because IFRS 1.D12 does not refer to the recognition of hedging gains or losses in other 
comprehensive income and accumulation in a separate component of equity (only the subsequent 
reclassification thereof). Accordingly, a very literal reading of the standard might suggest that an entity is 
required to identify historical gains and losses on such hedges. However, even if this position is accepted, 
the basis on which this might be done is not at all clear. 
How we see it 
We believe it is entirely appropriate for this exemption to be applied to net investment hedges as well as 
the underlying gains and losses. It is clear that the reasons cited by the IASB for including this exemption, 
apply as much to related hedges as they do to the underlying exchange differences. 


International GAAP® 2025 
Chapter 06 
First-time adoption 
376 
5.7 
Investments in subsidiaries, joint ventures and associates 
5.7.1 
Consolidated financial statements: subsidiaries and structured entities 
A first-time adopter must consolidate all subsidiaries (as defined in IFRS 10) unless IFRS 10 requires 
otherwise. [IFRS 1.IG26]. First-time adoption of IFRS accounting standards could therefore result in the 
consolidation for the first time of a subsidiary not consolidated under previous GAAP, either because the 
subsidiary was not regarded as such before, or because the parent did not prepare consolidated financial 
statements. If a first-time adopter did not consolidate a subsidiary under its previous GAAP, it must 
recognise the assets and liabilities of that subsidiary in its consolidated financial statements at the date of 
transition to IFRS accounting standards at either: [IFRS 1.IG27(a)] 
(a) If the subsidiary has adopted IFRS accounting standards, the same carrying amounts as in the 
IFRS financial statements of the subsidiary, after adjusting for consolidation procedures and for the 
effects of the business combination in which it acquired the subsidiary [IFRS 1.D17] 
Or 
(b) If the subsidiary has not adopted IFRS accounting standards, the carrying amounts that IFRS 
accounting standards would require in the subsidiary’s statement of financial position [IFRS 1.C4(j)] 
If the newly-consolidated subsidiary was acquired in a business combination before the date of the parent’s 
transition to IFRS accounting standards, goodwill is the difference between the parent’s interest in the 
carrying amount determined under either (a) or (b) above and the cost in the parent’s separate financial 
statements of its investment in the subsidiary. This is no more than a pragmatic ‘plug’ that facilitates the 
consolidation process but does not represent the true goodwill that might have been recorded if IFRS 
accounting standards had been applied to the original business combination (see section 5.2.7 above). 
[IFRS 1.C4(j), IFRS 1.IG27(b)]. 
How we see it 
If the first-time adopter accounted for the investment as an associate under its previous GAAP, it cannot 
use the notional goodwill previously calculated under the equity method as the basis for goodwill under 
IFRS accounting standards. 
If the parent did not acquire the subsidiary, but established it, it does not recognise goodwill. [IFRS 1.IG27(c)]. 
Any difference between the cost of investment in the subsidiary in the parent’s separate financial 
statements and the parent’s interest in the identifiable net assets as determined in (a) or (b) above would 
be treated as an adjustment to retained earnings, representing the accumulated profits or losses 
attributable to the parent that would have been recognised as if the subsidiary had always been 
consolidated. 
The adjustment of the carrying amounts of assets and liabilities of a first-time adopter’s subsidiaries might 
affect non-controlling interests and deferred tax, as discussed in section 5.2.9 above. [IFRS 1.C4(k), IFRS 1.IG28]. 
5.7.2 
Separate financial statements: Cost of an investment in a subsidiary, joint 
venture or associate 
When an entity prepares separate financial statements, IAS 27 requires a first-time adopter to account for 
its investments in subsidiaries, joint ventures and associates either: [IFRS 1.D14] 
• 
At cost 
• 
In accordance with IFRS 9 
Or 
• 
Using the equity method as described in IAS 28 (see Chapter 9 at section 2) 
However, if a first-time adopter measures such an investment at cost then it can elect to measure that investment 
at one of the following amounts in its separate opening IFRS statement of financial position: [IFRS 1.D15] 
(a) Cost determined in accordance with IAS 27 
Or 
(b) Deemed cost, which is its: 
(i) 
Fair value at the entity’s date of transition to IFRS accounting standards in its separate financial 
statements 
Or 
(ii) Previous GAAP carrying amount at that date 


International GAAP® 2025 
Chapter 06 
First-time adoption 
377 
A first-time adopter is permitted to choose to use either of these bases to measure its investment in each 
subsidiary, joint venture or associate where it elects to use a deemed cost. [IFRS 1.D15]. 
A first-time adopter that applies the exemption discloses certain additional information in its financial 
statements (see section 6.5.2 below). [IFRS 1.31]. 
For a first-time adopter that chooses to account for such an investment using the equity method 
procedures in accordance with IAS 28: [IFRS 1.D15A] 
(a) The first-time adopter applies the exemption for past business combinations in IFRS 1 (Appendix C) to 
the acquisition of the investment (see section 5.2 above) [IFRS 1 Appendix C] 
(b) If the entity becomes a first-time adopter for its separate financial statements earlier than for its 
consolidated financial statements and: 
(i) Later than its parent, the entity would apply IFRS 1.D16 in its separate financial statements (see 
section 5.8.1 below). [IFRS 1.D16]. 
(ii) Later than its subsidiary, the entity would apply IFRS 1.D17 in its separate financial statements 
(see section 5.8.2 below). [IFRS 1.D17]. 
5.8 
Assets and liabilities of subsidiaries, associates and joint ventures 
Within groups, some subsidiaries, associates and joint ventures could have a different date of transition to 
IFRS accounting standards than the parent, investor or venturer, for example, because national legislation 
required the use of IFRS accounting standards after, or prohibited the use of IFRS accounting standards at, 
the date of transition of the parent, investor or venturer. As this could have resulted in permanent 
differences between the carrying amounts reported under IFRS accounting standards in a subsidiary’s own 
financial statements and those it reports to its parent, the IASB introduced a special exemption regarding 
the assets and liabilities of subsidiaries, associates and joint ventures. 
IFRS 1 contains detailed guidance on the approach to be adopted when a parent adopts IFRS accounting 
standards before its subsidiary (see section 5.8.1 below) and when a subsidiary adopts IFRS accounting 
standards before its parent (see section 5.8.2 below). 
These provisions also apply when IFRS accounting standards are adopted at different dates by an investor 
in an associate and the associate, or by a venturer in a joint venture and the joint venture. [IFRS 1.D16, 
IFRS 1.D17]. In the discussion that follows ‘parent’ includes an investor in an associate or a venturer in a joint 
venture, and ‘subsidiary’ includes an associate or a joint venture. References to consolidation adjustments 
include similar adjustments made when applying equity accounting. IFRS 1 also addresses the requirements 
for a parent that adopts IFRS accounting standards at different dates for the purposes of its consolidated 
and its separate financial statements (see section 5.8.4 below). 
5.8.1 
Subsidiary becomes a first-time adopter later than its parent 
Extract from IFRS 1 
D16 
If a subsidiary becomes a first-time adopter later than its parent, the subsidiary shall, in its 
financial statements, measure its assets and liabilities at either: 
(a) 
the carrying amounts that would be included in the parent’s consolidated financial 
statements, based on the parent’s date of transition to IFRSs, if no adjustments were 
made for consolidation procedures and for the effects of the business combination in 
which the parent acquired the subsidiary (this election is not available to a subsidiary of 
an investment entity, as defined in IFRS 10, that is required to be measured at fair value 
through profit or loss); or 
(b) 
the carrying amounts required by the rest of this IFRS, based on the subsidiary’s date of 
transition to IFRSs. These carrying amounts could differ from those described in (a): 
(i) 
when the exemptions in this IFRS result in measurements that depend on the date 
of transition to IFRSs. 
(ii) 
when the accounting policies used in the subsidiary’s financial statements differ 
from those in the consolidated financial statements. For example, the subsidiary 
may use as its accounting policy the cost model in IAS 16 Property, Plant and 
Equipment, whereas the group may use the revaluation model. 
A similar election is available to an associate or joint venture that becomes a first-time adopter 
later than an entity that has significant influence or joint control over it. 


International GAAP® 2025 
Chapter 06 
First-time adoption 
378 
A subsidiary that becomes a first-time adopter later than its parent therefore has the option to measure its 
assets and liabilities at the carrying amounts included in the parent’s consolidated financial statements if no 
adjustments were made for consolidation procedures and for the effects of the business combination in 
which the parent acquired the subsidiary. [IFRS 1.D16(a)]. The Board provided this exemption so that a 
subsidiary would not have to keep two parallel sets of accounting records based on different dates of 
transition to IFRS accounting standards. [IFRS 1.BC55A]. If a subsidiary does not take advantage of the 
exemption, it applies IFRS 1, based on its own date of transition to IFRS accounting standards. 
[IFRS 1.D16(b)]. A subsidiary applying IFRS 1.D16(b) would prepare its own IFRS financial statements, 
completely ignoring the IFRS 1 elections that its parent used when it adopted IFRS accounting standards for 
its consolidated financial statements. 
How we see it 
IFRS 1 does not elaborate on exactly what constitutes ‘consolidation procedures’, for the purposes of 
IFRS 1.D16(a) but, in our view, it would encompass adjustments required in order to harmonise a 
subsidiary’s accounting policies with those of the group, as well as purely ‘mechanical’ consolidation 
adjustments such as the elimination of intragroup balances, profits and losses. 
Illustration 5-22 below, which is based on the implementation guidance in IFRS 1, demonstrates how an 
entity applies these requirements. [IFRS 1.IG Example 8]. 
Illustration 5-22: 
Parent adopts IFRS accounting standards before subsidiary 
Entity A presents its (consolidated) first IFRS financial statements in 20X0. Its foreign subsidiary B, wholly 
owned by Entity A since formation, prepares information under IFRS accounting standards for internal 
consolidation purposes from that date, but Subsidiary B will not present its first IFRS financial statements 
until 20X3. 
If Subsidiary B applies IFRS 1.D16(a), the carrying amounts of its assets and liabilities are the same in both its 
opening IFRS statement of financial position at 1 January 20X2 and Entity A’s consolidated statement of 
financial position (except for adjustments for consolidation procedures) and are based on Entity A’s date of 
transition to IFRS accounting standards. 
Alternatively, Subsidiary B is allowed to apply IFRS 1.D16(b) and measure all its assets or liabilities based on 
its own date of transition to IFRS accounting standards (1 January 20X2). However, the fact that Subsidiary B 
becomes a first-time adopter in 20X3 does not change the carrying amounts of its assets and liabilities in 
Entity A’s consolidated financial statements. 
Under IFRS 1.D16(a), the numbers in a subsidiary’s IFRS financial statements will be as close to those used 
by its parent as possible. However, differences other than those arising from business combinations will still 
exist in many cases, for example: 
• 
A subsidiary might have hedged an exposure by entering into a transaction with a fellow subsidiary. 
Such a transaction could qualify for hedge accounting in the subsidiary’s own financial statements but 
not in the group’s consolidated financial statements. 
• 
A pension plan might have to be classified as a defined contribution plan from the subsidiary’s point of 
view, but is accounted for as a defined benefit plan in the group’s consolidated financial statements. 
The IASB seems content with the fact that the exemption will ease some practical problems, [IFRS 1.BC62], 
though it will rarely succeed in achieving more than a moderate reduction of the number of reconciling 
differences between a subsidiary’s own reporting and the numbers used by its parent. 
How we see it 
Importantly, the choice of IFRS 1.D16(a) prevents the subsidiary from electing to apply any of the other 
voluntary exemptions offered by IFRS 1, since the parent had already made the choices for the group at 
its date of transition. Therefore, use of IFRS 1.D16(a) might not be appropriate for a subsidiary that 
prefers to use a different exemption (e.g., fair value as deemed cost) for property, plant and equipment 
due, for example, to a tax reporting advantage. However, in our view, application of IFRS 1.D16(a) would 
allow the subsidiary to adopt a new IFRS accounting standard that becomes effective during any of the 
periods presented in the same manner it was adopted by the parent. 


International GAAP® 2025 
Chapter 06 
First-time adoption 
379 
Illustration 5-23 below explains the interaction between IFRS 1.D16(a) and the business combination 
exemptions in Appendix C of IFRS 1 applied by its parent. 
Illustration 5-23: 
Interaction between IFRS 1.D16(a) and the business combination exemptions 
applied by its parent 
Entity D has been a subsidiary of Entity C since 20X0. Entity E was acquired by Entity D in 20X2 and Entity D 
accounted for the acquisition under its previous GAAP. Entity C transitioned from previous GAAP to IFRS 
accounting standards with a date of transition of 1 January 20X3 and decided to apply the business 
combination exemption in Appendix C of IFRS 1 to this acquisition. Entity D adopted IFRS accounting standards 
from its date of transition of 1 January 20X6 and chose to apply IFRS 1.D16(a). 
Can Entity D apply IFRS 3 retrospectively to the acquisition of Entity E in its first IFRS financial statements? 
How we see it 
In our view, Entity D cannot apply IFRS 3 to the acquisition of Entity E because, applying 
IFRS 1.D16(a), a subsidiary which becomes a first-time adopter later than its parent measures its 
assets and liabilities at the carrying amounts that would be included in the parent’s consolidated 
financial statements, based on the parent’s date of transition to IFRS accounting standards. Therefore, 
Entity D cannot apply IFRS 3 retrospectively, meeting the primary objective of using the IFRS 1.D16(a) 
election in keeping one set of books. 
  
A subsidiary might become a first-time adopter later than its parent, because it previously prepared a 
reporting package under IFRS accounting standards for consolidation purposes but did not present a full set 
of financial statements under IFRS accounting standards. In such circumstances, IFRS 1.D16 still applies. 
The above election of IFRS 1.D16(a) could be relevant not only when a subsidiary’s reporting package 
complies fully with the recognition and measurement requirements of IFRS accounting standards, but also 
when it is adjusted centrally for matters such as review of events after the reporting period and central 
allocation of pension costs. Adjustments made centrally to an unpublished reporting package are not 
considered to be corrections of errors for the purposes of the disclosure requirements in IFRS 1. [IFRS 1.26]. 
However, a subsidiary is not permitted to ignore misstatements that are immaterial to the consolidated 
financial statements of the group but material to its own financial statements. [IFRS 1.IG31]. 
How we see it 
If a subsidiary was acquired after the parent’s date of transition to IFRS accounting standards then it 
cannot apply IFRS 1.D16(a) because there are no carrying amounts included in the parent’s 
consolidated financial statements, based on the parent’s date of transition. Therefore, the subsidiary is 
unable to use the values recognised in the parent’s consolidated financial statements when it was 
acquired, since push-down of the group’s purchase accounting values is not allowed in the subsidiary’s 
financial statements. However, if the subsidiary had recognised those amounts in its previous GAAP 
financial statements, it could use the same amounts as deemed costs under IFRS accounting standards 
pursuant to the ‘event-driven’ deemed cost exemption (see section 5.4.2 above). 
Implementation difficulties in applying IFRS 1.D16(a) might arise if the parent and its subsidiary have 
different financial years, as explained below. 
How we see it 
Application of IFRS 1.D16(a) is more difficult when a parent and its subsidiary have different financial 
years. In that case, IFRS 1 would seem to require the carrying amounts of the subsidiary’s assets and 
liabilities under IFRS accounting standards to be based on the parent’s date of transition to IFRS 
accounting standards, which might not even coincide with an interim reporting date of the subsidiary; the 
same applies to any joint venture or associate. 
The exemption is also available to associates and joint ventures. This means that in many cases an 
associate or joint venture that wants to apply IFRS 1.D16(a) will need to choose which shareholder it 
considers its ‘parent’ for IFRS 1 purposes if more than one investor/joint venturer have already applied 
IFRS accounting standards. The associate or joint venture will determine the carrying amount of its assets 
and liabilities under IFRS accounting standards by reference to that ‘parent’s’ date of transition to IFRS 
accounting standards. 
A subsidiary that adopts IFRS accounting standards after its investment entity parent is not permitted to 
use IFRS 1.D16(a) (see section 5.8.5.A below). 


International GAAP® 2025 
Chapter 06 
First-time adoption 
380 
5.8.1.A 
Cumulative translation differences 
IFRS 1.D13A permits a subsidiary that applies IFRS 1.D16(a) to measure cumulative translation differences 
for all foreign operations at the carrying amount that would be included in its parent’s consolidated 
financial statements, based on the parent’s date of transition to IFRS accounting standards (if no 
adjustments were made for consolidation procedures and for the effects of the business combination in 
which the parent acquired the subsidiary). A similar election is available to an associate or joint venture that 
uses the exemption in IFRS 1.D16(a). [IFRS 1.D13A, IFRS 1.D16(a)]. The subsidiary still has the option to apply 
IAS 21 retrospectively or apply IFRS 1.D13 and deem the cumulative translation differences for all foreign 
operations to be zero at its own date of transition to IFRS accounting standards (see section 5.6 above). 
[IFRS 1.D12, IFRS 1.D13, IFRS 1.D13A, IFRS 1.BC55C]. 
5.8.2 
Parent becomes a first-time adopter later than its subsidiary 
Paragraph D17 of IFRS 1 addresses the situation where a parent becomes a first-time adopter later than its 
subsidiary. [IFRS 1.D17]. 
Extract from IFRS 1 
D17 
However, if an entity becomes a first-time adopter later than its subsidiary (or associate or joint 
venture) the entity shall, in its consolidated financial statements, measure the assets and 
liabilities of the subsidiary (or associate or joint venture) at the same carrying amounts as in the 
financial statements of the subsidiary (or associate or joint venture), after adjusting for 
consolidation and equity accounting adjustments and for the effects of the business 
combination in which the entity acquired the subsidiary. Notwithstanding this requirement, a 
non-investment entity parent shall not apply the exception to consolidation that is used by any 
investment entity subsidiaries. Similarly, if a parent becomes a first-time adopter for its 
separate financial statements earlier or later than for its consolidated financial statements, it 
shall measure its assets and liabilities at the same amounts in both financial statements, except 
for consolidation adjustments. 
Unlike other first-time adoption exemptions, this exemption does not offer a choice between different 
accounting alternatives. [IFRS 1.D17]. In fact, while a subsidiary that adopts IFRS accounting standards later 
than its parent can choose to prepare its first IFRS financial statements by reference to its own date of 
transition to IFRS accounting standards or that of its parent, the parent itself must use the measurements 
of the assets and liabilities already used in the subsidiary’s IFRS financial statements, adjusted as 
appropriate for consolidation procedures and the effects of the business combination in which it acquired 
the subsidiary. [IFRS 1.BC63]. However, this exemption does not preclude the parent from adjusting the 
subsidiary’s assets and liabilities for a different accounting policy, e.g., cost model or revaluation model for 
accounting for property, plant and equipment. 
The case where a non-investment entity parent adopts IFRS accounting standards after its investment 
entity subsidiary is discussed in section 5.8.5.B below. 
Illustration 5-24 below, which is based on the implementation guidance in IFRS 1, demonstrates how an 
entity applies these requirements. [IFRS 1.IG Example 9]. 
Illustration 5-24: 
Subsidiary adopts IFRS accounting standards before parent 
Entity F presents its (consolidated) first IFRS financial statements in 20X3. Its foreign subsidiary G, wholly 
owned by Entity F since formation, presented its first IFRS financial statements in 20X0. Until 20X3, 
Subsidiary G prepared information for internal consolidation purposes under Entity F’s previous GAAP. 
The carrying amounts of Subsidiary G’s assets and liabilities at 1 January 20X2 (date of transition to IFRS 
accounting standards for Entity F) are the same in both Entity F’s (consolidated) opening IFRS statement of 
financial position and Subsidiary G’s own financial statements (except for adjustments for consolidation 
procedures) and are based on Subsidiary G’s date of transition to IFRS accounting standards. The fact that 
Entity F becomes a first-time adopter in 20X3 does not change those carrying amounts. [IFRS 1.D17]. 
 


International GAAP® 2025 
Chapter 06 
First-time adoption 
381 
How we see it 
When a subsidiary adopts IFRS accounting standards before its parent, this will limit the parent’s ability 
to choose first-time adoption exemptions in IFRS 1 freely as related to that subsidiary, as shown in 
Illustration 5-25 below. 
However, this does not mean that the parent’s ability to choose first-time adoption exemptions will always be 
limited. For example, a parent could still deem a subsidiary’s cumulative translation differences to be zero 
because IFRS 1 specifically states that under the option “the cumulative translation differences for all foreign 
operations are deemed to be zero at the date of transition to IFRSs” (emphasis added). For a discussion of the 
various options in respect of cumulative translation differences, see sections 5.6 and 5.8.1.A above. 
 
Illustration 5-25: 
Limited ability to choose first-time adoption exemptions 
Entity H will adopt IFRS accounting standards for the first time in 20X6 and its date of transition to IFRS accounting 
standards is 1 January 20X5. Subsidiary I adopted IFRS accounting standards in 20X1 and its date of transition to 
IFRS accounting standards was 1 January 20X0: 
(a) Subsidiary I and Entity H both account for their property, plant and equipment at historical cost under IAS 16 
Upon first-time adoption, Entity H is only allowed to adjust carrying amounts of Subsidiary I’s assets and 
liabilities for the effects of consolidation and the business combination in which Subsidiary I was acquired. Entity 
H can therefore not apply the exemption to use fair value as deemed cost for Subsidiary I’s property, plant and 
equipment as at its own date of transition to IFRS accounting standards (1 January 20X5). 
(b) Subsidiary I accounts for its property, plant and equipment at revalued amounts under IAS 16, while Entity H 
accounts for its property, plant and equipment at historical cost under IAS 16 
In this case, Entity H would not be allowed to apply the exemption to use fair value as deemed cost of Subsidiary 
I’s property, plant and equipment because IFRS 1.D17 would only permit adjustments for the effects of 
consolidation and the business combination in which Subsidiary I was acquired. Although a consolidation 
adjustment would be necessary, this would only be to adjust Subsidiary I’s revalued amounts to figures based on 
historical cost. 
(c) Subsidiary I deemed the cumulative translation difference for all its foreign subsidiaries to be zero at its date of 
transition to IFRS accounting standards (i.e., 1 January 20X0) 
When Entity H adopts IFRS accounting standards it can deem Subsidiary I’s cumulative translation differences to 
be zero at its date of transition (1 January 20X5). This is because those cumulative translation differences result 
from translation of Subsidiary I’s financial statements into Entity H’s presentation currency. 
5.8.3 
Implementation guidance on accounting for assets and liabilities of 
subsidiaries, associates and joint ventures 
The requirements of IFRS 1 for a parent and subsidiary with different dates of transition to IFRS accounting 
standards do not override the following requirements of IFRS 1: [IFRS 1.IG30] 
• 
The parent’s election to use the business combinations exemption in Appendix C discussed in 
section 5.2 above, which applies to assets and liabilities of a subsidiary acquired in a business 
combination that occurred before the parent’s date of transition to IFRS accounting standards. 
However, the rules in IFRS 1.D17, [IFRS 1.D17], summarised in section 5.8.2 above (parent adopting 
IFRS accounting standards after subsidiary) apply to assets acquired and liabilities assumed by the 
subsidiary after the business combination and still held and owned by it at the parent’s date of 
transition to IFRS accounting standards. 
• 
To apply the requirements in IFRS 1 in measuring items in the financial statements for which the 
provisions in IFRS 1.D16 and D17 are not relevant. [IFRS 1.D16, IFRS 1.D17]. For example, the use of the 
exemption in IFRS 1.D16(a) to measure assets and liabilities at the carrying amounts in the parent’s 
consolidated financial statements does not affect the use of the exemption in IFRS 1.D13 to deem the 
cumulative translation differences for all foreign operations are zero at its date of transition, discussed 
in section 5.6 above, since this is neither an asset nor a liability. However, the subsidiary also has an 
option to measure the cumulative translation differences at the carrying amount that would be 
included in its parent’s consolidated financial statements, based on the parent’s date of transition to 
IFRS accounting standards (if no adjustments were made for consolidation procedures and for the 
effects of the business combination in which the parent acquired the subsidiary). See section 5.8.1.A 
above. [IFRS 1.D12, IFRS 1.D13, IFRS 1.D13A]. 
• 
A first-time adopter must give all the disclosures required by IFRS 1 as of its own date of transition to 
IFRS accounting standards - see section 6 below. 


International GAAP® 2025 
Chapter 06 
First-time adoption 
382 
Section 6.8 below discusses the disclosures required where IFRS 19 Subsidiaries without Public 
Accountability: Disclosures is applied.6 
5.8.4 
Adoption of IFRS accounting standards on different dates in separate and 
consolidated financial statements 
Sometimes, an entity becomes a first-time adopter for its separate financial statements earlier or later than 
for its consolidated financial statements. Such a situation could arise, for example, when a parent avails 
itself of the exemption under IFRS 10.4 from preparing consolidated financial statements and prepares its 
separate financial statements under IFRS accounting standards (see Chapter 7 at section 2.2). [IFRS 10.4]. 
Subsequently, if the parent ceases to be entitled to the exemption or chooses not to use it, the parent 
would be required to apply IFRS 1 in its first IFRS consolidated financial statements. 
Another example might be that, under local law, an entity is required to prepare its consolidated financial 
statements under IFRS accounting standards, but is required (or permitted) to prepare its separate financial 
statements under local GAAP. Subsequently, the parent chooses, or is required, to prepare its separate 
financial statements under IFRS accounting standards. 
If a parent becomes a first-time adopter for its separate financial statements earlier or later than for its 
consolidated financial statements, it must measure its assets and liabilities at the same amounts in both 
financial statements, except for consolidation adjustments. [IFRS 1.D17]. 
How we see it 
The requirement is merely that the ‘same’ amounts be used in the first-time adopter’s separate and 
consolidated financial statements under IFRS accounting standards, without being explicit as to which set 
of financial statements is used as the benchmark. However, we believe that it is clear from the context 
that the IASB intends that the measurement basis used in whichever set of financial statements first 
comply with IFRS accounting standards must also be used when IFRS accounting standards are 
subsequently adopted in the other set. 
5.8.5 
Application to investment entities under IFRS 10 
IFRS 10 requires a parent that is an ‘investment entity’ as defined in the standard (see Chapter 7 at 
section 10) to account for most of its subsidiaries at fair value through profit or loss in its consolidated 
financial statements rather than through consolidation. [IFRS 10.31, IFRS 10 Appendix A]. This exception from 
normal consolidation procedures does not apply to: 
• 
A parent entity that owns a subsidiary which is an investment entity but the parent itself is not an 
investment entity [IFRS 10.33] 
• 
A parent that is an investment entity in accounting for a subsidiary that is not itself an investment 
entity and whose main purpose and activities are providing services that relate to the investment 
entity’s investment activities [IFRS 10.32] 
In IFRS 1, there are provisions relating to a parent adopting IFRS accounting standards earlier or later than 
its subsidiary. Below, we deal with situations where: 
• 
A subsidiary that is required to be measured at fair value through profit or loss adopts IFRS accounting 
standards after its parent which is an investment entity (see section 5.8.5.A below) 
• 
A parent that is not an investment entity adopts IFRS accounting standards after a subsidiary which is 
an investment entity (see section 5.8.5.B below) 
 
6 
IFRS 19 Subsidiaries without Public Accountability: Disclosures was issued by the IASB in May 2024 to simplify the preparation of 
financial statements by eligible subsidiaries. Eligible subsidiaries that elect to apply IFRS 19 are allowed to provide reduced 
disclosures while still applying the recognition, measurement and presentation requirements in other IFRS accounting standards. 
IFRS 19 is effective for annual reporting periods beginning on or after 1 January 2027, with earlier application permitted (subject 
to jurisdictional endorsement). See Chapter 4 at section 7 for further details on the requirements of IFRS 19. 


International GAAP® 2025 
Chapter 06 
First-time adoption 
383 
5.8.5.A 
Subsidiary adopts IFRS accounting standards after investment entity parent 
In this case, the subsidiary that is required to be measured at fair value through profit or loss must measure 
its assets and liabilities under the general provisions of IFRS 1, based on its own date of transition to IFRS 
accounting standards (i.e., IFRS 1.D16(b) - see section 5.8.1 above), [IFRS 1.D16(b)], rather than (as would 
generally be permitted under IFRS 1.D16(a) - see section 5.8.1 above) by reference to the carrying 
amounts of its assets and liabilities in the IFRS consolidated financial statements of its parent, which are 
based on the fair value of the investment in the subsidiary. [IFRS 1.D16(a)], This effectively prevents the 
accounting anomaly of the subsidiary measuring its net assets at the fair value of its own equity on 
transition to IFRS accounting standards. 
5.8.5.B 
Non-investment entity parent adopts IFRS accounting standards after investment 
entity subsidiary 
In this case, the parent is required to consolidate its subsidiaries and is not able to use the exception to 
consolidation in IFRS 10 (used by its investment entity subsidiaries). [IFRS 1.D17]. 
If the provisions in section 5.8.2 above were to be applied, the effect would be that the parent would bring 
any investments in subsidiaries accounted for at fair value through profit or loss by the subsidiary into the 
parent’s IFRS consolidated statement of financial position at their fair value. This would be contrary to the 
intention of the investment entities concept that such an accounting treatment is applied only by a parent 
that is itself an investment entity and IFRS 1.D17 specifically prohibits this. 
5.9 
Compound financial instruments 
IAS 32 requires compound financial instruments (e.g., many convertible bonds) to be split at inception into 
separate equity and liability components on the basis of facts and circumstances existing when the 
instrument was issued. [IAS 32.15, IAS 32.28]. If the liability component is no longer outstanding at the date of 
transition to IFRS accounting standards, a full retrospective application of IAS 32 would involve identifying 
two components, one representing the original equity component and the other (retained earnings) 
representing the cumulative interest accreted on the liability component, both of which are accounted for 
in equity (see Chapter 43 at section 6). A first-time adopter does not need to make this possibly complex 
allocation if the liability component is no longer outstanding at the date of transition to IFRS accounting 
standards. [IFRS 1.D18]. For example, in the case of a convertible bond that has been converted into equity, it 
is not necessary to make this split. 
However, if the liability component of the compound instrument is still outstanding at the date of transition to 
IFRS accounting standards then a split is required to be made (see Chapter 43 at section 6). [IFRS 1.IG35, IFRS 1.IG36]. 
5.10 Designation of previously recognised financial instruments 
The following discusses the application of the exemptions in IFRS 1.D19-D19C regarding designation of 
previously recognised financial instruments to certain financial assets and financial liabilities. 
5.10.1 
Designation of financial asset as measured at fair value through profit or loss 
IFRS 9 permits a financial asset to be designated as measured at fair value through profit or loss if the 
entity meets the criteria in IFRS 9.4.1.5 at the date the entity becomes a party to the financial instrument 
(see Chapter 44 at section 6). [IFRS 9.4.1.5]. 
IFRS 1.D19A allows a first-time adopter to designate a financial asset as measured at fair value through 
profit or loss in accordance with IFRS 9.4.1.5 on the basis of the facts and circumstances that exist at the 
date of transition to IFRS accounting standards. [IFRS 1.D19A]. If this exemption is used, IFRS 1 requires 
certain additional disclosures (see section 6.4.1 below). [IFRS 1.29]. 
IFRS 7 Financial Instruments: Disclosures also requires an entity which designates a financial asset at fair 
value through profit or loss to disclose the cumulative change in the fair value of the financial asset 
attributable to changes in the credit risk of the financial asset. [IFRS 7.9(c)]. However, IFRS 1 and IFRS 7 do 
not describe how a first-time adopter calculates the cumulative change for this disclosure, i.e., which date 
the cumulative change is calculated from, that of initial recognition or initial designation. See 
section 5.10.2 below for further discussion. 


International GAAP® 2025 
Chapter 06 
First-time adoption 
384 
5.10.2 
Designation of financial liability at fair value through profit or loss 
IFRS 9 permits a financial liability to be designated as a financial liability at fair value through profit or loss 
if the entity meets the criteria in IFRS 9.4.2.2 at the date the entity becomes a party to the financial 
instrument (see Chapter 44 at section 6). [IFRS 9.4.2.2]. 
IFRS 1.D19 allows a first-time adopter to designate, at the date of transition to IFRS accounting standards, 
a financial liability as measured at fair value through profit or loss provided the liability meets the criteria in 
IFRS 9.4.2.2 at that date. [IFRS 1.D19]. If this exemption is used, IFRS 1 requires certain additional 
disclosures (see section 6.4.1 below). [IFRS 1.29A]. 
IFRS 7 also requires an entity which designates a financial liability at fair value through profit or loss to 
disclose the cumulative change in the fair value attributable to changes in the credit risk of the financial 
liability. [IFRS 7.10(a), IFRS 7.10A(a)]. However, IFRS 1 and IFRS 7 do not describe how a first-time adopter 
calculates the cumulative change for this disclosure, i.e., which date the cumulative change is calculated 
from, that of initial recognition or initial designation. As noted in section 5.10.1 above, there is a similar 
disclosure required for financial assets designated at fair value through profit or loss. [IFRS 7.9(c)]. 
How we see it 
We believe that the cumulative change in fair value of a financial liability or financial asset designated at 
fair value through profit or loss is calculated from the date of initial recognition of the financial 
instrument. For a financial asset, the date of origination by the entity or purchase from another entity is 
the initial recognition date. For a financial liability, the initial recognition date is the issuance date. The 
intention of the required disclosures in IFRS 7 for the cumulative change in credit risk for those financial 
liabilities and financial assets designated at fair value through profit or loss, is to assist users of financial 
statements to understand the portion of the change in fair value attributable to changes in credit risk. 
Since the carrying amount of the instrument reflects the entire fair value movement since initial 
recognition, the corresponding disclosure is intended to reflect the cumulative movement in credit risk 
since initial recognition. 
Where a financial liability is designated at fair value through profit or loss, the first-time adopter is also 
required to determine whether the accounting required by IFRS 9.5.7.7 would create or enlarge an 
accounting mismatch in profit or loss on the basis of the facts and circumstances that exist at the date of 
transition to IFRS accounting standards (see section 5.10.4 below). [IFRS 1.D19C, IFRS 9.5.7.7, IFRS 9.5.7.8]. 
5.10.3 
Designation of investment in an equity instrument 
At initial recognition, an entity might make an irrevocable election to designate an investment in an equity 
instrument (that is neither held for trading nor contingent consideration recognised by an acquirer in a 
business combination to which IFRS 3 applies) as at fair value through other comprehensive income in 
accordance with IFRS 9 (see Chapter 44 at section 7). [IFRS 9.5.7.5]. 
IFRS 1.D19B allows a first-time adopter to designate an investment in such an equity instrument as at fair 
value through other comprehensive income in accordance with IFRS 9.5.7.5 on the basis of the facts and 
circumstances that exist at the date of transition to IFRS accounting standards. [IFRS 1.D19B]. 
5.10.4 
Determination of an accounting mismatch for presenting a gain or loss on 
financial liability 
IFRS 9 requires a gain or loss on a financial liability that is designated as at fair value through profit or loss 
to be presented as follows (unless this presentation creates or enlarges an accounting mismatch in profit or 
loss): [IFRS 9.5.7.7, IFRS 9.5.7.8] 
• 
The amount of change in the fair value of the financial liability that is attributable to changes in the 
credit risk of that liability must be presented in other comprehensive income. 
• 
The remaining amount of change in the fair value of the liability must be presented in profit or loss. 
IFRS 1.D19C requires a first-time adopter to determine whether the treatment in IFRS 9.5.7.7 would create 
or enlarge an accounting mismatch in profit or loss on the basis of the facts and circumstances that exist at 
the date of transition to IFRS accounting standards (see Chapter 44 at section 2 and Chapter 46 at 
section 2.4). [IFRS 1.D19C]. 


International GAAP® 2025 
Chapter 06 
First-time adoption 
385 
5.11 Fair value measurement of financial assets or financial liabilities at 
initial recognition 
First-time adopters are granted similar transition relief in respect of the ‘day 1’ gain or loss 
recognition requirements of IFRS 9 as is available to existing IFRS reporters in response to concerns 
raised by constituents that retrospective application of the requirements would be difficult and 
expensive to implement, and might require subjective assumptions about what was observable and 
what was not. [IFRS 1.BC83A]. 
Consequently, first-time adopters may apply the requirements of IFRS 9.B5.1.2A(b) about a deferral of the 
‘day 1’ gain/loss prospectively to transactions entered into on or after the date of transition to IFRS 
accounting standards (see Chapter 45 at section 3.3). [IFRS 1.D20, IFRS 9.B5.1.2A(b)]. 
5.12 Decommissioning liabilities included in the cost of property, plant 
and equipment 
5.12.1 
IFRIC 1 exemption 
Under IAS 16, the cost of an item of property, plant and equipment includes “the initial estimate of the 
costs of dismantling and removing the item and restoring the site on which it is located, the obligation for 
which an entity incurs either when the item is acquired or as a consequence of having used the item during 
a particular period for purposes other than to produce inventories during that period.” [IAS 16.16(c)]. 
Therefore, a first-time adopter needs to ensure that property, plant and equipment cost includes an item 
representing the decommissioning provision as determined under IAS 37. IAS 37 requires the liability, both 
initially and subsequently, to be measured at the amount required to settle the present obligation at the 
end of the reporting period, reflecting a current market-based discount rate. [IAS 37.45, IAS 37.47, IFRS 1.IG201]. 
An entity applies IAS 16 in determining the amount to be included in the cost of the asset, before 
recognising depreciation and impairment losses which cause differences between the carrying amount of 
the decommissioning liability and the amount related to decommissioning costs to be included in the 
carrying amount of the asset. [IFRS 1.IG13]. 
An entity accounts for changes in decommissioning provisions in accordance with IFRIC 1 which requires 
that, subject to specified conditions, changes in an existing decommissioning, restoration or similar liability 
are added to or deducted from the cost of the related asset. The resulting depreciable amount of the asset 
is depreciated over its useful life, and the periodic unwinding of the discount on the liability is recognised in 
profit or loss as it occurs. [IFRS 1.IG202]. However, IFRS 1.D21 provides an exemption for changes in such 
liabilities that occurred before the date of transition to IFRS accounting standards and prescribes an 
alternative treatment if the exemption is used. [IFRS 1.D21, IFRS 1.IG203, IFRIC 1]. 
In such cases, a first-time adopter must: [IFRS 1.D21] 
(a) Measure the decommissioning liability as at the date of transition to IFRS accounting standards in 
accordance with IAS 37 
(b) To the extent that the liability is within the scope of IFRIC 1, estimate the amount that would have been 
included in the cost of the related asset when the liability first arose, by discounting the liability to that 
date using its best estimate of the historical risk-adjusted discount rate(s) that would have applied for 
that liability over the intervening period 
(c) Calculate the accumulated depreciation on that amount, as at the date of transition to IFRS accounting 
standards, on the basis of the current estimate of the useful life of the asset, using the entity’s 
IFRS depreciation policy 
Illustration 5-26 below demonstrates the determination of the amount of decommissioning liability and the 
property, plant and equipment related to that decommissioning liability, when using this exemption.

As can be seen above, a full retrospective application of IFRIC 1 would require an entity to go back in time 
and account for each revision of the decommissioning provision in accordance with IFRIC 1. In the case of a 
long-lived asset there could be a significant number of revisions that a first-time adopter would need to 
account for. It can be noted that despite the significant revision of the decommissioning costs, the impact 
on the carrying amount of the factory in many cases might not be significant. 
At its date of transition to IFRS accounting standards (1 January 20X5), Entity A makes the following 
adjustments: 
• 
The decommissioning liability is increased by CU56 (i.e., CU131 - CU75) to reflect the difference in 
accounting policy, irrespective of whether Entity A applies the exemption or not. 
• 
If Entity A applies the exemption, this increases the carrying amount of the factory by CU75. 
• 
If Entity A applies IFRIC 1 retrospectively, this increases the carrying amount of the factory by CU82. 
It is important to note that in both cases the decommissioning component of the factory’s carrying amount will 
be significantly lower than the decommissioning liability itself. 
From the above example, it is clear that the exemption reduces the amount of effort required to restate 
items of property, plant and equipment with a decommissioning component. In many cases the difference 
between the two methods will be insignificant, except where an entity had to make major adjustments to 
the estimate of the decommissioning costs near the end of the life of the related assets. 
It is important for a first-time adopter that elects the deemed cost approaches discussed in section 5.4 above 
and elects to use the IFRIC 1 exemption to recognise its decommissioning obligation to be aware of the 
interaction between these exemptions that could lead to a potential overstatement of the underlying asset. 
How we see it 
In determining the deemed cost of the asset equal to fair value, the first-time adopter would need to 
make sure that the fair value of the asset has taken the decommissioning into account to avoid the 
potential overstatement of the value of the asset that might result from the application of the IFRIC 1 
exemption. 


International GAAP® 2025 
Chapter 06 
First-time adoption 
388 
5.12.2 
IFRIC 1 exemption for oil and gas assets at deemed cost 
A first-time adopter that applies the deemed cost exemption for oil and gas assets in the development or 
production phases accounted for in cost centres that include all properties in a large geographical area 
under previous GAAP (see section 5.4.3 above) must not apply the IFRIC 1 exemption (see section 5.12.1 
above) or IFRIC 1 itself, but instead is required to: [IFRS 1.D8A(b), IFRS 1.D21A] 
(a) Measure decommissioning, restoration, and similar liabilities as at the date of transition to IFRS 
accounting standards in accordance with IAS 37 
(b) Recognise directly in retained earnings any difference between that amount and the carrying amount 
of those liabilities at the date of transition determined under previous GAAP 
The IASB introduced this requirement because it believed that the existing IFRIC 1 exemption would require 
detailed calculations that would not be practicable for entities that apply the deemed cost exemption for oil 
and gas assets. [IFRS 1.BC63CA]. 
5.13 Financial assets or intangible assets accounted for in accordance 
with IFRIC 12 
Service concession arrangements are contracts between the public and private sector to attract private 
sector participation in the development, financing, operation and maintenance of public infrastructure 
(e.g., roads, bridges, tunnels, prisons, hospitals, airports, water distribution facilities, energy supply and 
telecommunication networks). [IFRIC 12.1, IFRIC 12.2]. 
IFRS 1 allows a first-time adopter to apply the transitional provision in IFRIC 12. [IFRS 1.D22]. IFRIC 12 
requires retrospective application unless it is, for any particular service concession arrangement, 
impracticable for the operator to apply IFRIC 12 retrospectively at the start of the earliest period 
presented, in which case it must: [IFRIC 12.29, IFRIC 12.30] 
(a) Recognise financial assets and intangible assets that existed at the start of the earliest period 
presented, which will be the date of transition to IFRS accounting standards for a first-time adopter 
(b) Use the previous carrying amounts of those financial and intangible assets (however previously 
classified) as their carrying amounts as at that date 
(c) Test financial and intangible assets recognised at that date for impairment, unless this is not 
practicable, in which case the amounts must be tested for impairment at the start of the current 
period, which will be the beginning of the first IFRS reporting period for a first-time adopter 
This exemption was used by many Brazilian companies with service concession arrangements and a 
disclosure of the use of the exemption is given in Practical example 5-9 below from the financial statements 
of Centrais Elétricas Brasileiras S.A. - Eletrobras: 
Practical example 5-9: 
Centrais Elétricas Brasileiras S.A. - Eletrobras

5.14 Borrowing costs 
5.14.1 
Borrowing cost exemption 
For many first-time adopters, full retrospective application of IAS 23 Borrowing Costs would be problematic 
as the adjustment would be required in respect of any asset held that had, at any point in the past, satisfied 
the criteria for capitalisation of borrowing costs. To avoid this problem, IFRS 1 allows a modified form of 
the transitional provisions set out in IAS 23, which means that the first-time adopter can elect to apply the 
requirements of IAS 23 from the date of transition to IFRS accounting standards or from an earlier date as 
permitted by IAS 23.28. [IFRS 1.D23, IAS 23.28]. 
Extract from IFRS 1 
D23 
A first-time adopter can elect to apply the requirements of IAS 23 from the date of transition or 
from an earlier date as permitted by paragraph 28 of IAS 23. From the date on which an entity 
that applies this exemption begins to apply IAS 23, the entity: 
(a) 
shall not restate the borrowing cost component that was capitalised under previous GAAP 
and that was included in the carrying amount of assets at that date; and 
(b) 
shall account for borrowing costs incurred on or after that date in accordance with 
IAS 23, including those borrowing costs incurred on or after that date on qualifying 
assets already under construction. 
IAS 23 applies to borrowing costs that are directly attributable to the acquisition, construction or 
production of a qualifying asset, as defined in IAS 23. [IAS 23.1, IAS 23.5]. If a first-time adopter established a 
deemed cost for an asset (see section 5.4 above) then it cannot capitalise borrowing costs incurred before 
the measurement date of the deemed cost (see section 5.14.2 below). [IFRS 1.IG23]. IAS 23 requires 
disclosure of interest capitalised during the period. Neither IAS 23 nor IFRS 1 requires disclosure of the 
cumulative amount capitalised. [IFRS 1.IG24, IAS 23.26]. 
5.14.2 
Interaction with other exemptions 
There are limitations imposed on capitalised amounts under IAS 23. IAS 23 states that when the carrying 
amount of a qualifying asset exceeds its recoverable amount or net realisable value, the carrying amount is 
written down or written off in accordance with the requirement of other standards. [IAS 23.16]. 
How we see it 
Once an entity uses the ‘fair value as deemed cost exemption’ described in section 5.4 above and has 
recognised an asset at fair value, in our view, the entity does not increase that value to capitalise interest 
incurred before the fair value measurement date. Interest incurred subsequent to the date of transition 
could be capitalised on a qualifying asset, subject to the requirements of IAS 23 (see Chapter 22). 
5.15 Extinguishing financial liabilities with equity instruments 
IFRIC 19 Extinguishing Financial Liabilities with Equity Instruments deals with accounting for transactions 
whereby a debtor and creditor might renegotiate the terms of a financial liability with the result that the debtor 
extinguishes the liability fully or partially by issuing equity instruments to the creditor (see Chapter 43 at 
section 7). [IFRIC 19.2]. However, an entity does not apply IFRIC 19 to transactions in situations where: [IFRIC 19.3] 
(a) The creditor is also a direct or indirect shareholder and is acting in its capacity as a direct or indirect 
existing shareholder 
(b) The creditor and the entity are controlled by the same party or parties before and after the transaction 
and the substance of the transaction includes an equity distribution by, or contribution to, the entity 
Or 
(c) Extinguishing the financial liability by issuing equity shares is in accordance with the original terms of 
the financial liability 
The transitional provisions of IFRIC 19 require retrospective application only from the beginning of the 
earliest comparative period presented. [IFRIC 19.13]. The IFRS IC concluded that application to earlier periods 
would result only in a reclassification of amounts within equity. [IFRIC 19.BC33]. 
The Board provided similar transition relief to first-time adopters, effectively requiring application of 
IFRIC 19 from the date of transition to IFRS accounting standards. [IFRS 1.D25]. 


International GAAP® 2025 
Chapter 06 
First-time adoption 
390 
5.16 Severe hyperinflation 
Extract from IFRS 1 
D26 
If an entity has a functional currency that was, or is, the currency of a hyperinflationary 
economy, it shall determine whether it was subject to severe hyperinflation before the date of 
transition to IFRSs. This applies to entities that are adopting IFRSs for the first time, as well as 
entities that have previously applied IFRSs. 
If an entity has a functional currency that was, or is, the currency of a hyperinflationary economy, it must 
determine whether it was subject to severe hyperinflation before the date of transition to IFRS accounting 
standards. [IFRS 1.D26]. A currency of a hyperinflationary economy is subject to severe hyperinflation if it 
has both of the following characteristics: [IFRS 1.D27] 
(a) A reliable general price index is not available to all entities with transactions and balances in the currency 
(b) The currency is not exchangeable into a relatively stable foreign currency. Exchangeability is assessed 
in accordance with IAS 21. 
The functional currency of an entity ceases to be subject to severe hyperinflation on the ‘functional 
currency normalisation date’, when the functional currency no longer has either, or both, of these 
characteristics, or when there is a change in the entity’s functional currency to a currency that is not 
subject to severe hyperinflation. [IFRS 1.D28]. 
If the date of transition to IFRS accounting standards is on, or after, the functional currency normalisation 
date, the first-time adopter is allowed to elect to measure all assets and liabilities held before the functional 
currency normalisation date at fair value on the date of transition to IFRS accounting standards and use 
that fair value as the deemed cost in the opening IFRS statement of financial position. [IFRS 1.D29]. 
Preparation of information in accordance with IFRS accounting standards for periods before the functional 
currency normalisation date might not be possible. Therefore, entities are allowed to prepare financial 
statements for a comparative period of less than 12 months if the functional currency normalisation date 
falls within a 12-month comparative period, provided that a complete set of financial statements is 
prepared, as required by IAS 1.10, for that shorter period. [IFRS 1.D30, IAS 1.10]. The Board suggested that 
entities consider disclosure of non-IFRS comparative information and historical summaries if they would 
provide useful information to users of financial statements (see section 6.2 below). [IFRS 1.22]. The Board 
further noted that an entity should clearly explain the transition to IFRS accounting standards in 
accordance with IFRS 1’s disclosure requirements in IFRS 1.23-28. [IFRS 1.BC63J, IFRS 1.23-28]. 
If an entity elects to measure assets and liabilities at fair value and to use that fair value as the deemed cost 
in its opening IFRS statement of financial position because of severe hyperinflation, the entity must disclose 
an explanation of how, and why, the entity had, and then ceased to have, a functional currency that is 
subject to severe hyperinflation (see section 6.5.5 below). [IFRS 1.D26, IFRS 1.D27, IFRS 1.D28, IFRS 1.D29, 
IFRS 1.D30, IFRS 1.31C]. 
In August 2023, the IASB issued Lack of Exchangeability: Amendments to IAS 21 (‘the IAS 21 
amendments’) which set out the approach an entity must apply when assessing whether a currency can be 
exchanged into another currency, and the exchange rate to use when this is not the case. The amendments 
also changed IFRS 1.D27(b) and IFRS 1.31C and the new requirements are reflected above. The IAS 21 
amendments (including the consequential amendments to IFRS 1) must be applied for annual reporting 
periods beginning on or after 1 January 2025 with earlier application permitted. 
See Chapter 17 regarding accounting during periods of hyperinflation and Chapter 16 at section 7 for 
assessment of exchangeability. 
5.17 Joint arrangements 
A first-time adopter is allowed to apply the transitional provisions in Appendix C of IFRS 11 Joint 
Arrangements with the following exceptions: [IFRS 1.D31, IFRS 11 Appendix C] 
(a) A first-time adopter must apply these transitional provisions at the date of transition to IFRS 
accounting standards. 
(b) When changing from proportionate consolidation to the equity method, a first-time adopter must test 
the investment for impairment in accordance with IAS 36 as at the date of transition to IFRS 
accounting standards, regardless of whether there is any indication that it might be impaired. Any 
resulting impairment must be recognised as an adjustment to retained earnings at the date of 
transition to IFRS accounting standards.
