‡ 
A first-time adopter that ceases to publish financial statements under previous GAAP is not required to present 
reconciliations of equity and total comprehensive income at the end of the first IFRS reporting period. However, a first-time 
adopter that continues to publish previous GAAP financial statements might need to reconcile the equity and total 
comprehensive income and explain material cash flow adjustments as at the end of, and for, the first IFRS financial 
reporting period. See section 6.3.1.A below for further discussion. 


International GAAP® 2025 
Chapter 06 
First-time adoption 
414 
First-time adopters do not apply the requirements of IAS 8 relating to the changes in accounting policies in 
their first IFRS financial statements. This is because that standard does not apply to the changes in 
accounting policies an entity makes when it adopts IFRS accounting standards or changes in those policies 
until after it presents its first IFRS financial statements (see section 7.2 below). [IFRS 1.27]. 
A first-time adopter must explain changes in accounting policies or in its use of exemptions during the 
period between its first IFRS interim financial report and its first IFRS annual financial statements (see 
section 6.6 below) and update the reconciliations of equity and total comprehensive income discussed 
above. [IFRS 1.27A]. This requirement is necessary in order to explain the transition to IFRS accounting 
standards, [IFRS 1.23], since the first-time adopter is exempt from the requirements of IAS 8 concerning 
reporting of such changes. [IFRS 1.BC97]. 
6.3.1.A 
Reconciliation by a first-time adopter that continues to publish previous GAAP 
financial statements 
A first-time adopter that continues to publish financial statements under previous GAAP after adopting IFRS 
accounting standards must consider carefully the starting point of the reconciliations required by IFRS 1 in 
the first IFRS financial statements because the requirements to produce reconciliations do not consider this 
situation. IFRS 1.24 results in different applications depending on the timing of issuance of the previous 
GAAP financial statements in relation to the first-time adopter’s first IFRS reporting period. 
How we see it 
We believe a first-time adopter, that continues to publish previous GAAP financial statements and has 
already issued those financial statements for the first IFRS reporting period prior to the issuance of the 
first IFRS financial statements, has a choice of presenting reconciliations of equity and total 
comprehensive income: 
(a) Respectively as at the end of, and for, the first IFRS reporting period 
(b) Respectively as at the end of, and for, both the first IFRS reporting period, and the comparative period 
Or 
(c) Respectively as at the end of, and for, the comparative period 
In each case above, the reconciliation of equity as at the date of transition to IFRS accounting standards 
must be disclosed. 
However, if the first IFRS financial statements were issued prior to the previous GAAP financial statements 
for the period, then IFRS 1 can only mean the previous GAAP financial statements for the immediately 
preceding year.

6.3.3 
Recognition and reversal of impairments 
If a first-time adopter recognised or reversed any impairment losses on transition to IFRS accounting 
standards it must disclose the information that IAS 36 would have required if the entity had recognised 
those impairment losses or reversals in the period beginning with the date of transition to IFRS accounting 
standards (see Chapter 21 at section 13). [IFRS 1.24(c)]. This provides transparency about impairment losses 
recognised on transition that might otherwise receive less attention than impairment losses recognised in 
earlier or later periods. [IFRS 1.BC94]. 


International GAAP® 2025 
Chapter 06 
First-time adoption 
423 
6.3.4 
Inclusion of IFRS 1 reconciliations by cross reference 
The reconciliation disclosures required by IFRS 1 are generally quite lengthy. While IFRS 1 allows an entity’s 
first interim report under IAS 34 to give certain of these disclosures by way of cross-reference to another 
published document, [IFRS 1.32(b), IFRS 1.33], there is no corresponding exemption for disclosure in the 
entity’s first annual IFRS financial statements. Therefore, a first-time adopter must include all disclosures 
required by IFRS 1 within its first annual IFRS financial statements in the same way it would need to include 
other lengthy disclosures such as those on business combinations, financial instruments and employee 
benefits. Any additional voluntary information regarding the conversion to IFRS accounting standards that 
was previously published but that is not specifically required by IFRS 1 need not be repeated in the first 
IFRS financial statements. 
6.4 
Disclosure of financial instruments 
6.4.1 
Designation of financial instruments 
If, on transition, a first-time adopter designates a previously recognised financial asset or financial liability 
as a ‘financial asset or financial liability at fair value through profit or loss’ (see sections 5.10.1 and 5.10.2 
above), it must disclose: [IFRS 1.29, IFRS 1.29A, IFRS 1.D19, IFRS 1.D19A] 
(a) The fair value of financial assets and the fair value of financial liabilities so designated at the date 
of designation 
(b) Their classification and carrying amounts in the previous GAAP financial statements 
6.4.2 
Classification of financial instruments 
An entity must disclose the carrying amount at the reporting date of the financial assets - whose contractual 
cash flow characteristics have been assessed based on the facts and circumstances that existed at the date of 
transition to IFRS accounting standards without taking into account the requirements related to the 
modification of the time value of money element in IFRS 9.B4.1.9B-B4.1.9D - until those financial assets are 
derecognised. [IFRS 1.B8A, IFRS 7.42R, IFRS 9.B4.1.9B, IFRS 9.B4.1.9C, IFRS 9.B4.1.9D]. See section 4.9.1.A above. 
An entity must also disclose the carrying amount at the reporting date of the financial assets - whose 
contractual cash flow characteristics have been assessed based on the facts and circumstances that existed 
at the date of transition to IFRS accounting standards without taking into account the exception for 
prepayment features in IFRS 9.B4.1.12 - until those financial assets are derecognised. [IFRS 1.B8B, IFRS 7.42S, 
IFRS 9.B4.1.12]. See section 4.9.1.B above. 
6.5 
Disclosures regarding deemed cost 
6.5.1 
Use of fair value as deemed cost 
If a first-time adopter uses fair value as deemed cost for any item of property, plant and equipment, 
investment property, right-of-use asset or an intangible asset in its opening IFRS statement of financial 
position (see section 5.4.1 above), it must disclose for each line item in the opening IFRS statement of 
financial position: [IFRS 1.30, IFRS 1.D5, IFRS 1.D7] 
(a) The aggregate of those fair values 
(b) The aggregate adjustment to the carrying amounts reported under previous GAAP 
This disclosure is illustrated in Practical example 5-4 in section 5.4.1 above. 
6.5.2 
Use of deemed cost for investments in subsidiaries, joint ventures and 
associates in separate financial statements 
If a first-time adopter uses deemed cost in its opening IFRS statement of financial position for an 
investment in a subsidiary, joint venture or associate in its separate financial statements (see section 5.7.2 
above), the entity’s first IFRS separate financial statements must disclose: [IFRS 1.31, IFRS 1.D15] 
(a) The aggregate deemed cost of those investments for which deemed cost is their previous GAAP 
carrying amount 
(b) The aggregate deemed cost of those investments for which deemed cost is fair value 
(c) The aggregate adjustment to the carrying amounts reported under previous GAAP 


International GAAP® 2025 
Chapter 06 
First-time adoption 
424 
6.5.3 
Use of deemed cost for oil and gas assets 
If a first-time adopter uses the deemed cost exemption in IFRS 1.D8A(b) for oil and gas assets in the 
development or production phases (see section 5.4.3 above), it must disclose that fact and the basis on 
which carrying amounts determined under previous GAAP were allocated. [IFRS 1.31A, IFRS 1.D8A(b)]. 
6.5.4 
Use of deemed cost for assets used (or previously used) in operations subject 
to rate regulation 
If a first-time adopter uses the exemption for assets used (or previously used) in operations subject to rate 
regulation (see section 5.4.4 above), it must disclose that fact and the basis on which carrying amounts 
were determined under previous GAAP. [IFRS 1.31B, IFRS 1.D8B]. 
6.5.5 
Use of deemed cost after severe hyperinflation 
If a first-time adopter uses the exemption to elect fair value as the deemed cost in its opening 
IFRS statement of financial position for assets and liabilities because of severe hyperinflation (see 
section 5.16 above), it must disclose an explanation of how, and why, the first-time adopter had, and then 
ceased to have, a functional currency that is subject to severe hyperinflation. [IFRS 1.31C, IFRS 1.D26, 
IFRS 1.D27, IFRS 1.D28, IFRS 1.D29, IFRS 1.D30]. 
6.6 
Interim financial reports 
6.6.1 
Reconciliations in the interim financial reports 
If a first-time adopter presents an interim financial report under IAS 34 for part of the period covered by its 
first IFRS financial statements: 
(a) Each such interim financial report must, if the entity presented an interim financial report for the 
comparable interim period of the immediately preceding financial year, include: [IFRS 1.32(a)] 
(i) A reconciliation of its equity under previous GAAP at the end of that comparable interim period to 
its equity under IFRS accounting standards at that date 
(ii) A reconciliation to its total comprehensive income under IFRS accounting standards for that 
comparable interim period (current and year to date). The starting point for that reconciliation is 
total comprehensive income under previous GAAP for that period or, if an entity did not report 
such a total, profit or loss under previous GAAP 
(b) In addition, the entity’s first interim financial report under IAS 34 for part of the period covered by its 
first IFRS financial statements must include the reconciliations (supplemented by the further details) 
described in section 6.3.1 above, [IFRS 1.24(a), IFRS 1.24(b), IFRS 1.25, IFRS 1.26], or a cross-reference to 
another published document that includes these reconciliations. [IFRS 1.32(b)]. 
For an entity presenting annual financial statements under IFRS accounting standards, it is not compulsory 
to prepare interim financial reports under IAS 34. Therefore, the above requirements only apply to first-
time adopters that prepare interim reports under IAS 34 on a voluntary basis or that are required to do so 
by a regulator or other party. [IFRS 1.IG37].

covered by its first IFRS financial statements and will be applying IFRS 18 in its first IFRS financial

required by IFRS 18.69-74 (see Chapter 4 at section 3) in those interim financial statements. After the 
entity has issued its first IFRS financial statements, an entity applies the usual requirements in IAS 34.10 
for headings and subtotals in condensed financial statements (see Chapter 37 at section 3.2) after it has 
issued its first IFRS financial statements prepared in accordance with IFRS 18. [IFRS 1(2027).32(za)]. 


International GAAP® 2025 
Chapter 06 
First-time adoption 
427 
6.6.2 
Disclosures in the interim financial report 
Interim financial reports under IAS 34 contain considerably less detail than annual financial statements 
because they assume that users of the interim financial report also have access to the most recent annual 
financial statements. However, they would be expected to provide disclosure relating to material events or 
transactions to allow users to understand the current interim period. Therefore, a first-time adopter needs 
to consider what disclosures required by IFRS accounting standards are material to an understanding of the 
current interim period. If the most recent annual financial statements under previous GAAP did not disclose 
information material to an understanding of the current interim period, the interim financial report must 
disclose that information (or include a cross reference to another published document that contains that 
information). [IFRS 1.33, IAS 34.15, IAS 34.15A]. 
How we see it 
A full set of IFRS accounting policy disclosures and related significant judgements and estimates needs to 
be included in the first IFRS interim financial report as well as information on the IFRS 1 exemptions 
employed (or that report needs to contain a cross reference to another published document that includes 
this information (see Chapter 37 at section 4.2.3)). In addition, consideration needs to be given to both 
new disclosures not previously required under previous GAAP, and disclosures made under previous 
GAAP but for which the amounts contained therein have changed significantly due to changes in 
accounting policies resulting from the adoption of IFRS accounting standards. 
It is also important to note that such disclosures apply to balances in both the opening and comparative year-
end statement of financial position, each of which could be included in the first IFRS interim financial report 
(see section 6.6.1 above). First-time adopters are expected to include significantly more information in their 
first IFRS interim financial report than would normally be included in an interim financial report (alternatively, 
the report could cross refer to another published document that includes such information). [IFRS 1.33]. 
Examples of additional annual disclosures under IFRS accounting standards to be included in the entity’s 
first IAS 34 compliant interim financial report could include disclosures relating to retirement benefits, 
income taxes, goodwill and provisions, amongst other items that significantly differ from previous GAAP 
and those disclosures required under IFRS accounting standards that are more substantial than previous 
GAAP (see Chapter 37 at section 11.1). 
6.7 
Disclosure of IFRS information before adoption of IFRS accounting 
standards 
As the adoption of IFRS accounting standards might have a significant impact on their financial statements, 
many entities will want to provide information on its expected impact in advance of their first IFRS financial 
statements. There are certain difficulties that arise as a result of the application of IFRS 1 when an entity 
decides to quantify the impact of the adoption of IFRS accounting standards. In particular, IFRS 1 requires 
an entity to draw up an opening IFRS statement of financial position at its date of transition to IFRS 
accounting standards based on the standards that are effective at the end of its first IFRS reporting period. 
Therefore, it is not possible to prepare IFRS financial information - and assess the full impact of IFRS 
accounting standards - until an entity knows its date of transition to IFRS accounting standards and exactly 
which standards will be effective at the end of its first IFRS reporting period. 
How we see it 
If an entity wanted to quantify the impact of the adoption of IFRS accounting standards before its date of 
transition to IFRS accounting standards, it would not be able to do this in accordance with IFRS 1. While 
an entity would be able to select a date and apply by analogy the requirements of IFRS 1 to its previous 
GAAP financial information as of that date, it would not be able to claim that such additional information 
complied with IFRS accounting standards. Presentation of such additional information is to be avoided if 
there is a concern that the information, despite being clearly marked as not IFRS compliant, would be 
misleading or misunderstood. 
If an entity wants to quantify the impact of the adoption of IFRS accounting standards in advance of the 
release of its first IFRS financial statements but after its date of transition to IFRS accounting standards, 
there could still be some uncertainty regarding the standards that apply. If so, an entity discloses the 
nature of the uncertainty and considers describing the information as ‘preliminary’ IFRS information. 


International GAAP® 2025 
Chapter 06 
First-time adoption 
428

IFRS accounting standards in advance of its first IFRS financial statements. The Practical example complies 
with the versions of IFRS 1 and IAS 19 that were effective in 2011.

6.8 
Disclosure requirements for subsidiaries without public 
accountability (IFRS 19) 
In May 2024, the IASB issued IFRS 19 which is applicable for annual reporting periods beginning on or after 
1 January 2027. Earlier application is permitted. 
An entity can elect to apply IFRS 19 for its consolidated, separate and individual statements if, and only if, 
at the end of the reporting period: [IFRS 19.7] 
(a) It is a subsidiary 
(b) It does not have public accountability 
And 
(c) It has an ultimate or intermediate parent that produces consolidated financial statements available for 
public use that comply with IFRS accounting standards. 
An entity that elects to apply IFRS 19 in one reporting period may later revoke that election and an entity 
may elect to apply IFRS 19 more than once. [IFRS 19.13]. 
An entity whose financial statements comply with IFRS accounting standards and the requirements in 
IFRS 19 must make an explicit and unreserved statement of such compliance in the notes, specifically 
stating that IFRS 19 has been applied as part of that statement. [IFRS 19.20]. (see Chapter 4 at section 7) 


International GAAP® 2025 
Chapter 06 
First-time adoption 
432 
6.8.1 
Interaction with IFRS 1 
IFRS 19 provides no exemption or exception for recognition, measurement and presentation requirements 
in IFRS accounting standards. Therefore, the requirements of IFRS 1 (except those relating to disclosures) 
apply in full in the first IFRS financial statements or when IFRS 1 is reapplied (see section 2.2.1 above). 
[IFRS 19.2, IFRS 19.17, IFRS 1.4A]. 
It is important to note that electing or revoking an election to apply IFRS 19 does not, on its own, result in 
an entity meeting IFRS 1’s definition of a first-time adopter of IFRS accounting standards. [IFRS 19.18]. If an 
entity provided, in the immediately preceding period, a statement of compliance with both IFRS accounting 
standards and IFRS 19, it does not apply IFRS 1 for the current reporting period even if the entity revokes 
the election to apply IFRS 19 for the current reporting period. [IFRS 19.19]. Conversely, an entity that gave a 
complete and unreserved statement of compliance with IFRS accounting standards but did not apply 
IFRS 19 in the immediately preceding period, does not apply IFRS 1 for the current reporting period if it 
elects to apply IFRS 19 in the current reporting period. [IFRS 19.18]. 
See section 6.8.2 below in relation to the disclosures relating to transition to IFRS accounting standards. 
IFRS 19 is an optional standard and therefore an eligible subsidiary (see section 6.8 above) choosing not to 
apply IFRS 19 must instead apply IFRS 1’s disclosure requirements when it prepares its first IFRS financial 
statements (see section 6 above). 
6.8.2 
Disclosure requirements in IFRS 19 relating to the transition to IFRS 
accounting standards 
An eligible subsidiary who chooses to apply IFRS 19 when it prepares its first IFRS financial statements is 
required to apply the disclosure requirements in IFRS 19 rather than those in other IFRS accounting 
standards, although some disclosure requirements in other IFRS accounting standards remain applicable, 
which are specified in IFRS 19 (see further details in Chapter 4 at section 7). [IFRS 19.2, IFRS 19.4]. 
An entity that applies IFRS 19 when it prepares its first IFRS financial statements must apply the disclosure 
requirements in IFRS 19.21-30 instead of the disclosure requirements in IFRS 1.23-33. [IFRS 19.17]. A first-
time adopter applying IFRS 19 is required to make disclosures explaining the transition to IFRS accounting 
standards in its first IFRS financial statements. [IFRS 19.21]. These specifically cover requirements for: 
• 
Disclosures when an entity reapplies IFRS accounting standards [IFRS 19.22, IFRS 19.23] 
• 
Reconciliations [IFRS 19.24, IFRS 19.25, IFRS 19.26, IFRS 19.27] 
• 
Interim financial reports [IFRS 19.28, IFRS 19.29] 
These disclosures are similar to the corresponding requirements set out in IFRS 1 (see sections 2.2.1, 6.3, 
6.3.1 and 6.6.1 above). There are some simplifications compared to the corresponding disclosures required 
under IFRS 1 by a first-time adopter. For example, even if the entity recognised or reversed impairment 
losses for the first time in preparing its opening IFRS statement of financial position, it is not required to 
give the disclosures that IAS 36 would have required (see section 6.3.3 above). [IFRS 1.24(c)]. 
IFRS 19.30 further requires an entity who applies IFRS 19 to disclose specific information required by 
IFRS 2 when it applies IFRS 1.D2 (i.e., the same requirements as set out in IFRS 1.D2). [IFRS 19.30]. (see 
section 5.3 above and Chapter 30 at section 13). 


International GAAP® 2025 
Chapter 06 
First-time adoption 
433 
7 
Accounting policies and practical application issues 
The exceptions and exemptions of IFRS 1 are explained in sections 4 and 5 above, respectively. This section 
provides an overview of the detailed application guidance in IFRS 1 (to the extent that it is not covered in 
sections 4 and 5 above) and some of the practical application issues that are not directly related to any of 
the exceptions or exemptions. These issues are discussed by IFRS accounting standard as follows: 
• 
IAS 7 Statement of Cash Flows (see section 7.1 below) 
• 
IAS 8 Accounting Policies, Changes in Accounting Estimates and Errors (see section 7.2 below) 
• 
IAS 12 Income Taxes (see section 7.3 below) 
• 
IAS 16 Property, Plant and Equipment and IAS 40 Investment Property (cost model) (see section 7.4 
below) 
• 
IFRS 16 Leases (see section 7.5 below) 
• 
IFRS 15 Revenue from Contracts with Customers (see section 7.6 below) 
• 
IAS 19 Employee Benefits (see section 7.7 below) 
• 
IAS 21 The Effects of Changes in Foreign Exchange Rates (see section 7.8 below) 
• 
IAS 28 Investments in Associates and Joint Ventures (see section 7.9 below) 
• 
IAS 29 Financial Reporting in Hyperinflationary Economies (see section 7.10 below) 
• 
IFRS 11 Joint Arrangements (see section 7.11 below) 
• 
IAS 36 Impairment of Assets (see section 7.12 below) 
• 
IAS 37 Provisions, Contingent Liabilities and Contingent Assets (see section 7.13 below) 
• 
IAS 38 Intangible Assets (see section 7.14 below) 
7.1 
IAS 7 Statement of Cash Flows 
A statement of cash flows prepared under IAS 7 could differ in the following ways from the one prepared 
under an entity’s previous GAAP: 
• 
The definition of ‘cash and cash equivalents’ under IAS 7 could well differ from the one used under 
previous GAAP. [IAS 7.6, IAS 7.7, IAS 7.8]. In particular, IAS 7 includes within cash and cash equivalents 
those bank overdrafts repayable on demand which form an integral part of an entity’s cash 
management (see Chapter 36 at section 3). [IAS 7.8]. 
• 
The layout and definition of the categories of cash flows (i.e., operating, investing and financing) is 
often different from previous GAAP. [IAS 7.10]. In addition, IAS 7 contains specific requirements about 
the classification of interest, dividends and taxes (see Chapter 36 at section 4). [IAS 7.31, IAS 7.35]. 
• 
Differences in accounting policies between IFRS accounting standards and previous GAAP often have a 
consequential impact on the statement of cash flows. 
IFRS 1 requires disclosure of an explanation of the material adjustments to the statement of cash flows, if a 
first-time adopter presented one under its previous GAAP (see section 6.3.1 above). [IFRS 1.25]. Practical 
example 7-1 below illustrates how the IFRS statement of cash flows differed from that presented under 
previous GAAP. 


International GAAP® 2025 
Chapter 06 
First-time adoption 
434

Sony Group Corporation (2022) 
Japan

International GAAP® 2025 
Chapter 06 
First-time adoption 
435 
7.2 
IAS 8 Accounting Policies, Changes in Accounting Estimates and 
Errors 
Normally when an entity that is already using IFRS accounting standards changes an accounting policy, it 
applies IAS 8 to such a change. IFRS 1 requires that a first-time adopter applies the same accounting 
policies in its opening IFRS statement of financial position and throughout all periods presented in its first 
IFRS financial statements. [IFRS 1.7]. Therefore, the change in accounting policies must be treated as a 
change in the entity’s opening IFRS statement of financial position and the policy must be applied 
consistently in all periods presented in its first IFRS financial statements. 
7.2.1 
Changes in IFRS accounting policies during the first IFRS reporting period 
A first-time adopter might find that it needs to change IFRS accounting policies after it has issued an 
IFRS interim report but before issuing its first IFRS financial statements. Such a change in accounting 
policies could relate either to the ongoing IFRS accounting policies or to the selection of IFRS 1 exemptions. 
IAS 8 does not apply to the changes in accounting policies an entity makes when it adopts IFRS accounting 
standards or to changes in those policies until after it presents its first IFRS financial statements. [IFRS 1.27]. 
Therefore, “if during the period covered by its first IFRS financial statements an entity changes its 
accounting policies or its use of the exemptions contained in this IFRS”, it must explain the changes 
between its first IFRS interim financial report and its first IFRS financial statements in accordance with 
IFRS 1.23, i.e., the disclosure objective to explain the transition (see section 6.3 above) and update the 
reconciliations required by IFRS 1.24(a) and (b) (see section 6.3.1 above). [IFRS 1.23, IFRS 1.24(a), IFRS 1.24(b), 
IFRS 1.27A]. A similar requirement applies to the disclosures in a first-time adopter’s interim financial reports 
(see section 6.6 above). [IFRS 1.23, IFRS 1.32(c)]. 
The distinction between changes in accounting policies and changes in accounting estimates is discussed in 
detail in Chapter 3 at section 4.2. 
7.2.2 
Changes in estimates and correction of errors 
An entity that adopts IFRS accounting standards needs to assess carefully the impact of information that 
has become available since it prepared its most recent previous GAAP financial statements because the 
new information might: 
• 
Be a new estimate that must be accounted for prospectively (see section 4.2 above) [IFRS 1.14, IFRS 1.15, 
IFRS 1.16, IFRS 1.17] 
Or 
• 
Expose an error in the previous GAAP financial statements due to mathematical mistakes, mistakes in 
applying accounting policies, oversights or misinterpretations of facts, and fraud. [IAS 8.5]. In the 
reconciliation from previous GAAP to IFRS accounting standards such errors must be disclosed 
separately from the effect of changes in accounting policies (see section 6.3.1 above). [IFRS 1.26]. 
7.3 
IAS 12 Income Taxes 
There are no particular provisions in IFRS 1 with regard to the first-time adoption of IAS 12, except for 
IFRS 1.B14 modifying the initial recognition exception of deferred tax assets and liabilities (see 
section 4.14 above). [IFRS 1.B14]. 
However, the implementation guidance notes that IAS 12 requires entities to provide for deferred tax on 
temporary differences measured by reference to enacted or substantively enacted legislation by the end of 
the reporting period. Therefore, entities need to account for the effect of changes in tax rates and tax laws 
when those changes are enacted or substantively enacted. [IFRS 1.IG5, IFRS 1.IG6]. 
The full retrospective application of IAS 12 (as modified by IFRS 1.B14) poses several problems that might 
not be immediately obvious. First, IAS 12 does not require an entity to account for all temporary 
differences. For example, an entity is not permitted under IAS 12 to recognise deferred tax on: 
• 
Taxable temporary differences arising on the initial recognition of goodwill [IAS 12.15] 
• 
Taxable and deductible temporary differences arising on the initial recognition of an asset or liability in 
a transaction that is not a business combination and that, at the time of the transaction, affects neither 
accounting profit nor taxable profit (tax loss) and, at the time of the transaction, does not give rise to 
equal taxable and deductible temporary differences [IAS 12.15, IAS 12.24] 


International GAAP® 2025 
Chapter 06 
First-time adoption 
436 
However, IFRS 1.B14 requires a first-time adopter, at the date of transition to IFRS accounting standards, 
to recognise a deferred tax asset (to the extent that it is probable that taxable profit will be available 
against which the deductible temporary difference can be utilised) and a deferred tax liability for all 
deductible and taxable temporary differences associated with (1) right-of-use assets and lease liabilities; 
and (2) decommissioning, restoration and similar liabilities and the corresponding amounts recognised as 
part of the cost of the related asset (see section 4.14 above). [IFRS 1.B14]. 
In addition, a change in deferred tax must be accounted for in other comprehensive income or equity, 
instead of profit or loss, when the tax relates to an item that was originally accounted for in other 
comprehensive income or equity. [IAS 12.61A]. 
Therefore, full retrospective application of IAS 12 requires a first-time adopter to establish the history of 
the items that give rise to temporary differences because, depending on the type of transaction, deferred 
tax might or might not be recognised, or changes in the deferred tax might need to be accounted for in 
other comprehensive income or equity. 
The main issue for many first-time adopters of IFRS accounting standards will be that their previous GAAPs 
either required no provision for deferred tax, or required provision under a timing difference approach. 
They also need to be aware that many of the other adjustments made to the statement of financial position 
at the date of transition to IFRS accounting standards will also have a deferred tax effect that must be 
accounted for - see, for example, the potential deferred tax consequences of recognising or derecognising 
intangible assets where an entity uses the business combinations exemption, described in sections 5.2.4.A, 
5.2.4.B, 5.2.5 and 5.2.9 above. Entities that reported under US GAAP must also bear in mind that IAS 12, 
though derived from FASB’s Accounting Standard Codification 740 Income Taxes, is different in a number 
of important respects. 
7.3.1 
Previous revaluation of plant, property and equipment treated as deemed 
cost on transition 
In some cases, IFRS 1 allows an entity, on transition to IFRS accounting standards, to treat the carrying 
amount of plant, property or equipment revalued under its previous GAAP as its deemed cost as of the date 
of revaluation for the purposes of IFRS accounting standards (see section 5.4.1 above). [IFRS 1.D6]. 
Where an asset is carried at deemed cost on the date of transition to IFRS accounting standards but the tax 
base of the asset remains at original cost, or an amount based on original cost, the previous GAAP 
revaluation will give rise to a temporary difference which is typically a taxable temporary difference 
associated with the asset. IAS 12 requires deferred tax to be recognised on the date of transition to IFRS 
accounting standards on any such temporary difference (see Chapter 29 at section 10.10). 
If, after the date of transition to IFRS accounting standards, the deferred tax is required to be remeasured, 
e.g., because of a change in tax rate, or a re-basing of the asset for tax purposes, the entity elects the cost 
model of IAS 16 and the asset concerned was revalued outside profit or loss under previous GAAP, the 
question arises as to whether the resulting deferred tax income or expense is recognised in, or outside, 
profit or loss. 
How we see it 
In our view, either approach, i.e., recognising the deferred tax income or expense in profit or loss or in 
other comprehensive income, is acceptable, so long as it is applied consistently. See Chapter 29 at 
section 10.10. 
7.3.2 
Share-based payment transactions subject to transitional provisions of IFRS 1 
While IFRS 1 provides exemptions from applying IFRS 2 to share-based payment transactions that were 
fully vested prior to the date of transition to IFRS accounting standards, there are no corresponding 
exemptions from the provisions of IAS 12 relating to the tax effects of share-based payment 
transactions. Therefore, the provisions of IAS 12 relating to the tax effects of share-based payments 
apply to all share-based payment transactions, whether they are accounted for in accordance with IFRS 2 
or not. [IAS 12.68A, IAS 12.68B, IAS 12.68C]. IAS 12 effectively considers the cumulative expense associated 
with share-based payment transactions as an asset that has been fully expensed in the financial 
statements in advance of being recognised for tax purposes, thus giving rise to a deductible temporary 
difference (see Chapter 29 at section 10.8). 


International GAAP® 2025 
Chapter 06 
First-time adoption 
437 
How we see it 
This means that on transition to IFRS accounting standards, and subject to the restrictions on recognition 
of deferred tax assets, (see Chapter 29 at section 7.5), a deferred tax asset is established for all share-
based payment awards outstanding at that date, including those not accounted for under the IFRS 1 
transition provisions. 
Where such an asset is remeasured or recognised after transition to IFRS accounting standards, the 
general rule regarding the ‘capping’ of the amount of any tax relief recognised in profit or loss to the 
amount charged to the profit or loss applies (see Chapter 29 at section 10.8.1). Therefore, if there was 
no profit or loss charge for share-based payment transactions under the previous GAAP, all tax effects of 
share-based payment transactions not accounted for under IFRS 2 are dealt with within equity. 
7.3.3 
Retrospective restatements or applications 
The adjustments arising from different accounting policies under previous GAAP and IFRS accounting 
standards must be recognised directly in retained earnings (or, if appropriate, another category of equity) 
at the date of transition to IFRS accounting standards. [IFRS 1.11]. 
Because IAS 12 requires tax relating to an item that has been recognised outside profit or loss to be treated 
in the same way, any tax effect of a retrospective restatement or retrospective application on the opening 
comparative statement of financial position is dealt with as an adjustment to equity also. [IAS 12.61A]. This 
could be taken by some to mean that any subsequent remeasurement of tax originally recognised in equity 
as part of an adjustment at the date of transition to IFRS accounting standards must be accounted for in 
equity also. 
How we see it 
IAS 8 defines retrospective application as the application of “a new accounting policy to transactions, 
other events and conditions as if that policy had always been applied”, and the treatment required by 
IFRS 1 is based on the fact that an entity is restating amounts that arise from events and transactions 
before the date of transition to IFRS accounting standards. 
Accordingly, in our view, if the item that gave rise to the deferred tax would have been recognised in profit 
or loss in the normal course of events had the new accounting policies (or IFRS accounting standards) 
always been applied, the effect of subsequent re-measurement is also recognised in profit or loss. 
An example is given in Illustration 7-1 below. 
Illustration 7-1: 
Remeasurement of deferred tax asset recognised as at the date of transition to 
IFRS accounting standards 
Entity A’s date of transition to IFRS accounting standards was 1 January 20X2. After applying IAS 37, its 
opening IFRS statement of financial position shows an additional liability for environmental remediation costs 
of CU5 million as an adjustment to retained earnings, together with an associated deferred tax asset at 40% of 
CU2 million. 
The environmental liability does not change substantially over the next accounting period, but during the year 
ended 31 December 20X3 the tax rate falls to 30%. This requires the deferred tax asset to be remeasured to 
CU1.5 million giving rise to tax expense of CU500,000. Is this expense recognised in profit or loss for the 
period or in retained earnings? 
In the fact pattern, if IFRS accounting standards had always been applied, all the charge for environmental 
costs (and all the related deferred tax) would have been reflected in profit or loss in previous income 
statements. Therefore, the effect of the change in the tax rate is recognised in profit or loss. 
Where the retrospective restatement giving rise to a deferred tax asset or liability directly in equity 
represented the cumulative total of amounts that would have been recognised ordinarily in other 
comprehensive income or directly in equity in previous periods if the new accounting policy had always 
been applied (e.g., deferred tax relating to the revaluation of property, plant, or equipment), any 
subsequent re-measurement of the deferred tax is also accounted for in the same manner, i.e., in other 
comprehensive income or directly in equity. Remeasurement of deferred tax relating to an asset that is 
measured at deemed cost on transition to IFRS accounting standards but where the tax base of the asset 
remains at original cost, or is an amount based on original cost is discussed in section 7.3.1 above. 
Retrospective restatements or applications are discussed in detail in Chapter 29 at section 10.2. 


International GAAP® 2025 
Chapter 06 
First-time adoption 
438 
7.3.4 
Defined benefit pension plans 
IAS 19 requires an entity, in accounting for a post-employment defined benefit plan, to recognise actuarial 
gains and losses relating to the plan in other comprehensive income. At the same time, service cost and net 
interest on the net defined benefit liability (asset) is recognised in profit or loss. 
In many jurisdictions, tax relief for post-employment benefits is given on the basis of cash contributions 
paid to the plan fund (or benefits paid when a plan is unfunded). 
This significant difference between the ways in which defined benefit plans are treated for tax and financial 
reporting purposes can make the allocation of tax between profit or loss and other comprehensive income 
somewhat arbitrary. 
How we see it 
The issue is of particular importance when a first-time adopter has large funding shortfalls on its defined 
benefit schemes and at the same time can only recognise part of its deferred tax assets. In such a 
situation the method of allocation of tax could well affect the after-tax profit in a given year. In our view, 
this is an instance of the exceptional circumstances envisaged by IAS 12 when a strict allocation of tax 
between profit or loss and other comprehensive income is not possible. Accordingly, any reasonable 
method of allocation could be used, provided that it is applied on a consistent basis. See Chapter 29 at 
section 10.7 for further discussion, including approaches to allocation. 
7.4 
IAS 16 Property, Plant and Equipment and IAS 40 Investment 
Property (cost model) 
The implementation guidance discussed in this section applies to property, plant and equipment as well as 
investment properties that are accounted for under the cost model in IAS 40. [IFRS 1.IG62]. 
IFRS 1 requires an entity to use estimates made under its previous GAAP (after adjustment for different 
accounting policies), unless there is objective evidence those estimates are in error, [IFRS 1.14], but also 
recognises that an entity might need to make estimates in accordance with IFRS accounting standards at 
the date of transition to IFRS accounting standards that were not required at that date under previous 
GAAP. [IFRS 1.16] (see section 4.2 above). Errors made under previous GAAP are corrected retrospectively 
and separately disclosed in the reconciliations from previous GAAP to IFRS accounting standards (see 
section 6.3.1 above). [IFRS 1.26]. These general requirements are relevant where estimates or accounting 
policies differ under previous GAAP and IFRS accounting standards, as discussed further below. 
7.4.1 
Depreciation method and rate 
If a first-time adopter’s depreciation methods and rates under its previous GAAP are acceptable under IFRS 
accounting standards, then it accounts for any change in estimated useful life or depreciation pattern 
prospectively from when it makes that change in estimate (see section 4.2 above). [IFRS 1.14, IFRS 1.15, 
IAS 16.51, IAS 16.61]. However, if the depreciation methods and rates are not acceptable under IFRS 
accounting standards and the difference has a material impact on the financial statements, a first-time 
adopter adjusts the accumulated depreciation in its opening IFRS statement of financial position 
retrospectively. [IFRS 1.IG7]. Further differences could arise from the requirement in IAS 16 to review the 
residual value and the useful life of an asset at least at each financial year end, [IAS 16.51], which might not 
be required under a first-time adopter’s previous GAAP (see section 7.4.2 below). 
How we see it 
If a restatement of the depreciation methods and rates would be too onerous, a first-time adopter could 
opt instead to use fair value as the deemed cost at the date of transition to IFRS accounting standards 
(see section 5.4.1 above). However, application of the deemed cost exemption is not always the only 
approach available. In practice, many first-time adopters have found that, other than buildings, there are 
generally few items of property, plant and equipment that still have a material carrying amount after 
more than 30 or 40 years of use. Therefore, the carrying value that results from a fully retrospective 
application of IAS 16 might not differ much from the carrying amount under an entity’s previous GAAP. 


International GAAP® 2025 
Chapter 06 
First-time adoption 
439 
7.4.2 
Estimates of useful life and residual value 
IAS 16 requires the entity to re-assess the remaining useful life and residual value at least each financial 
year-end and, if expectations differ from previous estimates, the change is accounted for as a change in an 
accounting estimate in accordance with IAS 8. [IAS 16.51]. 
Consider the following situations: 
• 
An entity uses fair value as deemed cost for an item of property, plant and equipment still in use that it 
had depreciated to zero under its previous GAAP (i.e., the asset has already reached the end of its 
originally assessed economic life). 
• 
An entity does not use fair value as deemed cost, but there were indicators in the past that the useful 
life or residual value had changed but those changes were not required to be recognised under 
previous GAAP. 
How we see it 
In the former case, we believe that the asset’s deemed cost is required to be depreciated over its re-assessed 
economic life and taking into account its re-assessed residual value. 
The same applies to the latter case, i.e., the IFRS carrying amount as of the date of transition to IFRS 
accounting standards needs to be determined by taking into account the re-assessed useful life and the re-
assessed residual value. Often, this is difficult, as most entities would not have re-assessed the useful lives 
contemporaneously with the issuance of the previous GAAP financial statements. Accordingly, the fair value 
as deemed cost exemption might be the most logical choice. 
However, if there were indicators in the past that the useful life or residual value had changed but the entity 
did not recognise such changes under the previous GAAP despite its previous GAAP requiring an entity to 
adjust for changes in useful life or residual value, then, this is an ‘error’, that the entity corrects through 
retrospective restatement, and distinguishes from the effects of changes in policies in the reconciliations 
required to explain the transition (see section 6.3.1 above). [IFRS 1.26]. 
7.4.3 
Revaluation model 
A first-time adopter that chooses to account for some or all classes of property, plant and equipment under 
the revaluation model needs to present the cumulative revaluation surplus as a separate component of 
equity. IFRS 1 requires that “The revaluation surplus at the date of transition to IFRSs is based on a 
comparison of the carrying amount of the asset at that date with its cost or deemed cost.” [IFRS 1.IG10]. 
How we see it 
A first-time adopter that uses fair value as the deemed cost for those classes of property, plant and 
equipment would be required to reset the cumulative revaluation surplus to zero. Therefore, any 
previous GAAP revaluation surplus related to assets valued at deemed cost cannot be used to offset a 
subsequent impairment or revaluation loss under IFRS accounting standards. 
Illustration 7-2 below demonstrates the treatment of the revaluation reserve at the date of transition to 
IFRS accounting standards based on different deemed cost exemptions applied under IFRS 1. 
Illustration 7-2: 
Determination of revaluation reserve under IAS 16 as at the date of transition 
to IFRS accounting standards 
Entity A, with a date of transition to IFRS accounting standards of 1 January 20X2, has freehold land classified 
as property, plant and equipment. The land was measured under previous GAAP using a revaluation model 
that is comparable to that required by IAS 16. The previous GAAP carrying amount is CU185,000, being the 
revaluation last determined in April 20X1. The cost of the land under IFRS accounting standards as at 
1 January 20X2 is CU90,000. The fair value of the land on 1 January 20X2 is CU200,000. The entity elects to 
apply the revaluation model under IAS 16 to the asset class that includes the land. 
The revaluation reserve at the date of transition would depend on the exemption applied by the entity: 
• 
If Entity A chooses to use the transition date fair value as deemed cost, the IFRS revaluation reserve is 
zero (CU200,000 - CU200,000). 
• 
If Entity A chooses to use the previous GAAP revaluation as deemed cost, the IFRS revaluation reserve is 
CU15,000 (CU200,000 - CU185,000). 
• 
If Entity A does not use the deemed cost exemption under IFRS 1, the IFRS revaluation reserve is 
CU110,000 (CU200,000 - CU90,000). 


International GAAP® 2025 
Chapter 06 
First-time adoption 
440 
7.4.4 
Parts approach 
IAS 16 requires a ‘parts approach’ to the recognition of property, plant and equipment. Thus, a large item 
such as an aircraft is recognised as a series of ‘parts’ that could have different useful lives. An engine of an 
aircraft could be a part. IAS 16 does not prescribe the physical unit of measure (the ‘part’) for recognition 
i.e., what constitutes an item of property, plant and equipment. Instead, the standard relies on judgement 
in applying the recognition criteria to an entity’s specific circumstances. [IAS 16.9, IFRS 1.IG12]. However, the 
standard does require an entity to: 
• 
Apply a restrictive definition of maintenance costs or costs of day-to-day servicing which it describes as 
“primarily the costs of labour and consumables, and may include the cost of small parts. The purpose 
of these expenditures is often described as for the ‘repairs and maintenance’ of the item of property, 
plant and equipment” [IAS 16.12] 
• 
Derecognise the carrying amount of the parts that are replaced [IAS 16.13] 
• 
Depreciate separately each part of an item of property, plant and equipment with a cost that is 
significant in relation to the total cost of the item [IAS 16.43] 
Based on this, it is reasonable to surmise that parts can be relatively small units. Therefore, it is possible 
that even if a first-time adopter’s depreciation methods and rates are acceptable under IFRS accounting 
standards, it might have to restate property, plant and equipment because its unit of measure under 
previous GAAP was based on physical units significantly larger than parts as described in IAS 16. 
Accounting for parts is described in detail in Chapter 19 at section 3.2. 
How we see it 
In practice, however, there is seldom a need to account for every single part of an asset separately. Very 
often there is no significant difference in the reported amounts once all significant parts have been 
identified. Furthermore, as explained in Chapter 19 at section 3.2, an entity might not actually need to 
identify the parts of an asset until it incurs the replacement expenditure. 
7.5 
IFRS 16 Leases 
Other than the exemption in IFRS 1.D9 (concerning the assessment of whether a contract existing at the 
date of transition to IFRS accounting standards contains a lease - see section 5.5 above), there are no 
exemptions regarding lease accounting available to a first-time adopter that is a lessor. 
Therefore, at the date of transition to IFRS accounting standards, a lessor classifies a lease as operating or 
financing on the basis of circumstances existing at the inception of the lease. Lease classification is 
reassessed only if there is a lease modification. [IFRS 16.66]. Changes in estimates (for example, changes in 
estimates of the economic life or the residual value of the underlying asset) or changes in circumstances 
(for example, default by the lessee) do not give rise to a new classification of a lease. [IFRS 1.IG14]. See 
Chapter 24 at section 5.5 for further discussion about modifying lease terms under IFRS 16. 
7.5.1 
Straight-lining of lease payments including incentives by the lessor 
A first-time adopter that elects either fair value or fair value as deemed cost under IFRS 1.D7 for investment 
property leased out under an operating lease, might have paid lease incentives to a lessee. [IFRS 1.D7]. 
IFRS 16 requires a lessor to recognise lease payments from operating leases, less any incentives 
provided by the lessor to the lessee, as income on either a straight-line basis or another systematic basis. 
[IFRS 16.81]. As there is no relief in IFRS 1 from full retrospective application of IFRS 16 by lessors, the 
lessor as first-time adopter would recognise an asset as a result of the recognition of the lease incentive 
on a straight-line basis or other basis. 
However, the fair value of the investment property might include the effect of the lease incentive, as a 
market participant would take this into consideration when measuring fair value. Recognition of both assets 
by the spreading of lease incentives and investment property at fair value would therefore lead to ‘double 
counting’. IAS 40 requires an entity which chooses the fair value model not to double-count assets or 
liabilities that are recognised as separate assets or liabilities, such as prepaid or accrued operating lease 
income. [IAS 40.50(c)]. 


International GAAP® 2025 
Chapter 06 
First-time adoption 
441 
How we see it 
In our view, the entity would elect either of the following approaches in order to avoid double counting 
the effect on the straight-lining of lease payments on the fair value or fair value as deemed cost of 
investment property: 
(a) Assets or liabilities recognised as a result of straight-lining lease payments are not separately 
presented and are considered a component of investment property. 
Or 
(b) The fair value (or fair value as deemed cost) of the investment property is adjusted for the assets or 
liabilities presented separately. 
When the entity uses approach (a) above, the component parts of investment property are adjusted to 
ensure the subsequent pattern of depreciation on the investment property is appropriate. 
See Chapter 20 at section 4.9.1 for detailed guidance (including an Illustration) on the situation where an 
entity subsequently accounts for the investment property using the fair value model in IAS 40. 
7.6 
IFRS 15 Revenue from Contracts with Customers 
The exemptions in IFRS 1.D34-D35 allow a first-time adopter not to restate contracts that were completed 
before the earliest period presented (i.e., the date of transition to IFRS accounting standards) or completed 
contracts that begin and end within the same annual reporting period (see section 5.20 above). [IFRS 1.D34, 
IFRS 1.D35, IFRS 15.C5(a)]. 
However, where IFRS 15 is applied to a contract, a first-time adopter that has received amounts that do not 
yet qualify for recognition as revenue under IFRS 15 (e.g., the proceeds of a sale that does not qualify for 
revenue recognition) must recognise those amounts as a liability in its opening IFRS statement of financial 
position and adjust the liability for any significant financing component as required by IFRS 15. [IFRS 1.IG17]. 
It is therefore possible that revenue that was already recognised under a first-time adopter’s previous 
GAAP will need to be deferred in its opening IFRS statement of financial position and recognised again (this 
time under IFRS accounting standards) as revenue at a later date. 
Conversely, it is possible that revenue deferred under a first-time adopter’s previous GAAP cannot be 
recognised as a contract liability in the opening IFRS statement of financial position. A first-time 
adopter would not be able to report such revenue deferred under its previous GAAP as revenue under 
IFRS accounting standards at a later date. See Chapter 28 for matters relating to revenue recognition 
under IFRS 15. 
Some further application issues in relation to IFRS 15 on the transition to IFRS accounting standards are 
discussed in section 5.20 above. 
7.7 
IAS 19 Employee Benefits 
7.7.1 
Sensitivity analysis for each significant actuarial assumption 
IAS 19 requires the disclosures set out below about sensitivity of defined benefit obligations. Therefore, for 
an entity’s first IFRS financial statements careful preparation is needed to compile the information required 
to present the sensitivity disclosure for the current and comparative periods. IAS 19 requires an entity to 
disclose: [IAS 19.145] 
(a) A sensitivity analysis for each significant actuarial assumption as of the end of the reporting period, 
showing how the defined benefit obligation would have been affected by changes in the relevant 
actuarial assumption that were reasonably possible at that date 
(b) The methods and assumptions used in preparing the sensitivity analyses required by (a) and the 
limitations of those methods 
(c) Changes from the previous period in the methods and assumptions used in preparing the sensitivity 
analyses, and the reasons for such changes 


International GAAP® 2025 
Chapter 06 
First-time adoption 
442 
7.7.2 
Full actuarial valuations 
An entity’s first IFRS financial statements reflect its defined benefit liabilities or assets on at least three 
different dates, i.e., the end of the first IFRS reporting period, the end of the comparative period and the 
date of transition to IFRS accounting standards (four different dates if it presents two comparative 
periods). If an entity obtains a full actuarial valuation at one or two of these dates, it is allowed to roll 
forward (or roll back) to another date but only as long as the roll forward (or roll back) reflects material 
transactions and other material events (including changes in market prices and interest rates) between 
those dates. [IFRS 1.IG21, IAS 19.59]. 
7.7.3 
Actuarial assumptions 
A first-time adopter’s actuarial assumptions at its date of transition to IFRS accounting standards must be 
consistent with the ones it used for the same date under its previous GAAP (after adjustments to reflect any 
difference in accounting policies), unless there is objective evidence that those assumptions were in error 
(see section 4.2 above). The impact of any later revisions to those assumptions is an actuarial gain or loss 
of the period in which the entity makes the revisions. [IFRS 1.IG19]. 
If a first-time adopter needs to make actuarial assumptions at the date of transition to IFRS accounting 
standards that were not necessary for compliance with its previous GAAP, those actuarial assumptions 
must not reflect conditions that arose after the date of transition. In particular, discount rates and the fair 
value of plan assets at the date of transition need to reflect the market conditions at that date. Similarly, 
the entity’s actuarial assumptions at the date of transition about future employee turnover rates must not 
reflect a significant increase in estimated employee turnover rates as a result of a curtailment of the 
pension plan that occurred after the date of transition. [IFRS 1.16, IFRS 1.IG20]. 
How we see it 
If there is a material difference arising from a change in assumptions at the date of transition to IFRS 
accounting standards, consideration needs to be given to whether there was an error under previous 
GAAP. Errors must be disclosed separately from other transition adjustments (see section 6.3.1 above). 
7.7.4 
Unrecognised past service costs 
IAS 19 requires immediate recognition of all past service costs as an expense. [IAS 19.103]. Accordingly, a 
first-time adopter that has unrecognised past service costs under previous GAAP must recognise such 
amounts in retained earnings at the date of transition, regardless of whether the participants are fully 
vested in the benefit. 
7.8 
IAS 21 The Effects of Changes in Foreign Exchange Rates 
7.8.1 
Functional currency 
A first-time adopter needs to confirm whether all entities included within the financial statements have 
appropriately determined their functional currency. IAS 21 defines an entity’s functional currency as “the 
currency of the primary economic environment in which the entity operates”, [IAS 21.8], and contains 
detailed guidance on determining the functional currency. [IAS 21.9-14]. See Chapter 16 at section 4. 
If the functional currency of an entity is not readily identifiable, IAS 21 requires consideration of whether 
the activities of the foreign operation are carried out as an extension of the reporting entity, rather than 
being carried out with a significant degree of autonomy. [IAS 21.11]. This requirement often leads to the 
conclusion under IFRS accounting standards that intermediate holding companies, treasury subsidiaries and 
foreign sales offices have the same functional currency as their parent (see Chapter 16 at section 4.2). 
Many national GAAPs do not specifically define the concept of functional currency, or they might contain 
guidance on identifying the functional currency that differs from that in IAS 21. Consequently, a first-time 
adopter that measured transactions in a currency that was not its functional currency under IFRS 
accounting standards would need to restate its financial statements because IFRS 1 does not contain an 
exemption that would allow it to use a currency other than the functional currency under IFRS accounting 
standards in determining the cost of assets and liabilities in its opening IFRS statement of financial position. 


International GAAP® 2025 
Chapter 06 
First-time adoption 
443 
How we see it 
While there is an exemption that allows a first-time adopter to reset the cumulative exchange differences in 
equity to zero (see section 5.6 above), a first-time adopter recognises and measures its assets or liabilities 
in its functional currency retrospectively in accordance with IAS 21 (see Chapter 16 at section 5). 
The IFRS IC considered whether a specific exemption should be granted to first-time adopters to permit 
entities to translate all assets and liabilities at the transition-date exchange rate rather than applying the 
functional currency approach in IAS 21 but declined to offer first-time adopters any exemptions on 
transition on the basis that the position under IFRS 1 and IAS 21 was clear.15 
How we see it 
The principal difficulty relates to non-monetary items that are measured on the basis of historical cost, 
particularly property, plant and equipment, since these will need to be re-measured in terms of the 
IAS 21 functional currency at the rates of exchange applicable at the date of acquisition of the assets 
concerned, and recalculating cumulative depreciation charges accordingly. It could be that, to overcome 
this difficulty, an entity considers using the option in IFRS 1 whereby the fair value of such assets at the 
date of transition is treated as being their deemed cost (see section 5.4.1 above). 
7.9 
IAS 28 Investments in Associates and Joint Ventures 
There are a number of first-time adoption exemptions that have an impact on the accounting for 
investments in associates and joint ventures: 
• 
The business combinations exemption, which also applies to past acquisitions of investments in 
associates and interests in joint ventures (see section 5.2.1.B above) 
• 
An exemption in respect of determining the cost of an investment in an associate and a joint venture 
within any separate financial statements that an entity could prepare (see section 5.7.2 above) 
• 
Separate rules that deal with situations in which an investor or a joint venturer adopts IFRS accounting 
standards before or after an associate or a joint venture does so (see section 5.8 above) 
Otherwise, there are no specific first-time adoption provisions for IAS 28, which means that a first-time 
adopter of IFRS accounting standards is effectively required to apply IAS 28 as if it had always done so. 
How we see it 
For some first-time adopters, this could mean application of the equity method for the first time. For 
other first-time adopters, however, the issue is likely to be that they are already applying the equity 
method under their previous GAAPs and will now need to identify the potentially significant differences 
between the methodologies of the equity method under their previous GAAPs and under IAS 28. 
In particular, there could be GAAP differences related to: 
• 
The criteria used to determine which investments are associates or joint ventures 
• 
The elimination of transactions between investors or joint venturers and associates or joint ventures 
• 
The treatment of loss-making associates or joint ventures 
• 
The permitted interval between the reporting dates of an investor or a joint venturer and an associate 
or joint venture with non-coterminous year-ends 
• 
The treatment of investments in entities formerly classified as associates or joint ventures 
• 
The requirement for uniform accounting policies between the investor or joint venturer and the 
associate or joint venture 
In September 2024, the IASB issued an Exposure Draft addressing application questions relating to the 
equity method.16 See the discussion of the IASB project - Equity Method in Chapter 12 at section 13. 
 
15 
IFRIC Update, October 2004, available on the IFRS Foundation’s website. 
16 
Exposure Draft IASB/ED/2024/9 Equity Method of Accounting - IAS 28 Investments in Associates and Joint Ventures, IASB, 
September 2024. Available on the IFRS Foundation’s website.

First-time adoption
